# pg-engine benchmarks

## Current standalone defaults (2026-11-09)

`roll-engine` now always uses the former performance defaults. Shared caches use one
quarter of the container's memory limit (or host memory), split 3:1 between blocks and
plans, with a 64 MiB block minimum and 36 MiB–1 GiB plans. Databases and idle sessions
stay warm, and connections poll for up to 50 µs while cores are free before sleeping.
Idle-session trimming is disabled. Sync and SQL behavior are unchanged.

Use `--cache-mb`, `--plan-cache-mb`, `--memory-budget-mb` and `--idle-close-secs` for
explicit overrides. Individual cache flags override their part of the memory budget.
There is no `--mode`; remove saved `roll.engine_mode` settings before restarting.
`SET` and `ALTER SYSTEM SET roll.engine_mode` are rejected. `ALTER SYSTEM RESET
roll.engine_mode` can remove a stale saved setting on an already running server.

The efficient/performance comparisons below are historical, including the former
33 MiB footprint acceptance limit. That limit does not apply to this default; a prior
measurement of the former performance profile was about 36 MiB with 100 idle sessions.
It is not a new measurement or a new acceptance limit. The current scoreboard runs
one engine arm, with memory-derived sizing by default (`CACHE_MB` overrides it), and
reports idle-session memory without the retired limit. Old raw results and preliminary
quick read/write comparisons remain historical; they do not establish universal
performance superiority over PostgreSQL.

## Scoreboard

The goal is to beat PostgreSQL 18.6 everywhere: throughput, CPU per operation, peak memory and
bytes written on every workload, on the same cores, with the footprint lead kept. The table
below is generated by `tools/bench/scoreboard.sh` (with `--update`); don't edit it by hand.

Notes on agent 156's run (2026-11-03; both servers in containers on cores 4-7, the
clients on 8-11, machine load 12-45 from other agents' builds):

- **Both servers run in containers now.** A host engine sits in the user slice, which every
  agent's builds share, while PostgreSQL's container has system.slice's own CPU weight, so on
  a loaded machine the host engine got less CPU (agent 154's finding). `run.sh`'s
  `ENGINE_DOCKER=1` runs the dist binary, bind-mounted, in a pinned `debian:trixie-slim` with
  PostgreSQL's cpuset, `--cpu-shares 2`, host network and slice; the scoreboard reads both
  servers' CPU, bytes written and memory (anon + shmem) from their cgroups. Against a host-
  engine quick run an hour earlier, throughput cells moved by a median of +4% (from -12% to
  +12%: TPC-B-like 1 client 0.78x -> 0.86x, 32 clients 1.03x -> 1.12x, point reads 0.77x ->
  0.84x, range reads 0.24x -> 0.28x; inserts 0.72x -> 0.63x), within this machine's
  run-to-run spread, and CPU per operation by less: the host table understated the engine a
  little, not by much, at this load. Memory cells rose (select-only 1.17x -> 1.72x): the
  engine's RSS counted its 68 MB mapped binary, the cgroup's anon doesn't. So the idle-sessions
  gate's baseline is re-recorded the new way, 63 -> 30 MB (29.9 measured), to keep guarding.
- Against agent 147's first run (`e73340f4`), a6a8b7ec's once-per-query Gather search shows:
  select-only at 1 client 0.49x -> 0.90x, at 8 clients 0.40x -> 0.81x; TPC-B-like at 1 client
  0.53x -> 0.86x.
- New cells: pgbench at 32 clients (select-only 0.86x; TPC-B-like 1.12x, where scale 1's one
  branch row serialises both servers) and the write-heavy `oltp update_indexed` at 32 clients
  (0.53x throughput, 0.54x CPU per update).
- `oltp range_scan_100` (the checked campaign's range read) is the widest OLTP gap, 0.28x. In
  the host run its first round's servers were starved of their cores (about one of four busy)
  on both sides; cells are therefore the median of each round's own ratio (the two sides run
  back to back), not the ratio of the medians.
- The fresh-data-directory gate's 3.0 MB baseline (re-recorded in ed0c85fe) is the running
  server with three databases open, a preallocated 1 MB WAL file each; after a clean stop the
  directory is 60 kB.
- Quick mode's idle CPU resolves one 10 ms clock tick per 60 s window (0.05 s per 5 minutes);
  an engine that used none shows its lead as a lower bound. Full mode measures the 5 minutes.

Current measurement procedure (`tools/bench/scoreboard.sh`, `scoreboard.py`):

- Both servers in containers on the same pinned cores (`SERVER_CPUS`, the clients on
  `CLIENT_CPUS`), at the same low CPU weight (`--cpu-shares 2`) in the same slice, on the host
  network (no docker-proxy); the engine's container is a pinned `debian:trixie-slim` with the
  dist binary bind-mounted (`run.sh`'s `ENGINE_DOCKER=1`; empty runs it on the host). PostgreSQL is the pinned image with
  `tools/bench/run.sh pg-start`'s settings; the engine uses memory-derived defaults, built
  with `--profile dist` (fat LTO, what users run: "Build profile: dist" below).
- A run has up to four servers: one engine (`engine`), PostgreSQL tuned (`pg`),
  PostgreSQL default (`pgdef`) and pgrust. `SERVER_MEM` defaults to 8 GiB.
  `CACHE_MB` overrides the engine's block cache. `SB_PG_DEFAULT=` leaves out PostgreSQL
  default, and `SB_PGRUST=` leaves out pgrust. The earlier five-server mode comparison
  and its `SB_PERF` selector are historical.
- Every workload of `tools/bench` (pgbench select-only and TPC-B-like, the OLTP cases, the
  analytic set, TPC-H, bulk loads, DDL, connection churn) runs on PostgreSQL and on the engine
  in turn, in interleaved rounds (each round swaps which goes first); a cell is the median of
  the rounds' own ratios (each round's two sides ran back to back), written so that above 1x
  the engine is ahead.
- Around each phase on the server that runs it, from its container's cgroup: its CPU
  (`usage_usec`, every process in it), the bytes it wrote (`io.stat` wbytes; a CHECKPOINT ends
  every phase on both, so deferred page writes count), and its peak memory (anon + shmem,
  sampled every 0.5 s). CPU per operation divides by the phase's operations (pgbench
  transactions, OLTP operations) or, for fixed work, compares the phase's CPU.
- The footprint gate, on fresh servers one at a time: idle CPU over 5 minutes, the server's
  memory while 100 sessions sit idle (`pgbench -c 100` of `SELECT 1; \sleep 40 s`), the size of a
  fresh data directory. Each must stay below PostgreSQL's and within its tolerance of the lead
  measured 2026-11-03 (0.01 s against 0.27 s; 63 MB against 150 MB, re-recorded in a
  container as 30 MB; 3 MB, re-recorded, against 39 MB): `scoreboard.py` exits non-zero
  when one fails.
- `quick` (agents; about half an hour at nice 19): 2 rounds, pgbench scale 1 at 1, 8 and 32
  clients, OLTP 100k rows (8 clients; `update_indexed` at 32 too: the write-heavy case at 32
  clients, as pgbench's TPC-B-like at scale 1 serialises on its one branch row), analytic 200k
  rows, TPC-H SF 0.05, bulk 100k rows, 100 tables of DDL, idle CPU measured over 60 s. `full`
  (releases; hours): 3 rounds at `tools/bench/suite.sh`'s sizes (pgbench scales 1 and 10 at
  1 / 8 / 32 clients, OLTP 1M rows at 1 and 8 clients and `insert`, `update_indexed`,
  `short_txn` at 32, analytic 200k and 2M, TPC-H SF 1, bulk 100k and 1M, 500 tables), idle CPU
  over 5 minutes.
- `oltp range_scan_100` is the checked OLTP campaign's range read (below): the same statement
  (`id BETWEEN $1 AND $1 + 99 ORDER BY id`, a named prepared statement, binary int4) with every
  returned id and value checked inside the timer, on 100k accounts instead of 200k.
- A ratio marked `~` is one the rounds disagree on (one round has the engine ahead, the other
  behind): within the run's noise. `SB_COMMIT` names the engine's commit when the checkout's
  HEAD isn't it.

```sh
tools/heavy.sh cargo build --profile dist -p pg-engine --bin roll-engine
tools/heavy.sh cargo build --release -p pg-engine --example bench
SB_DATA=~/.cache/<you>/scoreboard SERVER_CPUS=20-23 CLIENT_CPUS=8-11 PG_PORT=<port> \
  ENGINE_PORT=<port> PG_NAME=<you>-sb-pg tools/bench/scoreboard.sh quick        # or: full --update
```

Pick cores no other benchmark pins at the time (`docker inspect` the running containers'
cpusets), ports in your range, and a container name of your own. A run's raw lines are in
`$SB_DATA/score-<mode>.tsv`, every client's output in `$SB_DATA/logs/`.

## Historical modes (2026-11-08, agent 180)

`roll-engine --mode efficient|performance` (HANDOFF "Modes"): efficient, the default, is the
engine as it was; performance takes memory and a little CPU for speed. Results, errors, plans,
everything SQL sees (but `roll.engine_mode` itself) and durability (fsync on commit) are the same
in both.

**How it was measured.** Dist builds in containers as the scoreboard runs them (`run.sh`'s
`ENGINE_DOCKER=1`: pinned `debian:trixie-slim`, cores 0-3, `--cpu-shares 2`, host network), the
clients on cores 32-35; each variant a server of its own, all up at once, 10 s phases in
interleaved rounds (each round rotates the order), a cell the median of the rounds' own ratios
(the per-round ratios in brackets). CPU per operation and memory (anon + shmem) from each
container's cgroup. The machine ran other agents' builds at load 20-40, so a single round
moves by up to ±15%; a knob counts only when its rounds agree. pgbench `-M prepared`, scale 1
unless said; `oltp` cases on 100k rows, 8 clients.

**Each knob** (performance against efficient unless said):

| knob | efficient | performance | measured | kept |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| shared caches | 64 MB blocks, 36 MB plans | a quarter of the memory the server may use (cgroup limit, else the machine's), 3 : 1, plans at most 1 GB: in 8 GB, 1.5 GB + 512 MB | pgbench scale 10 select-only 1 client **2.21x** [2.21, 2.26, 1.81], CPU per transaction 119 -> 75 µs; 8 clients **1.29x** [1.19, 1.50, 1.29], 79 -> 62 µs; TPC-H SF 0.05 **1.24x** [1.25, 1.24, 1.23], CPU 5.30 -> 4.26 s; analytic 200k 1.02x (noise); memory 275 -> 433 MB | yes |
| wait spin | none | a waiting connection polls its socket up to 50 µs, when its client's last message came within that and while working + polling connections fit in a quarter of the cores | select-only 1 client **1.38x** [1.36, 1.38, 1.37], latency 0.082 -> 0.060 ms, CPU per transaction 52 -> 58 µs; TPC-B-like 1 client **1.22x** [1.21, 1.22, 1.28], CPU 372 -> 430 µs (the polls between its statements); 8 clients: select-only 1.02x, point reads 1.05x, range reads 0.96x [1.02, 0.94, 0.96] with 5% more CPU per read; 32 clients 1.02x (the half-cores rule's run) | yes |
| databases closed when idle | 60 s after the last session | never | the first statement on a database closed for idleness: +5.0 to +15.7 ms over the connection (6 tries, `--idle-close-secs 3` standing in for 60), on an open one +0.6 to +1.1 ms | yes |
| idle trim | a connection idle 2 s gives its thread's free memory back | sessions keep it | CPU per statement after a 3 s pause (32 clients, 3 rounds): 341-478 µs with the trim, 290-301 without (after a 0.5 s pause, no trim either way: 202-253); latency within the noise; 100 idle sessions 31.9 -> 34.7 MB | yes |
| transparent huge pages | off | (tried on) | select-only, TPC-B-like, point and range reads: no direction beyond the noise; analytic 0.98x, TPC-H 0.98x; +17-20 MB | no |
| memtable size | 8 MB | (tried 32 / 64 MB) | bulk 100k 0.95x / 0.85x, pgbench init scale 10 1.01x / 1.02x, inserts 1.06x / 0.94x, reads 0.89-0.98x | no |
| allocator purging | mimalloc's 10 ms | (tried never: `MIMALLOC_PURGE_DELAY=-1`) | no workload faster beyond the noise; +80 MB | no |

What limits each gain:

- **Caches.** Scale 10 (a million accounts) and TPC-H SF 0.05 don't fit in 64 MB of decoded
  blocks (performance mode's cache held about 160 MB more: 275 -> 433 MB): a miss reads the
  block from the page cache, checks its CRC, decompresses it (zstd) and decodes it again. With
  1.5 GB every block stays decoded and what is left per statement is the executor's. The
  analytic set's 200k rows fit either way. The scoreboard always gave the engine `--cache-mb
  2048` (and PostgreSQL `shared_buffers=2GB`), so its efficient column doesn't show this; its
  performance column sizes itself from the 8 GB container to the same scale.
- **Spin.** At one client a transaction is the server's work (50-60 µs a select) plus the round
  trip, and the round trip was a sleep and a wake-up on each side (futex, scheduler, a cold
  core). A thread polling for its client's next message keeps its core and skips the server's
  half of that; the client's half remains. The polls cost CPU: a few µs a select, 58 µs a
  TPC-B-like transaction (seven statements, each followed by a poll while the client sends the
  next). Polling where it can't pay only takes cores and CPU, so it is gated three ways, each
  from a run that lost without it: a fixed cap of two pollers on 4 cores cost 32 clients 15%;
  polling while working + polling threads fit all 4 cores cost 8 clients 8%, in half of them 8
  clients' range reads 9% ([1.07, 0.93, 0.83, 0.89]), in a quarter (on 4 cores: only while no
  other connection works) still 7% with 11% more CPU per read, because the benchmark client,
  checking 100 rows a read, is slower than the server and leaves every server thread waiting
  at once. Polling only for a client whose last message came within the 50 µs took that to
  0.96x and 5% (and select-only at 8 clients 1.02x, point reads 1.05x). Range reads at 8
  clients are the one case it may still cost a little; at 32 clients nothing polls.
- **Idle close.** Reopening a database opens its store (manifest, every SST's index), reads its
  catalog and rebuilds the per-database state: 5-15 ms on the first statement. Kept open, the
  store costs its memtable (up to 8 MB) and open files.
- **Idle trim.** The trim (`mi_collect(true)`, the unused stack `madvise`d away) and then the
  next statement faulting the heap and stack pages in again: 50-190 µs of CPU per statement
  after a pause.

**Footprint per mode** (dist builds, containers, 45 s windows scaled to 5 minutes, two runs each;
"before" is origin's head `d3f493e51`, this work's base, built the same way):

| | before | efficient | performance |
|---|---|---|---|
| idle CPU, no session (s / 5 min) | 0.009-0.010 | 0.008-0.009 | 0.009-0.011 |
| CPU with 100 idle sessions (s / 5 min) | 0.126-0.133 | 0.008-0.009 | 0.007-0.009 |
| memory with 100 idle sessions (MB) | 31.3-32.2 | 31.3-31.4 | 32.1-32.6 |

The scoreboard's gate on the final build (`scoreboard.sh quick`, `SB_ONLY=none`): efficient mode
idle CPU 0.03 s, 100 idle sessions 30.0 MB and 0.015 s of CPU, fresh directory 3.03 MB, all
pass (PostgreSQL 0.625 s, 211 MB, 0.30 s, 38.9 MB); performance mode (measured on the build that still
disabled huge pages late, below) 0.010 s, 34.3 MB, 0.015 s, 3.03 MB. A build that disabled transparent huge
pages after parsing its arguments instead of first thing measured 32-34 MB with 100 idle sessions
(the gate's 33.9 failed it): the pages faulted before the prctl kept their huge pages.

The 100 idle sessions' CPU fell 15x in efficient mode too: a connection idle past its trim
parks (d7e454cc5) where it woke every 2 s. Performance mode never wakes an idle session (no
trim to wait for), and a connection polls at most 50 µs after its client's last message, so an
idle server polls nothing.

**What the modes can't close.** Range reads (`oltp range_scan_100`), TPC-H, the analytic set,
bulk loads (COPY, INSERT ... SELECT, pgbench init) and select-only or point reads at 8 clients
and more are behind PostgreSQL by the engine's CPU per row and per statement in the executor
and storage, not by anything a mode trades: at 8 and 32 clients the four cores are busy either
way, so performance mode measures 0.96-1.05x of efficient there, and on data that fits the
cache both modes do the same work per row. Those gaps belong to the executor, planner, OLTP,
storage, bulk-write and index owners (agents 167, 177, 178, 169, 175, 176). Performance mode
helps where the engine waits rather than computes: one or two clients (spin), data bigger than
efficient mode's caches, intermittent traffic (open databases, warm sessions).

## Other measurements

The current checked measurements compare release engine builds and PostgreSQL 18.6 over
the wire. Older rounds below are historical records, with their original methods and
limits. Their results have not been reverified on the current source.

## Analytic path, round 2 (2026-11-13, agent 226)

HANDOFF "Analytic path, round 2" has what changed and the phase study. **b1** is `66fb5dc38`
(origin's head before the round's commits; `b0`, `a79309351`, was the base of the per-change
gates), **l1** the round's commits on it (`25b06d0cd`, `5b26cef02`, `dd93493f1`, `87fc55b14`, the
same tree). Dist builds (fat LTO, line tables) of one checkout, equal-length paths
(`~/.cache/a226/bin`). Lane C: each server in its own container on CPUs 14,15,32,33 (`--memory
8g`, host network), the client on 16,17,34,35, every pass under `flock ~/.cache/lane-c.lock`
(shared with agents 224 and 225). Settings on all three servers:
`max_parallel_workers_per_gather = 2`, `max_parallel_workers = 8`, a 2 GB cache (the engine
`--cache-mb 2048`, PostgreSQL and pgrust `shared_buffers = 2GB`; pgrust's set with `-c` and
ALTER SYSTEM, checked with SHOW after its restart). PostgreSQL 18.6 with the bench settings,
pgrust `malisper/pgrust` 0.4-beta. TPC-H SF1 from `bench tpch-load --sf 1`, the engine's data
directory restored before every start. A gate pass: a fresh engine, a warm-up pass, then per
query three runs inside one `perf stat -e instructions:u,cycles:u` window (agent 220's
`gpass.sh`). The host was at load 25-47 through the round (other sessions' unpinned builds at
nice 19, some on the lane's hyperthread siblings): cycles moved by up to +-30% a pass, so the
gates read instructions, and every target's wall time was checked in single-query runs (5 runs
a pass, 3-5 alternating pairs, fresh engines).

### Per-query CPU against pgrust, where the engine loses (the head; `perf record` of b0 / c1)

| query | engine CPU s | pgrust | where the engine's time goes |
|---|---|---|---|
| q18 | 6.76 | 2.40 | the IN subquery's GroupAggregate over lineitem's primary key index: 6 M rows fetched by point gets a chunk at a time (`get_versions` 22%, block seeks), aggregated on the row path (`partial_row` 29%); pgrust plans it serially (its own planner) |
| q13 | 2.49 | 1.05 | the orders scan's hash probe (`run_stages`, `lookup` 21%), the row path (`partial_row`, `Lazy`), the per-customer partial counts (`add_words` 9%); LIKE 8% before c5 |
| q09 | 4.12 | 3.69 | index probes and seeks of the joins (`Block::seek` 14%, `find_block`, `get_with`) |
| q10 | 1.96 | 1.03 | the lineitem decode (30% before c1), the probe, `Value` clones of the joined rows |
| q21 | 2.03 | 1.12 | the lineitem decode (32% before c1), `Pre::fate`'s probe, the semi / anti joins' index scans |
| q17 | 1.84 | 0.70 | the subplan's bitmap fetches per part (block reads and seeks 26%), the lineitem decode (14%) |
| q03, q07, q08, q20, q02, q05 | | | point lookups and block seeks of index joins (12-14% in `Block::seek` alone for q07 / q08 / q20) |
| q01, q06, q12, q15 | 1.54, 1.20, 1.48, 1.20 | 1.06, 1.10, 1.25, 0.55 | the column decode (40-48% after c1: a tag dispatch per value up to the last column read), q01's lanes and sums (33%) |
| q04, q22 | 0.81, 0.34 | 0.33, 0.09 | the row path (`K::run` over `Lazy`, `LazyBuf::read`) |
| q16 | 0.98 | 0.41 | `Value` comparisons of count(distinct)'s sort |

## Profile-guided build (2026-11-12, agent 225)

`crates/pg-engine/tools/dist-pgo.sh` builds roll-engine profile-guided: the `dist` profile plus
LLVM's profile of a training run (`tools/pgo-train.sh`), generated in the build. HANDOFF.md
"Profile-guided build" has the recipe and how to gate on it. **Every table from here on says
which roll build it shows: "plain dist" (`cargo build --profile dist`) or "PGO"
(`tools/dist-pgo.sh`, its default training).**

How the three servers' binaries are built (for every same-day comparison):

| server | build |
|---|---|
| roll, plain dist | `cargo build --profile dist`: release + fat LTO, `codegen-units = 1`, no `target-cpu` (x86-64 baseline), mimalloc; rustc 1.99.1 |
| roll, PGO | the same profile and flags, plus `-Cprofile-use` of a profile made by an instrumented build of the same commit running `tools/pgo-train.sh`; nothing else differs (same target CPU) |
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | the pinned official image (`postgres@sha256:77f58511…`, Alpine, musl): `pg_config --configure` shows gcc, `CFLAGS=-O2`, `--with-llvm` (JIT), no `-flto`, no `-fprofile-use`: neither LTO nor PGO |
| pgrust | the pinned image (`malisper/pgrust@sha256:9ec8cbd6…`): a stripped Rust binary from an unpublished builder stage; `.comment` says rustc 1.96.0 and LLD 22; whether it used LTO or PGO can't be read from the binary |

### PGO against plain dist, same commit (236568f2a)

Each workload on its own lane (HANDOFF machine rules), 3 alternating pairs, fresh server and data
each run; user cycles and instructions of the server's connection threads (OLTP) or of the whole
process over the run (bulk, TPC-H); medians, every pair in the same direction unless noted. No
pair had more than 1.0 foreign core except where marked.

| workload | plain dist | PGO | throughput / wall | user cycles / op | user instr / op |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| point reads, 8 clients, extended protocol, binary results | 73,121 tps | 94,992 tps | +29.9% | -37.4% | -21.1% |
| point reads, 8 clients, simple protocol | 66,613 tps | 85,841 tps | +28.1% | -34.4% | -14.9% |
| pgbench -S -M prepared, 8 clients (text) | 72,529 tps | 103,840 tps | +44.2% | -40.3% | -20.7% |
| range reads (100 rows), 8 clients | 26,670 tps | 33,782 tps | +26.5% | -29.0% | -20.6% |
| short write transactions, 1 client | 2,573 tps | 3,228 tps | +21.8% | -30.6% | -16.8% |
| short write transactions, 8 clients | 10,779 tps | 13,412 tps | +24.4% | -28.5% | -14.8% |
| short write transactions, 32 clients | 10,066 tps | 12,086 tps | +20.6% | -25.4% | -18.3% |
| bulk (1M rows: COPY in/out, 2 index builds, INSERT SELECT, UPDATE, DELETE, VACUUM, ANALYZE) | 7.46 s | 6.16 s | -17.4% wall | -21.6% | -18.9% |
| TPC-H SF1, 22 queries, parallel | 15.8 s | 13.6 s | -13.7% wall | -15.7% | -16.7% |

- Bulk: every step faster (count -36%, VACUUM -32%, ANALYZE -28%, COPY out -27%, INSERT SELECT
  -21%, DELETE -19%, UPDATE -18%, unique index -16%, COPY in -11%, index -2%).
- TPC-H: result hashes equal in every run. With one run a query, q01 (+2.7%) and q12 (+5.6%)
  read slower at the median; run alone 5 times a run (3 pairs) they are -9.8% and -8.9%.
- Memory after a short-transaction run, a CHECKPOINT and 3 s (3 pairs): plain 72.8 MB, PGO 67.6 MB
  (median). Right after a fixed-time run PGO holds more (+13%): it committed 22-24% more.
- Idle footprint (100 idle sessions, cgroup anon + shmem, 3 runs each): plain 36.7-36.9 MB, **PGO
  42.9-43.1 MB** (+6.1 MB, 62 KB a session). Not landed, see below. One idle process: PGO 2.7 MB
  less RSS (29.9 against 32.6 MB). Fresh data directory: 3.03 MB both.
- Startup (40 alternating rounds, exec to the first TCP connect): new data directory 33.4 ms
  plain, 34.4 ms PGO; restart 13.1 and 12.1 ms (the same within the polling's 1 ms).
- Binary: plain 70.7 MB, PGO 66.0 MB.

**The idle footprint.** The +62 KB a session is the session thread's stack (stack page faults by
function, `perf record -e page-faults -d`): PGO inlines the wire message handlers (Parse, Bind,
Execute, Describe, Close, simple query, with the session methods they call) into
`server::message_loop`, whose frame grows from 11 KB to 70 KB, and that frame is under every
session for its whole life. Tried: the handlers `#[inline(never)]` (footprint 37.0 MB, but point
reads +1.8% instructions and +3.5% cycles at 1 client, PGO; in plain dist -0.8 MB for +0.3%);
all but Bind and Execute (37.5 MB, +2.6% instructions); `-Cllvm-args=-inline-max-stacksize=4096`
(36.9 MB, but range reads +9.3% instructions, +10.1% cycles). None kept: the PGO build ships with
the 6 MB at 100 sessions; HANDOFF says what to try next.

### Code PGO's training never ran

Code no training run reaches is cold to LLVM: placed and inlined as such. Measured:

- **A class left out** (profile trained without TPC-H, TPC-H measured against plain dist, 3 pairs):
  all 22 queries -3.3% user cycles, -6.3% instructions at the median, but one pair +1.3% cycles;
  q01, q06 and q16 alone (5 runs each, 3 pairs) **+5.6% user cycles** (q01 +13.4%, q06 +7.5%
  wall). With TPC-H trained, the same queries are 9-10% faster than plain dist.
- **Fresh hold-outs no profile trains** (the bench client's `analytic` set, 2M rows, and `nl`,
  nested loops over a parameterised index scan), the default (benchmark-class) profile against
  plain dist, each query alone, 5 runs, 3 pairs: numeric_sum -12.4% user cycles, sort_float -4.1%,
  sort_text -1.4%, seq_filter -9.2%; nested loops -24.0% (inner -34.5%, semi -26.7%, left
  -16.1% wall). Not slower in any pair. (Read from one multi-query run, numeric_sum and sort_text
  looked 8-10% slower: that was the attribution, not the code.)
- **Wider training** (`PGO_BREADTH=1`: every golden SQL file and PostgreSQL's regression suite,
  merged with the benchmark classes' profile at weights that leave the benchmarks 81%, 95% and
  98% of the counts), against the default profile: TPC-H +2.2%, +1.4%, +1.4% instructions; bulk
  +1.4%, +0.2%, +2.2%; point reads +1.0%, +0.1%, -0.2% instructions with +1.1-2.4% cycles; short
  transactions +2% cycles. And on the hold-outs it is worse, not better: against plain dist at 98%,
  seq_filter **+1.9%** user cycles (every pair), sort_text +0.9% (two pairs of three slower),
  sort_float one pair +4.2%; at 81%, sort_float **+8.6%** and sort_text **+3.9%** in every pair.
- **A guard** (`-pgso=false -inline-cold-callsite-threshold=225`: no size optimisation of cold
  code, cold call sites inlined as others; `-pgo-cold-func-opt` already defaults to no attribute
  in LLVM 23) on the no-TPC-H profile: TPC-H -6.5% cycles instead of -3.3%, q01+q06+q16 +2.0%
  instead of +5.6%, but q01 +10.4% and q12 +14.0% wall at the median; binary 74 MB. Halves the
  loss, does not remove it; not used.

**A workload class missing from the training can run slower than plain dist (TPC-H q01 +13%
with TPC-H left out); new benchmark classes must be added to `tools/pgo-train.sh`.** So the
default trains the benchmark classes only (and a slice of the golden files), and the rule is: **every workload class we benchmark is in `tools/pgo-train.sh`**; a workload outside them gets
what its code paths share with the trained ones (nested loops: all of it; numeric aggregates,
sorts, filters: 1-12% cycles here), and a whole class left out can come out slower than plain dist
(TPC-H's q01: +13%).

### Builds and disk

`dist-pgo.sh` on 2 jobs (heavy.sh): bench client (release) 1,123 s if not given (`PGO_CLIENT`),
instrumented build 1,162-1,225 s, training 310 s (611 s with `PGO_BREADTH=1`), optimised build
1,015-1,115 s; 41 minutes with a client given, 63 without. Disk: each dist target dir ~3 GB
(instrumented a little more), deleted after its binary is copied; raw profiles 35 MB; the merged
profile 19 MB. A plain dist build of the same commit: 17-19 minutes.
### Agent 218's stack under PGO (part 2)

The ten steps of `wip/a218` (BENCH "Short write transactions, round 5"), rebased, against their
base, both PGO (each `tools/dist-pgo.sh` from its own checkout: its own profile, the same
training); lane A, user cycles of the connection threads. Pairs with more than 1.0 foreign core
on the server CPUs are flagged and left out (the host ran at load 20-46 for most of a night).

On `aae3e83f6` (5 alternating pairs at 8 and 32 clients, 3 at 1, none flagged at 8/32):

| | user cycles / txn | tps | user instr / txn |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| short txn, 8 clients | -5.4% (every pair down) | +0.4% | -1.9% |
| short txn, 32 clients | -8.5% (every pair down) | +12.0% (every pair up) | -1.7% |
| short txn, 1 client | -5.0% | +0.6% | -3.8% |
| point reads, 8 clients | -7.4% | +4.8% | -3.2% |
| range reads, 8 clients (1 pair) | -5.5% | +16.8% | -0.9% |
| idle footprint, 100 sessions | 42.2-42.4 MB against 43.0-43.1 | | |

Plain dist of the same two commits (5 pairs each): 8 clients -4.4% user cycles (every pair down),
32 clients -7.4%: the PGO deltas were the plain ones plus about a point.

On `a79309351` (the branch had moved: the fast commit path of a transaction that wrote nothing,
prepared statements in hashbrown maps, the wire protocol's accept paths), unflagged pairs of four
alternating series pooled:

| | unflagged pairs | user cycles / txn | tps | user instr |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| short txn, 8 clients | 5 | -4.4% (all 5 down: -1.1 to -6.3%) | +6.2% | |
| short txn, 32 clients | 6 | ~-8.5% (all 6 down: -3.5 to -10.3%) | ~+8% | |
| short txn, 1 client | 3 | -4.5% (all down) | +0.9% | -3.6% |
| point reads, 8 clients | 2 | -6.2%, -5.0% | | -2.2%, -4.2% |
| range reads, 8 clients | 3 | -1.1% | | -0.9% (median; -3.7 to +0.7) |
| fsyncs / commit at a fixed 2,000 tps, 8 and 32 clients | 3 + 3 | base 0.988-1.016, stack 0.993-1.017 | | |
| idle footprint, 100 sessions | 3 + 3 | stack 43.2-44.6 MB, base 43.2-44.6 MB | | |

The quietest 8-client series alone (3 pairs, 0.3-0.8 foreign cores) was -1.1% to -4.5%: part of
the stack's 8-client gain overlaps what landed meanwhile. Fsyncs per commit at full load were
lower with the stack (32 clients, 0.51 against 0.52-0.53) because it ran 7-13% more transactions
a second (more commits a group; fsyncs a second higher); at an equal commit rate they are equal,
and pwrites per commit fell from ~1.0 to the fsync count (step 2 writes the WAL once per fsync).

Verification train on the stack's PGO build: see HANDOFF "Profile-guided build".

## Executor, round 16 (2026-11-13, agent 227)

HANDOFF "Executor, round 16" has what changed: a correlated SubPlan's executor tree kept through
the statement and rescanned per outer row; a Hash Right Semi / Anti Join's Hash and sides of the
columns the join reads; an outer join's clause a constant made redundant parameterizes no scan;
EXPLAIN ANALYZE's loop and row counts under a Gather and at semi / anti joins as PostgreSQL's;
a Parallel Hash over a whole index-only scan read by the scan's workers. **base** is
`a79309351`; `c001` the SubPlan rescans against base, `c007` the behaviour fixes against `c001`,
`c008` the Parallel Hash against `c007` (`c002`, a kept store scan for index fetches, dropped).
Plain dist builds (fat LTO, line tables) of one snapshot path, equal-length binary paths
(`~/.cache/a227/bin`).

Lane C: each server in its own container on CPUs 14,15,32,33 (`--memory 8g`, host network), the
client on 16,17,34,35, every pass under `~/.cache/lane-c.lock` (shared with agents 224, 225, 226).
All three servers at `max_parallel_workers_per_gather = 2`, `max_parallel_workers = 8` and a 2 GB
cache (roll `--cache-mb 2048`; PostgreSQL 18.6 and pgrust `shared_buffers = 2GB`, set on pgrust
at start). TPC-H SF1 from `bench tpch-load --sf 1`, the engine's data directory restored before
every start. Other tenants kept the host at load 13-77 through the round (verification trains
and builds on every other core); each pass waited up to 60 s for the lane to fall under 30% busy
and usually didn't get there, so the wall times below carry that load. The gates read user
instructions (`perf stat -e instructions:u,cycles:u`, a fresh engine, a warm-up, two runs a
window) first.

### TPC-H SF1, PostgreSQL, base, head (`c008`) and pgrust (three interleaved rounds, medians)

| | q01 | q02 | q03 | q04 | q05 | q06 | q07 | q08 | q09 | q10 | q11 | q12 | q13 | q14 | q15 | q16 | q17 | q18 | q19 | q20 | q21 | q22 | sum |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| pg s | 3.01 | 0.25 | 0.56 | 0.21 | 0.41 | 0.54 | 0.49 | 0.24 | 1.69 | 0.78 | 0.09 | 0.77 | 0.90 | 0.19 | 0.41 | 0.32 | 0.92 | 3.51 | 0.08 | 0.11 | 0.75 | 0.17 | 16.38 |
| base s | 0.64 | 0.60 | 0.57 | 0.30 | 0.49 | 0.51 | 0.60 | 0.30 | 1.51 | 0.76 | 0.20 | 0.58 | 1.14 | 0.21 | 0.49 | 0.36 | 0.88 | 2.34 | 0.07 | 0.41 | 0.83 | 0.15 | 13.96 |

## Range reads, round 8 (2026-11-12, agent 223)

HANDOFF.md, "Range reads round 8", has what changed: a rechosen custom plan restarts the executor
tree of the plan it was made after (`Node::adopt`; a btree range scan is now restartable), and a
handful of per-execution costs in planning and scanning (`PlanRef` without a call through a
function pointer, the parallel worker settings from the derived settings, the fixed result row
compared in place, `toast::may_hold_rows` without allocating, each qual's cost found once when the
WHERE is placed). Agent 215's harness (`~/.cache/a223/h`, containers `a223-`): dist builds in
containers with PostgreSQL's limits, 100,000 accounts, 2 s warmup, 10 s measured; user
instructions and cycles of the connection threads (`perf stat --per-thread`) and the server
containers' cycles (`perf stat -a -G`). Lane B: servers on CPUs 10,11,28,29, clients on
12,13,30,31; builds on 0-5,18-23.

**Same day, the three servers** (three rounds each, the arms' order reversed in round 2; cycles
are the server container's, all its processes and threads; medians):

| binary | pgrust | 23,616 | 154.8 | 519k | 626k | 200 |

| binary | roll, this change | 29,889 | 122.2 | 393k | 485k | 52 |

(Other tenants' load on the server CPUs 0.09-0.35 cores.) Against PostgreSQL the change is +19%
ops/s binary and +17% text, -8.4% and -9.3% user cycles, -17% total cycles both, half the memory;
pgrust is behind PostgreSQL in binary and level in text. The round's target, user cycles at least
15% below PostgreSQL's, isn't met. Again after rebasing onto `29e94da4c` (the point-read probe and
portal-map commits; the verification train's build busy on CPUs 0-5,18-23, foreign load up to 0.56
cores, so ops/s swing 5-10% between rounds):

User cycles per operation -5.1% in both formats against the rebased base (-8.7% and -10.0%
against PostgreSQL); the ops/s of three single runs under that load don't separate the builds (the
five alternating pairs below do).

The change against the round's base (`01da1c495`), five alternating pairs each (foreign load on
the server CPUs 0.09-0.35 cores, the 1-client runs 0.41-0.55; none above a core):

| workload | instr / op (median; range) | cycles / op (range) | ops/s (median; worst pair) | CPU / op (median) | MiB after (base; change) |
|---|---|---|---|---:|---|
| range read, binary, 8 clients | -2.10%; -1.92% to -2.34% | -3.15% to -5.44% | +3.7%; +2.4% | -4.1% | 48.7-54.1; 52.8-53.7 |
| range read, text, 8 clients | -1.57%; -1.25% to -2.77% | -3.91% to -5.90% | +7.0%; +5.0% | -5.5% | 48.4-53.1; 49.8-53.7 |
| range read, binary, 1 client | -2.07%; -1.81% to -3.24% | -3.39% to -9.70% | +1.2%; -0.7% | -2.7% | 46.6-48.1; 47.3-48.4 |
| point read, 8 clients | -0.25%; -0.49% to +0.12% | -1.62% to +0.23% | -0.2%; -1.2% | -0.3% | 48.4-50.1; 49.7-51.2 |
| UPDATE one row, 8 clients | -0.44%; -0.72% to -0.12% | -2.41% to -0.23% | +0.9%; -1.0% | -1.1% | 51.2-56.0; 56.5-58.0 |

Memory: the range reads' is unchanged. The UPDATE's after-run value is where the memtable's
sawtooth stopped: both builds swing between about 50 and 85 MiB during the run (sampled each
second). Two rechecks after the rebase (five pairs each) gave the base 46.6-54.7 MiB after and the
change 48.6-79.7, with the same peaks (78-84 and 79-85 MiB) and run means 64-70 against 63-74; no
mechanism of the change holds memory on that path (its statement is a point update, whose tree
restarted before too), but the change's means ran a little higher in the first recheck.

The first change alone (binary): instructions -1.25% to -1.75%, cycles -1.13% to -3.77%, ops/s
+2.0%: what goes is the node allocations, `scan_need` and the tree's drop; the restart still makes
the ranges (`ranges_from`, 1.0% of samples), binds them (`bind_scan`, 0.9%) and decides whether
the bounds imply the filter (`filter_implied_plan`, 0.4%).

DataRows are the base's: the change makes no output of its own (the same `row_stored` and
`put_data_row` paths), and `tests/wire.rs` (72 tests, `stored_rows_go_out_as_decoded_ones`
included) passes.

Dropped (`~/.cache/a223/patches/C-readers-dropped.*`): agent 215's reader pool on the kept trees
(a restarted scan takes back its own store readers, moved over the new range at the new snapshot):
-0.15% to -1.00% instructions against the two changes.

Where the time goes now (LBR profile of the change, binary, connection threads; shares of all
samples, kernel included): planning 23.4% (`rechoose` 12.5%: paths and their costs 5%, the
selectivities 2.8%, `apply` 1.2%; placing the WHERE 4.3%; the rest the planning context, `lit_env`
and the plan's assembly); the scan 38% (`fill_batch` 12.7%, positioning the sources 4.9%, new
store readers 2.3%); the restart 3.2%; the DataRows from stored bytes 4.5%; the kernel's send at
ReadyForQuery 12.5% and receive 4.3%; the allocator 7.5% (`mi_malloc_aligned`) and 1.0% (free),
spread over some twenty call sites. In `rechoose` alone 4.7% of all samples are allocation, `Expr`
clones and their drops: each index clause owns three cloned expressions (`ClauseAt::made`), the
bitmap candidate clones the index path, the selectivity memo clones its key, and `apply`,
`index_quals` and `fill_bounds` clone them again into the plan. Costing only the chosen path when
the others provably lose would save about 1.5% (the alternatives here are a sequential scan and a
bitmap scan over the same index path) and needs a bound that holds where a bitmap heap's cost
falls as it reads more of the table; sharing the clauses (`Arc<Expr>` or indexes into
`rel.quals` through `IClause`, `IPath` and `apply`) is the larger lead.

## Executor, round 15 (2026-11-12, agent 219)

HANDOFF "Executor, round 15" has what changed: a parallel scan of a heap only inserted into,
some of its rows out of key order, reads chunks of keys in the workers with those rows put in
their places (no read and sort of every row on the executor's thread first), and a hash join's
reloaded batch of few outer rows keeps the inner rows none of them can match as stored, never
decoded. **base** is `abfa993e5`; `c005` the displaced rows against base, `c006` the batches
against `c005` (the dropped `c002` and `c004` against base). Dist builds (fat LTO, line tables)
of one checkout, equal-length paths (`~/.cache/a219/bin`).

Lane C: each server in its own container on CPUs 14,15,32,33 (two cores and their
hyperthreads, `--memory 8g`, host network), the client on 16,17,34,35 at nice 19, each pass
under `~/.cache/lane-c.lock` (shared with agents 217 and 220). The same settings on all three
servers: `max_parallel_workers_per_gather = 2`, `max_parallel_workers = 8` (PostgreSQL's
defaults, set on pgrust, whose own default is 4 / 16) and a 2 GB cache (the engine
`--cache-mb 2048`, PostgreSQL and pgrust `shared_buffers = 2GB`; pgrust's heap is also read
through the OS page cache, counted in its container's peak). PostgreSQL 18.6 with the bench
settings, pgrust `malisper/pgrust` (0.4-beta). TPC-H SF1 from `bench tpch-load --sf 1`; the
engine's data directory restored before every start. Other tenants kept the host at load 9-30;
each pass waited up to 60 s for the lane's CPUs to average under 30% busy.

## Analytic path, round 1 (2026-11-12, agent 220)

HANDOFF "Analytic path against pgrust" has the phase study and what changed: a parallel scan's

`max_parallel_workers_per_gather = 2` (pgrust's default is 4: set to 2 with ALTER SYSTEM); cache
budgets 2 GB each (the engine `--cache-mb 2048`, PostgreSQL and pgrust `shared_buffers = 2GB`;
pgrust's default is 128 MB). PostgreSQL 18.6 with the bench settings, pgrust `malisper/pgrust`

cycles by up to +-6% a round and q17 / q22's instructions by up to -2.4%, so the gate reads
instructions first.

### Memory at smaller block caches

The engine (c002) on q01, q06, q13 and q22 alone at `--cache-mb` 2048 / 1024 / 512 / 256: anon
1,188 / 1,183 / 662 / 381 MB; q01 CPU 1.99 / 1.91 / 2.65 / 3.37 s, q06 1.28 / 1.32 / 2.12 / 2.77 s.
At 256 MB, a block not cached costs its zstd decompression (q06: 25.6% of the cycles in
`ZSTD_decompressSequences`, 5% in its tables), `Block::decode_with` (5.5%) and the pread's copy
(1.3%). pgrust at 128 MB `shared_buffers`: 331 MB, q01 1.12 s CPU (its 8 kB pages copied from
the OS page cache: 12-22% of its cycles in the kernel's copy). PostgreSQL with
`shared_buffers = 2GB`: 1,204-1,243 MB on the same four.

|---|---:|---:|
| Bind (`exec_bind_message`: plan cache, portal, its point path's probe set up, `open_kept`) | 31% | 4.7 |

| transaction end (`CommitTransactionCommand`: `PreCommit_Portals` / `PortalDrop` 8%, lock release 3%) | 20% | 3.0 |
| the message loop's rest (read, flush, dispatch) | 21% | 3.2 |

Roll's read at 8 clients (this round's code, LBR profile, 28.7 µs user in that run): the
storage reads 22% (the index entry's probe 12.5%, the row's point read 10%: one SST file and
the memtable), the executor's restart 6% (`rescan_index` 5%), Bind 8% (the implicit
transaction's begin 4%), the transaction's end 9%, `run_query` around the executor 19%
(relation locks 5%, `begin_statement` 3%, the execution context 3.5%), the message read 14%
(user-mode polling of the socket while cores are free: roll counts it as user CPU, pgrust
sleeps). Against pgrust's phases roll loses most in the storage reads (about twice its btree
and heap fetch) and in `run_query`'s statement machinery, and wins at the transaction's end.

Dropped (`~/.cache/a215/patches/R-reader-pool-dropped.patch`, with its numbers): a connection
thread's finished scans' store readers kept (up to four, emptied at the idle hook) and moved over
the next scans' ranges at their new snapshots (`Store::scan_raw_reusing`, `Scan::reseek_at`:
allowed while the store's sources are the reader's). Against the change, five pairs: instructions
-0.5% to -1.0% (median -0.9%), cycles -0.6% to -2.9%, ops/s +2.1%, CPU -1.7%: the new scans it
saves (2.1% of samples) are mostly paid again in repositioning (the sources looked up again when
the index's and the table's readers trade places, the toast source, the gc horizon's lock).

The kernel send at ReadyForQuery is the same work in both servers: round 3's strace found one
`sendto` and one `recvfrom` per query on the engine (2.0 system calls per query) against
PostgreSQL's 8.5.

- `g3`'s first round is one where the compaction after DELETE ran late (8.0 G, VACUUM and
  count(*) still merging part of it); the other eight rounds have none. DELETE's own count moves
  ±3% between runs of one binary (round 11); the c1+c4 build without the compaction change
  (`new7`, `g5`) showed DELETE +5.3% in one round too. The sum of DELETE, its compaction,
  VACUUM, ANALYZE and count(*) is 16.8-18.2 G base, 17.0-17.1 G new in `g4`.
- Point reads and short transactions (`smoke.sh`, pgbench scale 10, prepared, 8 clients, 20 s,
  three interleaved rounds): select-only 54,234-54,278 user instructions a transaction base,
  52,778-52,834 new (-2.7% every round); tpcb-like 902-953 k base, 903-911 k new.

### Measured and not kept

Patches and notes in `~/.cache/a224/patches/` (HANDOFF lists them):
- **A parallel index build's entries as one merged run** (alone in its range, it goes to L1 at
  commit): CREATE INDEX -13..-15%, CREATE UNIQUE INDEX -13..-22%, both their compactions gone
  (12 MB written less), all steps 70.6 -> 60.0 G with the three kept. But nothing compacts
  the tiny L0 files a commit's memtable rotation leaves any more: the L0 count reached the
  trigger at the UPDATE's commit (a compaction while its snapshot was pinned kept the old
  versions, 1.5M entries in the table's file) and DELETE's deletions stayed under it (ANALYZE
  +43%, count(*) +64%, UPDATE +4.9%); and pgbench select-only +3.4% instructions a transaction
  (a 3 kB L0 file spanning every table: every `PointReader::first_raw` seeks in it).
- **Tiny L0 files not counting toward the trigger** (to put that timing back): built, not
  measured: it keeps the spanning tiny files longer, which is what cost the point reads.
- zstd at level 2 instead of 1 (offline, on the compaction's 16 kB blocks of the table and its
  indexes, `zstd` 1.5.7 as the crate's): row blocks 5.8% smaller for 2.5% more compression
  instructions, index blocks 0.5% larger; level 1's parameters are already its minimum
  (minMatch 5, hashLog 14), so compression can't get cheaper at the same size.

### Where the instructions still go

- The compaction after DELETE (6.4-7.1 G both arms): zstd compression a quarter, decompression
  and `Block::decode` of its inputs a quarter (an uncached read decodes each block to the cache
  form before the merge walks it), the merge's per-entry `min` / `head` / `advance` and
  `SstWriter::add` the rest. Its filter building (18% before) is gone.
- CREATE INDEX (2.1x PostgreSQL): writing the entries' run 29% (zstd 15%), the table's first
  read 23% (12% zstd decompression: COPY's runs never enter the block cache), the sorts 13%,
  `stored_tuple_len` and the row decode 15%.
- count(*) (2.7x PostgreSQL, 7.8x pgrust): `parscan::partial_row` and `LazyBuf::read` per row,
  and `sum(c)` of a float8 collected per row (`Partial::Floats`) and summed on the executor's
  thread.

## Bulk writes, round 12 (2026-11-12, agent 217)

HANDOFF.md, "Bulk writes, round 12", has what changed. Method as round 11's (agent 208's
harness, copied to `~/.cache/a217/bench`), on lane C: dist builds with line tables, each server
a fresh container on CPUs 14,15,32,33 (two whole cores; 8 GiB, `--cpu-shares 2`, host network),
psql and perf in containers on CPUs 16,17,34,35, 1M rows (`bulk1m.tsv`), autovacuum off; per
step the server cgroup's CPU and bytes written, `perf stat -e instructions:u,cycles:u` of the
server cgroup, VmHWM; rows checked equal on every run (count and sums of every column of both
tables). **base** is `d6e642356`, **new** base with the one change kept. Gated on user
instructions of settled rounds, interleaved, three or more rounds a pair.

New in the harness: **a settle is measured too**, as a step `bg_<step before it>`. Settled
rounds waited out the background work a step left (its flush and compaction) and counted it
nowhere: after DELETE that is a compaction of 7.7-9.0 G instructions and 45.7 MB written,
more than DELETE itself (6.5 G), which the step table (unsettled) puts in VACUUM's row. Gating
on user steps alone would have passed changes that only move work into the background.

|---|---|

- Peak RSS (VmHWM) 252-256 MB both, container peaks 289-317 MB both; PostgreSQL's 952-956.
  Bytes written per step equal (230.2-230.7 MB over the script in the runs where no
  compaction's fsync fell into the last step's window, both arms).
- Point reads and short transactions (`smoke.sh`: pgbench scale 10, prepared, 8 clients, 20 s,
  three interleaved rounds, with the compaction run change also in): select-only 54,142-54,216
  user instructions per transaction base, 54,177-54,213 new; tpcb-like 901-940 k base,
  899-934 k new; tps within 3% both ways.

### Measured and not kept

Patches and a note of each in `~/.cache/a217/patches/` (HANDOFF lists them):

- **Folding a bulk UPDATE's new versions into the table's file at its commit** (agent 208's
  lead; `fold-at-commit.patch`: `Store::fold_if_due` at the end of `Txn::commit`, a
  compaction when an L0 file of 64k entries or more holds a quarter as many entries as the L1
  files it overlaps). Settled, three rounds (`s1`): DELETE -13.1 / -15.1 / -13.9%, VACUUM
  -6.7 / -6.9 / -6.9%, the compaction after DELETE gone (-7.8 G), but UPDATE +27.5 / +27.5 /
  +27.6% (the fold: 3.7 G), ANALYZE +51.8 / +48.4 / +51.5% and count(*) +45.9% x3. All steps
  with background work 73.3 -> 64.2 G and 13 MB less written, mostly because DELETE's
  tombstones then stay in L0 (two files, under the L0 trigger), so the compaction that folds
  them never runs within the script (DROP TABLE discards them); the scans after DELETE pay
  for that, merging a deleted row's tombstone against its row (+0.5 G each in ANALYZE and
  count(*)). Folding the tombstones as well costs a compaction of the table and both indexes
  (~7 G) in DELETE. So the fold moves the compaction's cost, it doesn't remove it.
- **VACUUM's and ANALYZE's scans a batch at a time** (`batch-vacuum-analyze-scans.patch`):
  VACUUM +5.9% x3, ANALYZE +11.8 to +14.2%. Their scans put out-of-line values back
  (`TxnScan` with a TOAST source), so `TxnScan::fill_batch` falls back to one row a call
  through `next` and copies it into the batch.
- **Compaction taking a block's entries below the other inputs' heads in a loop of its own**
  (`compaction-block-runs.patch`, on top of the change kept): the compaction after DELETE
  -7.8 / -3.2 / -3.3 (`s3`), then -4.8 / -3.1 / -0.8 (`s4`): under 3% in every round.

### Where the instructions still go

- DELETE (3.8x PostgreSQL's instructions): unchanged in kind from round 11. The fold above
  shows what merging the UPDATE's versions with the table's rows costs it: 13-15% (0.9 G).
- The compaction after DELETE (6.3-6.9 G now, in VACUUM's row unsettled): zstd compression
  about a third, the merge's per-entry `min`/`fill`/`head` and `SstWriter::add` most of the
  rest. It rewrites the table and both indexes to drop 250k rows and 500k index entries.
- ANALYZE (3.4x) and VACUUM settled: the scan by `Iterator::next`, a row copied out per row
  (`Cursor::take`), and VACUUM's reads of the compacted file cold (zstd decompression 22%).
- count(*) (2.75x): `parscan::partial_row` and `LazyBuf::read`, a row decoded lazily per
  row; the storage scan is 14% of it.

cycles per transaction down in every pair of every series, but 8 clients' median reached the

`work-a218-final`). HANDOFF.md "Short write transactions, round 5" has the steps, the PGO
measurement and the leads.

2`; clients on 8,9,26,27), `short_txn` of 100,000 accounts, fsync and synchronous_commit on,
`CHECKPOINT` and 5 s of quiet before each run, user cycles and instructions of the connection
threads over 0.8-9.6 s of the 10 s run. Gate: user cycles per transaction down in every
unflagged pair and -5% at the median, tps up at the median and in no unflagged pair down.

Nothing kept: four steps measured, none met the keep gate (user cycles per transaction down in
every pair at 8 and 32 clients, median -5% at 32). Patches and numbers in
`~/.cache/a216/patches/` (`MEASURED.txt`), code on origin `wip/a216`. HANDOFF.md "Short write
transactions, round 4" has the design and the leads.
Harness: 213's (rebuilt in `~/.cache/a216/bench`) on the new lane A: servers on CPUs 6,7,24,25
(2 cores, 4 threads; 8 GiB, `--cpu-shares 2`), clients on 8,9,26,27, `short_txn` of 100,000
accounts, fsync and synchronous_commit on, a `CHECKPOINT` and 5 s of quiet before each run, user
instructions and cycles per transaction over 0.8-9.6 s of the 10 s run. Numbers are not comparable
with round 3's (4 cores then).

**Three servers, same night** (one round, `tri`; roll is `f41a4749e`; PostgreSQL's and pgrust's
own earlier round that evening gave 12,162 / 13,095 and 13,433 / 14,457 tps at 8 / 32 clients:
the host's other tenants move them by 10-20%):

| clients | server | tps | CPU µs/txn (user+sys) | p50 / p99 ms | peak MiB | roll ÷PG | roll ÷pgrust |
|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | roll | 2,719 | 333.4 (153.6+179.8) | 0.333 / 0.881 | 137 | 1.39x | 1.28x |
| | PostgreSQL | 1,955 | 304.1 (147.8+156.3) | 0.461 / 1.099 | 213 | | |
| | pgrust | 2,124 | 269.5 (149.5+120.1) | 0.431 / 1.045 | 459 | | |
| 8 | roll | 11,542 | 286.1 (172.5+113.6) | 0.619 / 2.227 | 138 | 0.87x | 0.76x |
| | PostgreSQL | 13,303 | 247.4 (119.3+128.1) | 0.545 / 1.717 | 283 | | |
| | pgrust | 15,163 | 214.2 (114.7+99.5) | 0.470 / 1.599 | 538 | | |
| 32 | roll | 10,611 | 340.7 (201.3+139.5) | 2.440 / 12.095 | 148 | 0.78x | 0.60x |

| | pgrust | 17,730 | 208.0 (114.6+93.4) | 1.672 / 6.618 | 624 | | |

**Steps** (5 alternating pairs each; Δ is the step over the arm before it):

1. *The size models' read side without the shard lock*: a heap publishes a `ReadSide` (its prune
   hints, whether stored TIDs are its, and an all-visible watch published by VACUUM, layouts and
   locked readers) that a thread keeps between statements; `Fetcher::new` and `Kills::new` take no
   lock while it is current; `updated` looks for the old version's out-of-line values under its
   write lock instead of a read lock before it, `inserted_with` checks the size limit in its write
   section. Shard lock acquisitions about 9 -> 4 a transaction (two `mark_passed` reads, two
   writes). 8 clients: ops +1.3% median (every pair up), CPU -1.0%, cycles -0.1%. 32 clients:
   ops +1.6%, CPU -2.6%, cycles 0.0% (-1.4..+3.8). Per transaction at 32: context switches 14.2
   -> 11.6, `sched_yield` 5.7 -> 3.3, futex 5.9 -> 5.7.
2. *Group commits' WAL records written by the fsync leader* (appended in memory under the commit
   lock, one write per fsync; `commit_async` and DDL commits write at once): pwrite a commit 1.01
   -> 0.55, fsyncs a commit 0.58 -> 0.54 (every commit still acknowledged after the fsync covering
   its record). Over step 1: 8 clients ops -0.4%, cycles +0.8%; 32 clients ops +1.8%, CPU
   -1.2%, cycles -0.5%.
3. *213's candidates 2 and 3* (lock bookkeeping without allocations, catalog and xids read
   without a lock, hot fields padded) on top of 1 and 2, all four against the base: 8 clients ops
   +1.0% (every pair up), CPU -1.4%, cycles -1.8% (one pair +0.3%), instructions -3.6%; 32 clients
   ops +3.0% (+1.2..+6.8), CPU -3.6%, cycles -2.2% (one pair +0.6%). 213's 32-client loss with
   candidate 2 is gone once step 1 takes the readers off the size models' lock. Context switches
   at 32 11.7 a transaction, `sched_yield` 3.2, futex 5.9.

## Short write transactions, round 3 (2026-11-11, agent 213)

Nothing kept: three candidates measured, all dropped (patches and numbers in
`~/.cache/a213/patches/`). HANDOFF.md "Short write transactions, round 3" has the leads.
Harness: agent 205's (`~/.cache/a213/bench`), servers on CPUs 32-35 (8 GiB, `--cpu-shares 2`),
clients on 4-7, `short_txn` of 100,000 accounts, fsync and synchronous_commit on, data on the
same disk for all three. Two changes to it: a `CHECKPOINT` and 5 s of quiet after the
fixture's reload, and the per-thread `perf stat` window over 0.8 s to 9.6 s of the 10 s run
(it was 1 s to 8 s; the ops in it were taken as the run's average rate, which moved every
symbol's count per transaction by the same 10-15% between runs of one binary). One binary's
user instructions per transaction still spread about ±4% from run to run.

**Three servers, same day** (`sb2`, one round, roll is `a9ced4585`):

| clients | server | tps | CPU µs/txn (user+sys) | p50 / p99 ms | peak MiB | roll ÷PG | roll ÷pgrust |
|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | roll | 2,415 | 328.5 (182.3+146.2) | 0.317 / 3.199 | 147 | 0.89x | 0.88x |
| | PostgreSQL | 2,699 | 233.2 (113.7+119.5) | 0.336 / 0.845 | 212 | | |
| | pgrust | 2,732 | 227.6 (127.8+99.8) | 0.350 / 0.766 | 363 | | |
| 8 | roll | 12,185 | 273.5 (170.1+103.5) | 0.576 / 2.195 | 148 | 0.79x | 0.70x |
| | PostgreSQL | 15,427 | 211.9 (103.7+108.3) | 0.463 / 1.511 | 286 | | |
| | pgrust | 17,403 | 181.0 (100.3+80.7) | 0.398 / 1.522 | 463 | | |
| 32 | roll | 11,420 | 298.9 (180.9+118.0) | 2.104 / 12.465 | 151 | 0.74x | 0.59x |
| | PostgreSQL | 15,369 | 231.9 (119.9+112.1) | 1.709 / 8.307 | 351 | | |
| | pgrust | 19,397 | 180.2 (103.5+76.6) | 1.392 / 8.501 | 569 | | |

(Roll's 1-client run had 0.76 cores of other tenants on its CPUs; quieter runs the same day
gave 2,923-2,952 tps. Roll's 1-client CPU includes up to 50 µs of polling for the client's
next message per round trip, about 25 µs a transaction.)

**Where a transaction's CPU goes** (8 clients, `a9ced4585`):

- All three servers are CPU-bound at about 3.1 of the 4 cores, so tps follows CPU per
  transaction. Kernel time is close (roll 89-105 µs, PostgreSQL 108-118, pgrust 81-84: TCP
  loopback and nftables dominate all three; fsync's block I/O is about 2% of cycles in each).
  The gap is user CPU: roll 158-171 µs against 100-115.
- User instructions per transaction: roll 279k at 0.52 instructions a cycle, PostgreSQL 215k
  at 0.64, pgrust 203k at 0.65 (`perf stat`, whole servers). Roll's cycles: 37% more
  instructions, the rest lower IPC: 21% of its user cycles are instruction-fetch stalls
  (`icache_16b.ifdata_stall`), 5% iTLB walks.
- Roll's work grows with clients; PostgreSQL's and pgrust's shrinks. User µs a transaction
  of the same functions (LBR call stacks), 1 / 8 / 32 clients: `execute_with` 115 / 138 /
  158, `mi_malloc_aligned` self 10.8 / 15.9 / 25.5, the lock slow paths 0 / 1.3 / 2.3.
  Context switches a transaction: roll 8.5 at 8 clients (4.6 waiting for the client, 1.06
  futex, 0.95 `sched_yield`, 0.75 in fdatasync, 1.1 preempted), 12.2 at 32 (3.6
  `sched_yield`, 2.8 futex); PostgreSQL about 7.5 at 8. The yields are parking_lot's spin
  before it parks: the sizing models' shard `RwLock` (`relfiles.of`: `Fetcher::new` and
  `mark_passed` reading, `sizing::updated` and `inserted_with` writing, every transaction),
  the commit mutex, `wait_durable`'s mutex, the sequence's mutex.
- perf c2c, 8 clients (HITM loads by line): the `RwLock<Arc<Xids>>` and `RwLock<Arc<Catalog>>`
  of `Database` (every `db.xids()` and `db.catalog()` is a lock and a count write), the
  `Arc<Database>` count beside `next_txn` and the fields every statement reads, `Store`'s
  `assigned` / `sync_assigned` written by each commit beside `visible` / `view` read by each
  statement, the `Xids` mutex beside its read-only fields, the server's `WORKING` /
  `SPINNING` counters (written around every message) beside `log::active` and `WAIT_SPIN_NS`.
- Roll's user time by part (171 µs, `a9ced4585`, 8 clients): the UPDATE 44.6 (its index
  scan 27.6; after the row is found 13: `sizing::updated` 5.5, the row lock 3.4, TOAST's
  replace 1.3), the INSERT 25.6 (`sizing::inserted_with` 9.1 of which btree model 5.9, the
  identity key's unique probe 5.2, new row 2.5), COMMIT 20 (the memtable's skiplist 7.1 under
  the commit mutex, `release_locks` 3.7, pgstat 2.0, the `Txn`'s drop 1.6, `wait_durable` 1.1,
  the WAL record 0.6), Bind 6.7, the allocator 18 across everything (125 allocations and 127
  frees a transaction, counted with uprobes). The heap and btree models (the sizing models)
  together are about 22 µs, which PostgreSQL and pgrust spend on their real pages instead.
- Group commit: roll fsyncs 0.70 times a commit at 8 clients (0.53 at 32), PostgreSQL 0.46 and
  pgrust 0.45; roll's fsyncs are shorter (one 220-byte record a commit, one pwrite a commit),
  so its leader is free again sooner. Each leaves about the same CPU: not the gap.

**Candidates** (5 alternating pairs, 8 clients unless noted; base `a9ced4585`):

1. *Memtable keys inline* (agent 197's / 199's `memkey`: `SmallVec<[u8; 40]>` keys, no
   `mvcc_key` allocation): first run (old window) instructions -3.9% to -7.0% in four pairs,
   +0.3%..-5% ops; second run (new window): instructions +3.2%, +1.1%, -6.2%, -7.2%, +5.7%,
   cycles -2.1%..+2.0%, ops +1.6% median. Dropped: no difference the measurement can see.
2. *Lock bookkeeping without allocations* (lock keys inline, `SmallVec<[u8; 24]>`; the lock
   tables and lists kept by the thread between transactions; `release_locks` without a `Vec`
   per key; statement-trigger marks in an inline list) together with *shared state read
   without writes* (`Database::catalog` and `xids` as `ArcSwap`, read without a lock or a
   count; `shared_search_path` borrowing the catalog; `Txn::commit` borrowing its database;
   the spin counters on lines of their own). 8 clients, run 1: instructions -4.2%, -4.8%,
   -4.0%, +1.4% (one pair lost its counts), ops +0.8% median; run 2 (quiet: every pair
   12.4-13.0k): ops +3.4%, +0.5%, +1.9%, +1.2%, +1.7%, CPU -3.3%, -0.8%, -1.9%, -1.3%,
   -1.5%, cycles -3.5%, -1.1%, -2.1%, 0.0%, -1.9%, instructions flat. 32 clients, three
   pairs: ops -1.8%, -6.0%, -20.4%, CPU +2.8% to +11.1%, kernel time up in all three
   (`sched_yield` 3.60 -> 4.07 and futex 2.80 -> 3.15 a transaction): the faster parts bring
   more threads to the sizing models' lock at once. Dropped (no pair rule met at 8; worse at
   32).
3. *Hot fields on lines of their own* (`Padded<T>`, 128-byte aligned: `Store`'s view, commit,
   visible, assigned, durable, sync and snapshot locks; the `Xids` mutex; `Database` aligned so
   the `Arc` counts don't share its first fields' line; `next_txn`), on top of 2: built, not
   measured (the build queue held it past the round).

## Executor, round 14 (2026-11-11, agent 212)

HANDOFF "Executor, round 14" has what changed: a dense bitmap scan's entries counted a batch at
a time with each distinct value checked once, the workers' non-unique index lookups sought on
one scan, and decoded blocks kept in allocations of their own size. **base** is `a9ced4585`;
each change was measured against the one before it (`c001` the dense count, `c003` the lookup
scans, `c004` the block allocations; `c002`, dropped, against `c001`). Dist builds (fat LTO, line
tables) of snapshots of one checkout, equal-length paths (`~/.cache/a212/bin`); PostgreSQL 18.6
with the bench settings and pgrust (`malisper/pgrust`, 0.4-beta, its defaults) as `run.sh`
starts them; each server in its own container on CPUs 8-11 (`--memory 8g`, host network, the
engine with `--cache-mb 2048`), the client on 12-15 at nice 19, TPC-H SF1 from

between passes of one binary, so the gate is the user instructions; the wall table below is the

  1,778 -> 1,632 MB, q10 1,865 -> 1,715, q13 1,840 -> 1,701, every query 135-150 MB lower; the
  other changes within +-10 MB.

### Where the memory went

A sampled heap profile of base after one pass (`--features heapprof`): 1.54 GB live, of it 1.28
GB decoded blocks (`Block::decode_with`), bloom filters 22 MB, the heap and btree models about
30 MB. RSS was 1.83 GB: the rest was the allocator's. A decoded block was built in a buffer the
size of the raw block and shrunk with `shrink_to_fit`; mimalloc's realloc keeps a block in place
when it shrinks by less than half, so each cached block held its raw size. Copying it into an
allocation of its own size (c004) took 145 MB off RSS and the cgroup peak.

### Dropped: a per-thread front table before the block cache

`c002`: each thread's 1,024-slot direct-mapped table of weak references to the blocks it found
last, before the shared shards' mutex and hash (a hit: no mutex, no probe). Against c001, three
rounds: user instructions +0.1 to +0.9% on every query, cycles -6 to +7% (noise), wall medians
q02 0.770 -> 0.822, q20 0.418 -> 0.437, q07 0.586 -> 0.567. The workers' lookups land on too many
different blocks for a small table to hit, and a miss pays for the table too. Patch:
`~/.cache/a212/patches/dropped-c002-block-cache-front.patch`.

## Executor, round 13 (2026-11-11, agent 207)

HANDOFF "Executor, round 13" has what changed: q21's plan made PostgreSQL's (three planner
fixes), a parallel aggregation's parts of fixed-width keys and states kept as words, 204's
order-free parts on top of them, and the workers' unique-key lookups read by a point probe.
**base** is `db6830deb`; each change was measured against the one before it (`pl01` the
planner fixes, `cp01` compact parts, `up01` order-free parts, `lk01` unique lookups). Dist
builds (fat LTO, line tables) of the same checkout, equal-length paths (`~/.cache/a207/bin`);
PostgreSQL 18.6 with the bench settings; each server in its own container on CPUs 8-11
(`--memory 8g`, host network, the engine with `--cache-mb 2048`), the client on 12-15 at
nice 19, TPC-H SF1 from `bench tpch-load --sf 1`, the data directory restored before every
engine start. Other tenants used about one core of CPUs 8-15 throughout (the runs waited for
them to average below 20-25% busy).

### Dropped: an index scan's heap fetches as point reads

`get_sorted` (one merging scan sought from key to key) against `get_many_snap` (a point read
each), one binary, the path switched by an environment variable (patch in
`~/.cache/a207/patches/experiment-point-fetch.patch`), one round: q20 -1.8%, q03 -1.7%, q17
-1.0%, q05 +1.1%, everything else within 1%. The storage's point lookups (`Block::seek`,
`SstReader::find_block`, `block_with`) cost about the same either way.

## Point reads, round 3 (2026-11-11, agent 210)

HANDOFF.md "Point reads, round 3" has what changed. Agent 205's harness (`~/.cache/a210/bench`):
dist builds in a container on CPUs 32-35 (8 GiB, swap off, `--cpu-shares 2`), clients on
4-7; 100,000 accounts, 2 s warmup, 10 s measured; user instructions and cycles per operation
from `perf stat --per-thread` of the connections' threads (a privileged helper, `a210-perf`);
a pair is run again when another tenant used more than 0.3 cores of the server CPUs. From
the afternoon on every run held `~/.cache/lane-32-35.lock` (agent 213 shares the server
CPUs). **base2** is `10d70e555` (the head this round's commits are on), **base1** `5a67e195a`
and **base0** `0dba28977` (round 2's head) the heads the candidates were measured on before.

**Three servers, same day** (`sb3`: two rounds, each server started once a round; roll is
this round's head, PostgreSQL 18.6 and pgrust `malisper/pgrust:v0.4-beta-amd64` as in rounds
1 and 2; medians):

Prepared point selects at 8 clients: 1.25x PostgreSQL (round 2: 1.14x) and 0.76x pgrust
(0.67x); user CPU 18.2 µs a read against pgrust's 11.5 (round 2: 22.5 against 12.5). Short
transactions are agent 213's from here on; they stay behind both (user 141 µs against 96).

**Where the time went at 8 clients** (base0, `perf record` of cycles:u and instructions:u,
600k samples, then `perf c2c` of a run of the same server): 70k user cycles a read for 49k
instructions, CPI 1.42 (1 client: 1.13). `perf c2c` sampled 4,009 loads that hit a line
another core had modified (HITM, about 200 cycles each), on these lines:

| line (HITM samples) | written by | read by |
|---|---|---|
| the catalog's `RwLock` word (426) | every `db.catalog()` (lock, unlock) | `bound_as`, `shared_search_path`, `finish_txn`, `accept_catalog`, `catalog_version`, `begin_statement` |
| the id table's `RwLock` and its count (275, 131) | `db.xids()`, `SnapshotXmin` | `start_activity` (`current_xmin`, under the id table's mutex), `finish_xid`, `begin_statement`, `emit` |
| `Database` around `next_txn` (239) | `Database::begin` | `begin_statement`, `get_many_snap_with`, `read_entries`, `Txn::lock` |
| the store's pin table (166) | `pin_visible`, `pin`, `SnapshotPin::drop` | the same |
| the id table's state mutex (164) | `register_snapshot_of`, `SnapshotXmin::drop` | the same |
| the visibility watch's count (155, 153) | `Kills::new` and its drop (an `Arc` clone a rescan) | `Kills::passed`, `mark` (`VisWatch::holds`) |
| statics around `WORKING` / `SPINNING` (154) | every message (`Waiting`) | `set_ps_idle` (`log::active`) |

The functions touching them ran at CPI 3 to 12 (`pin_visible` 12.6, `Store::pin` 10.4,
`SnapshotPin::drop` 9.7, `point_reader` 6.3, `begin_statement` 5.1).

**Kept**: four commits, each removing cache lines every read shared, measured as one change
(alone each is within this host's noise) and in steps on the older heads.

The round's commits against base2 (landD, alternating pairs of dist builds):

| cell | pairs | Δ ops/s | Δ CPU/op | Δ instr/op | Δ cycles/op | cycles by pair |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|
| point select, 8 clients | 5 | +4.3% | -5.9% | +1.6% | -8.0% | -5.5% to -10.4% |
| point select, 1 client | 3 | +6.0% | -4.0% | -1.8% | +1.1% | -17.0% to +1.3% |
| point select, 32 clients | 3 | -0.1% | +0.2% | +2.8% | -3.3% | -5.1% to -2.2% |
| range read (100 rows), 8 clients | 3 | -1.6% | +1.2% | +0.3% | +0.1% | -0.2% to +1.7% |
| short transactions, 8 clients | 3 | -0.2% | -0.9% | +0.7% | -2.1% | -3.1% to -0.0% |

Run again (3 pairs each): range reads -0.6% ops/s, +0.8% CPU (pairs -2.8% to +3.4%), so
over six pairs -1.5% ops/s and +1.0% CPU, pairs on both sides of zero: within this host's
noise, but not a gain; 32 clients +1.0% ops/s, -1.6% CPU, cycles -4.2%.

Point select, 8 clients, every pair:

| pair | base2 ops/s | landD ops/s | Δ ops/s | base2 µs/op | landD µs/op | Δ CPU | Δ instr | Δ cycles |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | 113,937 | 120,672 | +5.9% | 31.0 | 29.1 | -6.3% | +1.6% | -6.7% |

Before (landA, the same commits with the threads' copies kept until the thread read again,
against base1): point select 8 clients +4.9% ops/s, -5.5% CPU, cycles -10.0% (every pair
-7.3% to -12.6%); 1 client +0.9%, +0.1%; 32 clients +5.9%, -5.7%; range +1.0%, -1.4%; short
+2.6%, -2.2%. The copies are now emptied when the value is replaced (an idle connection held
an old view's memtables and files), at the cost of a mutex of the thread's own per read of
a copy. And on base0, step by step (point select, 8 clients, 5 pairs): the pins and xmins
alone cycles -4.1% median (one pair disturbed); with the published catalog, id table and
view -5.2% (every pair -2.1% to -6.4%, ops/s +2.5%); all four -6.6% (every pair -4.1% to
-8.2%, ops/s +4.9%, CPU -4.8%); the four on base0's other cells: 1 client +0.7% ops/s, -0.6%
CPU; 32 clients +5.8%, -4.9%; range +0.6%, -0.7%; short transactions +1.4%, -1.9% (cycles
-6.0% every pair).

Instructions per read stay level (the slots' fences, the copies' lookups and the counters'
sums replace the atomics they remove); the gain is in cycles: the read stopped waiting for
lines other cores held. As round 2 suspected, part of roll's gap to pgrust at 8 clients was
sharing, not only path length.

Footprint (fresh server each, cgroup anon+shmem 25 s into 100 idle sessions, two of each,
alternating; fresh data directory 3.03 MB each): base2 36.4 / 36.5 MB, landD 36.8 / 36.7 MB
(+0.3 MB, 0.9%: each connection's thread keeps three copies and its session two slots).

**Dropped** (`~/.cache/a210/patches/dropped-update-point-path.*`): an UPDATE's or DELETE's
scan of one unique key ending at its visible row (`unique_point_plan` without the `with_key`
exclusion; `roll-point-update.spec`, kept, gives PostgreSQL 18.6's output with it). Short
transactions, 8 clients, 5 pairs each: on base0 +4.9% ops/s, -4.7% CPU, cycles -5.2% (one
pair +0.5%); on base1 +1.3%, -0.8%, cycles +2.1% (pairs -7.4% to +3.0%), and again +1.1%,
-1.5%, cycles -2.1% with one pair -14.9% ops/s: unclear.

**Where a read goes now** (LBR call stacks of cycles:u, 8 clients, the commits on base1 with
the earlier copies, a run another tenant disturbed: 24.5 µs user a read there): allocation
3.4 µs (`mi_malloc` 2.9 self: `Txn::lock`'s keys and sets 0.4, the message loop 0.3, a block
entry read in `Block::seek` 0.3, `first_raw` 0.3, `fetch_entries` 0.2, `bind_scan` 0.14, the
transaction's settings 0.13); the index probe 2.4 and the row's point read 1.9; the
executor's restart 1.8 (`rescan_index` 1.55); Bind 2.0, its Describe 1.3; the transaction's
end 2.2 (`Txn::commit` 1.0).

## Bulk writes, round 11 (2026-11-11, agent 208)

HANDOFF.md, "Bulk writes, round 11", has what changed. Method as round 10's (agent 201's
harness, copied to `~/.cache/a208/bench`): dist builds with line tables, each server a fresh
container on CPUs 0-3 (8 GiB, `--cpu-shares 2`, host network), psql in a third container on
CPUs 4-7, 1M rows (`bulk1m.tsv`), autovacuum off; per step the server cgroup's CPU and bytes
written, `perf stat -e instructions:u,cycles:u` of the server cgroup, VmHWM; rows checked
equal on every run (count and sums of every column of both tables). Each change was gated on
**settled** rounds (`settle` before every step: a step's count then carries no earlier step's
background compaction, only its own), interleaved, three rounds. **base** is `5a67e195a`
(origin with agent 201's L0 fix), **new** the three changes on it (pushed rebased as
`10d70e555`).

CPU seconds and cycles were not usable this round. CPUs 0-3's hyperthread siblings are 18-21,
inside the build CPUs 16-23, and other agents' builds kept 3.1-3.5 of those 4 busy through
nearly every run (`bulk2.sh` records it, `sib`); the server's cycles per instruction then
moved by 20-80% from one run to the next of the same binary while its instruction count
moved by under 1% (COPY, UPDATE, ANALYZE, count) to 3-5% (DELETE and the parallel index
builds, whose background work and participants' shares vary with timing). The CPU column
below is the median of six unsettled runs per arm, as noisy as that implies; the instruction
column is the comparison that holds.

| **all steps** | 9.14 CPU s / 52.1 G | 10.49 / 75.7 | 9.92 / 73.7 | 1.15 -> 1.09 | 1.45 -> **1.41** | |

(Unsettled, VACUUM's row carries DELETE's background compaction: 11 G instructions against
2.5 G settled. Round 10's 0.98x of PostgreSQL's CPU over all steps was measured with the
siblings quieter; with them this busy the same base measures 1.15x.)

### The changes, gated on settled rounds (per round %, user instructions)

| step | **new against base** (`s6`) | `stored_tuple_len` debug-only counter | index build's keys in place | COPY's plain lines parsed in place |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| COPY in | -4.1 / -4.1 / -4.1 | +0.0 x3 | (COPY not touched) | **-4.2 / -4.2 / -4.2** |
| CREATE INDEX | **-7.3 / -5.4 / -7.9** | cycles -26.8 / -10.8 / -13.6, -6.7 / -1.0 / -2.8 / -12.7 / -2.5 | **-11.1 / -7.3 / -6.5** | |
| CREATE UNIQUE INDEX | **-9.3 / -10.1 / -17.5** | cycles -26.0 / -7.5 / -2.2, -20.6 / -19.9 / -5.6 / +1.2 / -20.7 | **-8.8 / -13.0 / -6.6** | |
| every other step | within 2% but DELETE +1.6 / -3.2 / -1.6, VACUUM's first round (the base's left-over compaction) | within 0.5% | | |

- The counter's effect is in cycles, not instructions (a contended `lock xadd`): eight
  interleaved rounds against round 10's head (`s2`, `ix`), CREATE INDEX's cgroup CPU better
  in 6 of 8 (median -11.5%, worst +4.6%), CREATE UNIQUE INDEX's in 8 of 8 (median -12.1%);
  `stored_tuple_len` 7.5% -> 5.2% of CREATE INDEX's cycles and 10.9% -> 5.7% of CREATE UNIQUE
  INDEX's (perf record, LBR), the `lock` instruction half of the function's samples before.
- The index-build change against its predecessor in `s2` (`c6` against `c3`, which differ
  elsewhere only in DELETE's and COPY's paths). The COPY change against the stack without it
  (`nf` against `n9`, `s5`), deterministic to 0.1%.
- Peak RSS (VmHWM, nine runs each): base 271-277 MB, new 272-280, both medians 274 (the peak
  is during UPDATE, which no change touches); container peaks 308-375 / 310-354;
  PostgreSQL's 952. Bytes written per step equal
  (the totals differ only by where background compaction fell).
- Behaviour: COPY edge cases (`~/.cache/a208/data/edge`: a bad int, an out-of-range int, a
  missing and an extra column, `\N`, an escaped tab, HEADER true and MATCH, the end marker,
  `DELIMITER '|'`, `NULL ''`, a column list, `DEFAULT` on a column without one, ON_ERROR
  ignore with `log_verbosity verbose` and with `reject_limit 1`, NOT NULL, a unique violation
  past 1,000 rows) print the same rows, errors, contexts and notices on base, new and
  PostgreSQL 18.6.

### Measured and not kept

Patches and a note of each in `~/.cache/a208/patches/` (HANDOFF lists them): the merge's
re-ranking of the source it moved (DELETE -1.9 / -4.3 / +0.5), compact lock sets growing
without rehashing and `%` in 64 bits (DELETE -7.3 / -2.2 / -3.6, then +3.3 / -1.1 / -2.6 with
the SST prefix change), COPY's feed taken a batch at a time with the load's maps on the fast
hasher (COPY -1.9 x3), the run writer's key buffer (COPY -0.6), DELETE's tombstone arena
(+4.6 / -0.8 / -3.9), the SST writer's word-at-a-time shared prefix (about -0.5), relsize's
mask alignment (cycles both ways), agent 191's two-source merge a pair at a time (DELETE
+1.5 / -7.6 / -3.3 against `10d70e555`; `Merge::min` 13.5 -> 2.4% and `Scan::position`
17.2 -> 0.1% of DELETE's instructions, `Scan::fill_batch` 24.2 -> 21.2%).

### Where the instructions still go (base, `perf record -e instructions:u`, settled)

- DELETE (3.7x PostgreSQL's instructions): the scan 44% (`Merge::min` 12-14%, almost all of
  it the per-entry `fill` / `head` of the current source; `ScanNode::emit` 26%: the filter's
  kernel, lazy column decode), the tombstones 18% (`SstWriter::add` 5%, a third of it the
  bloom's byte-at-a-time FNV hash, the sort 2%), the row locks 7% (`KeySet::grow` 2.2%,
  `__modti3` 1.3%), background compaction 4-7%.
- COPY in (1.6x): `parse_copy_data` 16% before this round (now about 11%), zstd 11%, the
  per-row heap-size model 13% (`sizing::inserted_with`: two shard locks, SipHash maps,
  `heap_fillfactor` parsing the table's options), `SstWriter::add` and the bloom 6%.
- Index builds (2.2x, 3.2x): zstd decompression of the table 7-8%, the entries' sort 9%,
  `SstWriter::add` 7% (the bloom hash a fifth), the measure's `stored_tuple_len` 5%.

(The three-server run's roll is this round's code before its rebase onto agent 203's round 4,
`6aaa71d13`, as are the tables below but the last.) Prepared point selects: roll is now ahead
of PostgreSQL at every client count (1.14x at 8, 1.22x at 32) and still 0.67-0.72x of pgrust at
8 and 32. The gap is user CPU: 22.5 µs a read against pgrust's 12.5 (round 1: 30.2 against
12.9); kernel CPU is close (13.3 against 11.4). Short transactions are about as round 1 left
them: 0.92x PostgreSQL, 0.78x pgrust (user 152.9 µs against 103-105).

**Where a read goes now** (prepared point select, 8 clients, this round's head, LBR call
stacks of cycles:u, 102k samples, 23.2 µs user a read): the index entry's probe 2.1
(`first_raw`: block search and seek 1.0, the version lookup 0.3) and the row's point read 2.0
(`file_get` 1.35); the executor's restart 1.6 (`rescan_index` 1.4: the key's range bytes,
`bind_scan`'s per-scan counts); the row's decode and checks (`emit`) 1.2; Bind 2.0 (Describe's
binding check 1.4, of which the implicit transaction's begin 0.9); the read-only commit 2.4
(`Txn::commit` 1.3, of which the `Txn`'s drop 0.6); statement begin 0.7, locks and privileges
0.8; wire encode 0.7; mimalloc 2.2 self across all of it.

**Behaviour, against PostgreSQL 18.6** (`tests/isolation/specs/roll-statement-snapshot.spec`;
the scenarios' psql transcripts for both servers in `~/.cache/a205/beh`):

| what | PostgreSQL 18.6 | roll before | roll now |
|---|---|---|---|
| backend_xmin of a query that waited for a lock while the holder aborted | moves on (768 -> 769) | stays | moves on |
| the same, the holder committed | moves on (771 -> 772) | stays (shown); registered xmin renewed | moves on |
| SQL EXECUTE that waited, holder aborted | stays | stays | stays |
| rows SQL EXECUTE sees of the commit it waited for | not seen (1 of 2) | seen | not seen |
| rows a simple query / Bind sees of it | seen | seen | seen |
| backend_xmin idle in a READ COMMITTED transaction block | NULL | NULL | NULL |
| VACUUM while a READ COMMITTED reader idles in its block | removes every dead row | (as PG) | (as PG) |
| pg_stat_statements total_exec_time of a statement that waited 2 s for a lock | 0 ms | 2,011 ms | 0 ms |
