## Range reads, round 6 (2026-11-11, agent 211)

HANDOFF.md, "Range reads round 6", has what changed: a kept plain index scan's rows go to the
client from their stored values (`Node::each_picked`, `RowSink::row_stored`), a binary DataRow of
null and fixed-width values written from the stored bytes without decoding the row. Agent 206's
harness, copied to `~/.cache/a211/h` (dist builds in containers with PostgreSQL's limits, the
server on CPUs 24-27 and the client on 28-31, 100,000 accounts, 2 s warmup, 10 s measured, every
row value-checked; user instructions and cycles of the connection threads, `perf stat
--per-thread`). New this round: each server container's own cycles (`perf stat -a -G` over its
cgroup: every process and thread, background ones included) for the three-server table, a
pgrust arm, and a client count.

**PostgreSQL's plan on this statement.** On PostgreSQL 18.6 with the bench's table the prepared
range read stays on custom plans (`pg_prepared_statements`: 0 generic, 8 custom after 8
executions): the generic plan's estimate is 500 rows (cost 25.30) against about 10.4 for a
custom plan plus its planning charge. Planning again at every Bind is what PostgreSQL does here
too; a kept generic plan would be a behaviour difference.

The change against base (`fce790984`), five alternating pairs, the prepared 100-row range read
at 8 clients (other tenants' load on 24-27 at most 0.21 cores in every run):

| pair | base ops/s | new ops/s | base us CPU / op | new us CPU / op | instr / op | cycles / op |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | 33,305 | 37,890 | 104.9 | 92.1 | -18.6% | -14.0% |
| 2 | 33,036 | 36,712 | 104.9 | 94.0 | -17.9% | -12.7% |
| 3 | 33,377 | 37,544 | 103.9 | 92.3 | -18.2% | -13.0% |
| 4 | 33,570 | 36,521 | 104.5 | 93.7 | -18.1% | -12.2% |
| 5 | 32,721 | 35,967 | 105.5 | 96.0 | -17.5% | -11.2% |

Medians: ops/s 33,305 -> 36,712 (+10.2%), CPU per operation 104.9 -> 93.7 us (-10.7%), user
instructions 430k -> 353k (-18.0%), p50 0.222 -> 0.198 ms; memory 54-56 MiB on both.

Rebased onto `a9ced4585` (the runner made generic over its closure, below), against that base,
under the machine's load as it is now (coordinator: the other tenants are the user's own
sessions; a run waits at most 60 s for them, a pair isn't run again for load, one above a core
is flagged). Instructions and cycles per operation are the gate; ops/s and CPU are noisy:

| workload | pairs | instr / op (median; range) | cycles / op (range) | ops/s (median, noisy) | CPU / op (median, noisy) |
|---|---:|---|---|---:|---:|

| UPDATE one row, 8 clients | 5 | -1.0%; -4.3% to +0.4% | -8.8% to +0.1% | +2.3% | -1.7% |

(Flagged, more than a core of other load in a run: the 8-client range read's pair 3 (1.10 on
its base run), the 1-client read's pairs 1, 2 and 5 (1.04-1.37).) The
1-client read waits on the round trip, so its ops/s barely move while its CPU drops. The

version, with the runner's per-row callback a `dyn` closure, measured +1.05% instructions on the
point read (-0.9% to +2.0%), and the generic one brought it back.

**Same day, the three servers** (one round each, the machine loaded, other tenants 0.06-0.49
cores; cycles are the server container's, all its processes and threads):

| server | ops/s | us CPU / op | user cycles / op | all cycles / op | MiB after |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | 30,180 | 117.8 | 306k | 429k | 103 |
| pgrust | 28,627 | 126.6 | 395k | 477k | 184 |
| roll, base `a9ced4585` | 27,434 | 130.0 | 382k | 461k | 54 |

Earlier the same day on a quieter machine (one run each): PostgreSQL 31,655 ops/s, 111.0 us

wasn't profiled. Roll with the change takes about 8% fewer cycles per operation than PostgreSQL
in all (less kernel time per operation), 5% less CPU, half the memory; its user cycles are still
about 5% above PostgreSQL's, so the round's goal (10% fewer cycles) isn't met yet.

DataRows: `~/.cache/a211/edge/binscan.py` (nine prepared scans of a table of fixed-width, text,
NULL and added columns, four result-format sets, three executions each) gives the same bytes
from both bases and both builds of the change; `tests/wire.rs`
`stored_rows_go_out_as_decoded_ones` checks the same against the decoding path.

The LBR profile with the change (connection threads; shares of the samples): planning
(`planned_as`) 23.1% (choosing the scan again 12.3%, placing the WHERE 4.1%), the index
entries' read 9.2% and the rows' dense scan 12.3% (`TxnScan::fill_batch` 12.1% of both,
positioning, `Scan::position`, 5.2%), the DataRows from the stored bytes 3.6%, the kernel send at
ReadyForQuery 13.6%, reading the next message 7.1%, reopening the kept tree 5.0%, the

the sink's row path 4.3%, the allocator 9.8% of leaf samples.

stack buffer copied in. Five pairs over the planning change: +0.57% median instructions
(-0.3% to +0.7%), -2.1% ops/s: the buffer's checks per value cost more than the zeroing.

PostgreSQL 18.6, the same session and CPUs (ops/s, us CPU / op, MiB after the run, p99 ms),
alternating with the engine with the change: three single runs while the gates built on
CPUs 16-23, PostgreSQL 34,095 / 101.9 / 103 / 0.553, 29,330 / 117.7 / 103 / 0.595, 31,999 /
107.8 / 103 / 0.572 against the engine's 32,806 / 104.9 / 51 / 0.590, 29,485 / 117.3 / 55 /
0.604, 34,454 / 101.2 / 55 / 0.540; then four pairs, PostgreSQL 33,353, 33,276, 33,751,
32,627 against 33,559, 33,552, 33,642, 33,356 (+0.6%, +0.8%, -0.3%, +2.2%; CPU per operation
-1.8% to +0.9%). The engine is level with PostgreSQL on this read (median +0.7% ops/s), on
about half its memory, not clearly past it.

The LBR profile of base this round started from (111k samples, shares of the server's user
samples): planning (`planned_as`) 21.1% (choosing the scan again 12.4%: path generation 6.6%
of it, `match_quals` 1.5%, `index_pathkeys` 0.45%, `index_only` 0.4%, the bitmap OR search's
clause copy 0.3%, selectivity 2.6% with the live endpoint 0.86%; placing the WHERE 3.6%;

stored bytes (`emit`) 10.2%, the kernel send at ReadyForQuery 12%, reading the next message
5.5%, the transaction's end 2.7%, Bind 2.2%, Describe 1.4% (the transaction it starts, 0.9%).
With the change: planning 19.4%, choosing the scan again 10.5% (path generation 4.4%), the
endpoint 0.62% (only its last-bin reverse scan left).

## Range reads, round 4 (2026-11-11, agent 203)

HANDOFF.md, "Range reads round 4", has what changed: a secondary index scan hands each row out
from the batch its rows were read into, and a DataRow of fixed-width binary values is written
in one copy. Agent 198's harness (copied to `~/.cache/a203/h`): dist builds in containers, the
server on CPUs 24-27 and the client on 28-31, 100,000 accounts, 8 clients, 2 s warmup, 10 s
measured, every row value-checked, user instructions and cycles from `perf stat -e
instructions:u,cycles:u` over 9 s of each run, a pair run again when another tenant used more
than 0.3 cores of the server's CPUs. The prepared 100-row range read, five alternating pairs
per step. **base** is `c92dd8116`.

Two harness changes. The candidate binary now sits at a path as long as the baseline's
(`bin/newbuild/`, was `bin/`): three A/A pairs (one binary on both arms) with the old paths
showed the candidate arm +1.2% to +4.0% ops/s, three with equal paths -3.2% to +0.2%
(instructions within 0.6% either way in both). And a process pinned to the CPUs is reported
but no longer refuses a run (only their load, the 20% idle wait and the foreign-core check
do): other agents' builds and verification trains were pinned to 16-31 for hours.

## Executor, round 12, batch 2 (2026-11-11, agent 204)

HANDOFF "Executor, round 12, batch 2" has what changed: a Parallel Hash's inner rows read and
keyed in the scan's workers, and the workers' nested-loop lookups keeping their reader and
stopping a semi or anti join's at its first row. **base** is origin's head `c92dd8116`;
**new** is the two commits on top of it. Both are dist builds (fat LTO, line tables) of the
same checkout (`~/.cache/a204/bin`); PostgreSQL 18.6 with the bench settings; each server in
its own container on CPUs 8-11 (`--memory 8g`, host network; the engine with
`--cache-mb 2048`), the client on 12-15 at nice 19, TPC-H SF1 from `bench tpch-load --sf 1`.

**The data directory is restored before every engine start** (`~/.cache/a204/scripts/r.sh`).
Each clean close of the engine leaves a small L0 file (150-180 bytes) that nothing compacts
while nothing else is written; after ~400 starts and stops one directory had 413 of them, and
point reads and scans consult every one: q18 went from 72 to 363 billion user instructions a
run, q04 from 5.7 to 11.9, q01 (a full scan) stayed at 30.3. The per-change gates below that
ran on such an aging directory say so.

## Executor, round 12 (2026-11-10, agent 200)

HANDOFF "Executor, round 12, batch 1" has what changed: a Parallel Hash Join's table is sized
from the participants' combined hash_mem, as PostgreSQL's shared table is, and a merge join
PostgreSQL's plan has as a Parallel Hash Join under its Gather runs as that hash join.
**base** is origin's head `a96bce378`; **new** is this batch on top of it (`c4`). Both are dist
builds (fat LTO, line tables) of the same checkout, copied aside with their manifests
(`~/.cache/a200/bin`).

How it was run: each server in its own container on CPUs 8-11 (`--memory 8g` both;
PostgreSQL 18.6 with the bench settings, `shared_buffers=2GB`, host network; the engine with
`--cache-mb 2048` through `run.sh ENGINE_DOCKER=1`), the client on 12-15 at nice 19, TPC-H SF1
from `bench tpch-load --sf 1` on both. A round is a fresh engine, a warm-up pass, then 3
measured passes per query (their median); rounds interleave PostgreSQL, new, base, then base,
new, PostgreSQL; tables are the median of 3 rounds. Each pass waited for CPUs 8-15 to average
below 35% busy; the machine ran at load 12-30 and wall times moved up to 2x between runs, so
user instructions (`perf stat -e instructions:u` over each run of a fresh engine, the binaries
alternating) are the gate's measure.

- Engine/PostgreSQL: wall 1.20x (13.60 s against 11.34 s), CPU 1.19x, peak 1,862-1,883 MB.
- q01, q17 and q18 are ahead of PostgreSQL; all 22 result hashes equal PostgreSQL's.
- Largest gaps by wall time: q02, q13, q14, q20, q12; by CPU: q13, q12, q17, q07, q22.

### Where the gaps went (LBR call stacks)

- **q12**: the executor's Merge Join over a full `orders_pkey` index scan (1.5M heap fetches
  in the workers, `Store::get_with`, `IndexChunk`) with the join itself on the executor's
  thread (17% of the samples), where PostgreSQL runs a Parallel Hash Join over two Parallel
  Seq Scans.
- **q14**: 78% of the samples on the executor's thread: the part table's 200,000 rows in a
  private hash table of hash_mem went to batch files, and every batch after the first was
  probed there (`next_batch`, `BatchReader`, `RowReader`); PostgreSQL's Parallel Hash fits in
  one batch of the participants' combined memory.
- **q13**: the merge of the workers' parts on the executor's thread (23%; `AggNode::run`).
- **q02, q20**: serial in both servers; index nested loops' point fetches (`Block::seek`,
  `SstReader::block_with`, `find_block`) and, in q02, the hash join's batches.
- **q04, q03, q07, q21, q09** (profiled too): almost all in the workers, 45-60% of their
  samples in point lookups (`Store::get_versions_with`, `point::newest`, `point::file_get`).

  difference both ways; nothing on those paths changed but two empty vectors set once per

  loaded rounds and these runs.

## Point reads, round 1 (2026-11-10, agent 202)

HANDOFF.md "Point reads, round 1" has what changed. Every server in a container on CPUs 32-35
(8 GiB, swap off, `--cpu-shares 2`, host network), the clients on 12-15 (`taskset`, nice 19):
roll dist builds, PostgreSQL 18.6 (the pinned image; shared_buffers 2 GB, maintenance_work_mem
256 MB, max_wal_size 8 GB, checkpoint_timeout 30 min, max_connections 300, fsync and
synchronous_commit on) and pgrust (`malisper/pgrust:v0.4-beta-amd64`, the digest run.sh pins;
the same settings plus its README's stack options and io_method=sync). 100,000 accounts
(`bench oltp-load`) or pgbench scale 1; 2 s warmup, 10 s measured; server CPU from the
container's cgroup; memory is the cgroup's peak. Point reads check every returned value.

pgrust's lead is real at 8 and 32 clients on every read workload: 1.75-1.9x roll's ops/s at
about half its server CPU per read. The gap is user CPU (roll 30-36 µs a read, pgrust 13-15,
PostgreSQL 19-22); kernel CPU is close (roll 14-15, pgrust 11-12, PostgreSQL 22-28: roll and
pgrust read and write once per Bind/Execute/Sync, about 1.3 context switches a read). At one
client roll is level with pgrust and 1.4x PostgreSQL (its connection polls; 0.97 cores busy).
Idle, a fresh server holds 8 MiB (roll), 80 (PostgreSQL), 206-215 (pgrust). The one-client
cells see 0.5-0.8 cores of foreign CPU for every server (wakeup work outside the cgroups), so
the 0.3 rule is not applied there. CPUs 14-15 of the clients are hyperthreads of the server's
32-33: the same for all three servers.

**Where a read goes** (prepared point select, 8 clients, roll at `16eccc605`: LBR call stacks
of cycles:u, 104k samples; user 30.1 µs of 45.0 µs server CPU per read, the kernel's 14.9 the
loopback reads and writes):

| part | µs / read (user) |
|---|---:|
| network read and write, framing, ReadyForQuery | ~1.0 (+ 14.9 kernel) |
| Bind: parameters, the portal, Describe's binding check | 2.0 (of which the implicit transaction's begin 0.9) |
| plan choice and reuse (`planned_as`) | 0.4 |
| executor open and close (nodes, `ExecCtx` made and dropped twice) | 4.3 |
| index lookup and row fetch (`next_raw_ref`), and the parallel check | 7.3 + 0.9 |
| row decode and checks (`emit`) | 1.4 |
| wire encode (`Feed::row`: the row's text, a format context with names) | 0.7 |
| statement begin twice, locks and privileges | 1.2 + 1.6 |
| commit of the read-only transaction (Txn drop 0.9, pg_stat counts 0.5) | 2.8 |
| pg_stat_activity per message | 0.7 |
| allocation (mimalloc, self) | 3.2 across the above |

Of the lookup, the index entry's merging scan is about 3.5 µs (sources made, each positioned)
and the row's point read 1.9 (one L1 file: block search 0.25, block cache 0.2, seek in the block
0.55). At 32 clients the same parts cost about the same per read (47.6 against 44.2 µs CPU):
no lock or block-cache shard shows; the connection threads (one per client) take the 4 cores.

**Kept changes** (alternating pairs of dist builds against **base2** = `16eccc605`, each run a
fresh server and data directory; user instructions and cycles per read from `perf stat
--per-thread` of the connections' threads over 7 s of each run):

1. *An index scan of one unique key ends at its visible row* (`unique_point`), **cand1**.
   Prepared point select, 8 clients:

| pair | base2 ops/s | cand1 ops/s | Δ ops/s | base2 µs/op | cand1 µs/op | Δ CPU | Δ instr | Δ cycles |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 1 | 87,077 | 90,261 | +3.7% | 41.2 | 39.5 | -4.2% | -4.6% | -6.2% |
| 2 | 85,698 | 89,779 | +4.8% | 41.7 | 39.6 | -5.0% | -3.9% | -6.7% |
| 3 | 85,438 | 89,382 | +4.6% | 40.9 | 39.7 | -3.1% | -3.2% | -4.4% |
| 4 | 87,800 | 91,362 | +4.1% | 40.9 | 39.3 | -4.0% | -2.9% | -4.9% |
| 5 | 87,208 | 91,428 | +4.8% | 41.2 | 39.4 | -4.2% | -3.0% | -3.8% |

   An earlier set of five counted the whole process's instructions: one pair's candidate run
   had the base's count (62.7k, a flush or compaction thread in its window) and lost 3.3% ops/s;
   the other four were -3.0% to -7.3%. The connections' threads are counted since.
   No regression, three pairs each: one client +0.4% ops/s, -1.9% CPU, -4.9% instructions;
   32 clients +4.1%, -3.4%, -4.7%; range reads +2.9%, -2.1%; short transactions +0.7%, -1.3%;
   pgbench -S prepared at 32 clients +1.6%, -3.1%.

2. *Agent 199's bundle* (executor trees restarted, the unique check's first entry, VALUES /
   INSERT events without copies, the snapshot xmin in one pass) on top, **cand2**: point
   select 8 clients against base2 -3.9% to -8.3% instructions in every pair (median -5.7%,

   Range reads regressed with it: -1.0% ops/s, +1.0% CPU, +2.0% cycles against base2.

3. *A query locks its relations in the context it then runs in* (`fused`), **cand3** against

   (cycles +3.2%, instructions -0.3%: item 2's restart of a range's scan).

4. *Only an index scan of one key restarts its executor tree*, **cand5** (what landed):
against base2, prepared point select at 8 clients:

| pair | base2 ops/s | cand5 ops/s | Δ ops/s | base2 µs/op | cand5 µs/op | Δ CPU | Δ instr | Δ cycles |

on CPUs 0-3 (client 4-7), user instructions of the server's cgroup per transaction. **base**
is round 10's build (its storage as pushed in `d54dcbe10`), **new** `d54dcbe10` with the fix.

| | L0 files after 50 / 200 / 400 restarts | tps | server µs CPU / txn | instructions / txn |
|---|---|---|---|---|

## Bulk writes, round 10 (2026-11-10, agent 201)

HANDOFF.md, "Bulk writes, round 10", has what changed. Method as rounds 8-9's, in
`~/.cache/a201/bench` (`bulk.sh`, `rounds.sh`, `med.py`): dist builds with line tables, each
server a fresh container on CPUs 0-3 (8 GiB, `--cpu-shares 2`, host network), psql in a third
container on CPUs 4-7; 1M rows (`bulk1m.tsv`, agent 182's), autovacuum off; per step the
server cgroup's CPU and bytes written, `perf stat -e instructions:u,cycles:u` of the server
cgroup (a privileged helper, `-a -C 0-3 -G`), the engine's VmRSS / VmHWM after each step;
rounds interleaved, medians of three. Rows checked equal on every run (count and sums of
every column of both tables). **base** is `a96bce378`; **new** the three changes on it.

The machine was busy: other tenants used 0.3-1.5 cores of CPUs 0-3 during most steps
(another agent's builds pinned to 0-7, and benchmarks on the hyperthread siblings 16-23), so
cgroup CPU and wall times move 10-30% from round to round; user instructions don't, and the
gates rest on them, with cycles and CPU alongside. A rerun on a quiet round was rarely
possible (a step was rerun once when it saw more than 0.3 foreign cores, then kept).

### The step table against PostgreSQL 18.6 (medians of three, interleaved)

| step | PostgreSQL wall / CPU s | base wall / CPU s | new wall / CPU s | CPU / PG base -> new | written MB PG / base / new |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| COPY in | 0.94 / 0.79 | 1.38 / 1.26 | 1.29 / 1.18 | 1.59 -> 1.49 | 189 / 32 / 32 |
| CREATE INDEX | 0.42 / 0.66 | 0.89 / 1.36 | 0.65 / 1.08 | 2.05 -> **1.62** | 48 / 17 / 18 |
| CREATE UNIQUE INDEX | 0.32 / 0.39 | 0.65 / 0.99 | 0.52 / 0.70 | 2.56 -> **1.80** | 71 / 9 / 9 |
| COPY out | 0.53 / 0.43 | 0.51 / 0.61 | 0.45 / 0.34 | 1.41 -> **0.79** | 0 / 59 / 0 |
| INSERT … SELECT | 0.94 / 0.72 | 0.98 / 0.86 | 0.92 / 0.80 | 1.20 -> 1.12 | 376 / 34 / 34 |
| UPDATE half | 4.29 / 4.30 | 2.23 / 2.12 | 2.02 / 1.86 | 0.49 -> 0.43 | 642 / 56 / 56 |
| DELETE a quarter | 0.34 / 0.22 | 1.08 / 0.97 | 0.99 / 0.87 | 4.33 -> 3.88 | 62 / 34 / 34 |
| VACUUM | 0.36 / 0.33 | 0.57 / 0.76 | 0.45 / 0.77 | 2.28 -> 2.32 | 14 / 48 / 48 |
| ANALYZE | 0.21 / 0.13 | 0.38 / 0.28 | 0.33 / 0.23 | 2.19 -> 1.79 | 0 / 0 / 0 |
| count(*) after | 0.14 / 0.13 | 0.19 / 0.18 | 0.18 / 0.18 | 1.38 -> 1.39 | 0 / 0 / 0 |
| TRUNCATE | 0.13 / 0.04 | 0.10 / 0.01 | 0.11 / 0.01 | 0.22 -> 0.22 | 0 / 0 / 0 |
| DROP TABLE | 0.14 / 0.05 | 0.10 / 0.09 | 0.11 / 0.02 | 1.68 -> 0.30 | 0 / 0 / 0 |
| **all steps** | 8.20 CPU s | 9.48 | 8.03 | **1.16 -> 0.98** | 1,485 / 334 / 230 |

(This table is the run of `a96bce378` with the first two changes and the third without its
memory packing; the final tree's CPU is the same within its spread, `c4` below. User
instructions over all steps: PostgreSQL 52.1 G, base 85.5-87.4 G, new 74.3-76.2 G, -13%.
Written: the medians of the per-round sums.) The VACUUM row carries the background
compaction of the DELETE's tombstones (48 MB written): with the server idle before it
(`settle`), VACUUM takes 0.25 s of CPU, PostgreSQL's 0.33-0.39. So does part of DELETE's,
ANALYZE's and count's CPU: the steps after UPDATE share one compaction between them.

Where the time went before: CREATE INDEX had a compaction rewrite the table loaded just
before it (all of L0's span, the catalog's keys at one end, the index's runs at the other),
so the next three statements read the table's new files cold (zstd and block decode in CREATE
UNIQUE INDEX; half of COPY out's CPU was the compaction itself); a sixth of CREATE INDEX was
memcmp over two empty `Vec`s, a microcode assist each (HANDOFF); DELETE's tombstone sort
compared keys that tied on 16 bytes.

### The changes, each against the one before (per-round %, instructions / cycles / CPU / wall)

**1. Compaction rewrites only the overlapped L1 files** (c1 against base, rounds of r1-r3):

| step | round 1 | round 2 | round 3 |
|---|---|---|---|
| CREATE UNIQUE INDEX | -35.6 / -36.1 / -18.2 / -6.2 | -30.8 / -50.6 / -41.5 / -21.5 | -31.7 / -34.0 / -27.2 / -20.0 |
| COPY out | -56.1 / -55.8 / -25.5 / +18.6 | -69.1 / -68.5 / -59.1 / -17.7 | -53.6 / -60.5 / -50.1 / -33.1 |
| INSERT … SELECT | -12.8 / -8.7 / -1.4 / +10.5 | -12.8 / -12.6 / -21.1 / -19.5 | -12.8 / -35.9 / -35.9 / -33.0 |

Instructions of every other step within 0.6% (CREATE INDEX -0.6/+2.8/-1.7; DELETE's -1.0/
+2.5/+2.7 move with the compaction that runs beside it: UPDATE through count together
+0.0/-0.2/+1.2). All steps: instructions -12%, bytes written 314/338/275 -> 276/231/276 MB.

**2. DELETE's tombstones sorted by group** (c2 against c1, r2): DELETE -3.1 / -2.6 / -1.0 /
+0.2, -4.4 / -15.2 / -20.9 / -19.1, -11.0 / -14.9 / -15.5 / -13.6; the other steps' instructions
within 0.1% but VACUUM's and COPY out's, which take the background compaction that moved
(UPDATE through count together -1.0 / -1.4 / +0.1).

**3. No empty-slice memcmp in the btree measure, the entry sort's 16-byte word** (c3 against
c1, r3 and the settled r4, which waits for the server to idle before DELETE, VACUUM, ANALYZE
and count):

| step | r3 round 1 | r3 round 2 | r3 round 3 | r4 (settled) round 1 | round 2 | round 3 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| CREATE INDEX | -6.3 / -27.9 / -26.0 / -31.6 | -10.7 / -31.4 / -28.2 / -31.7 | -5.2 / -28.5 / -26.1 / -29.9 | -4.6 / -28.9 / -31.9 / -32.7 | -4.5 / -27.8 / -27.0 / -31.4 | -1.8 / -26.1 / -27.8 / -28.0 |
| DELETE (with 2) | -6.8 / -12.9 / -12.8 / -12.2 | -4.2 / -11.2 / -9.2 / -8.7 | -6.5 / -11.6 / -14.5 / -12.5 | -6.9 / -10.7 / -3.5 / -0.3 | -5.4 / -13.7 / -16.0 / -14.1 | -4.0 / -7.7 / -14.1 / -11.3 |
| VACUUM | +6.6 / -0.5 / -8.3 / -14.6 | -0.1 / +4.9 / +10.2 / +13.5 | +2.7 / +2.6 / +2.7 / +16.4 | +0.0 / -0.2 / -5.7 / -3.4 | +0.0 / -6.5 / -5.1 / -10.4 | +0.0 / -1.1 / -1.2 / -5.6 |

Settled, VACUUM's instructions are equal and its CPU lower; in r3 it carried more of the
compaction that the faster DELETE left. ANALYZE's instructions moved by nothing or both ways
(-0.1, -0.1, +0.0, +0.0, +4.4, -4.1); its cycles moved with the other tenants' load (-6.9 ... +35%
in both directions, one round with 1.4 foreign cores). The final tree packs the measure's key
in 40 bytes (two words and a `u32`, not a `u128` and a `usize`) and the heap sizes as `u32`
(**c4**, against c3, r6): instructions within 1.1% on every step but those sharing the
compaction, CPU 7.83 -> 7.95 s over all steps (base 8.59 in the same rounds), the index
builds' peak RSS 254-273 -> 214-267 MB.

- Peak RSS (VmHWM, MB): base 266-306 over fifteen runs (median 274); final tree 277, 279, 278

## Short write transactions, round 1 (2026-11-10, agent 197)

locks 2.7; `with_search_path` 1.9; mimalloc 12 us over all (allocation's free-list pop, cache

own (five pairs each):

- Not rechecking the implied filter, against base: -6.1% to -7.3% instructions per pair

  `bitmap_or_paths`, `btcost`'s bound clauses moved, `match_quals` skipped for an index no

  (`~/.cache/a198/c3-planning-dropped.patch`).

The point read (`point_select`, five pairs: +3.1% median ops/s, -3.7% CPU per operation,
-2.3% to -4.3% instructions in every pair; one pair -9.2% ops/s with 0.27 cores of another
tenant on the candidate's run) and the prepared single-row UPDATE (`update_indexed`, three
pairs: +1.2% median ops/s, -0.3% CPU, -1.4% median instructions, the pairs -5.1% to +7.6%)
don't regress.

**The plan cache.** PostgreSQL 18.6 keeps choosing a custom plan for this statement: after
12 and 30 executions with different values, by `PREPARE`/`EXECUTE` and by the benchmark's
protocol (a named extended-protocol statement with an int4 parameter type),
`pg_prepared_statements` shows `generic_plans = 0`. Its generic plan costs 25.30 (500 rows
estimated) against a custom plan's 10.11 (91 rows) plus the planning overhead
`choose_custom_plan` adds. The engine shows the same counts and the same generic plan; its
custom plan estimates 100 rows (10.29), from its own ANALYZE sample (not compared further).
Planning each Bind is PostgreSQL's behaviour here too, not a difference to remove.

**The socket.** `strace -f` of each server for 3 s under the workload (from a container in
its pid namespace): both send each query's response in one `sendto` of 2,727 bytes and read
its 42 request bytes in one `recvfrom`. The engine makes 2.0 system calls per query;
PostgreSQL 8.5 (3 `lseek`, an `mmap`/`munmap` pair, a second `recvfrom` and 0.7
`epoll_pwait`). The time under `send` at ReadyForQuery is the kernel's loopback TCP work,
which PostgreSQL pays the same.

the switches wait for the client, 21.6% on futexes (11.3% condition variables: row-lock
waits on the hot rows and `wait_durable`; 10.3% parked on a mutex), 14.7% `sched_yield` in
parking_lot's mutex spin, 6.5% preemption. The mutexes' slow paths come from the lock
manager's state (`Txn::lock_row` 0.78% of CPU samples, `release_locks` 0.59%,
`LockManager::acquire` 0.34%, `Txn::xid` 0.29%) and `wait_durable` (0.36%): every row lock
goes through the lock table's one mutex, where PostgreSQL keeps tuple locks in the tuple.

The schema is `tools/bench/plans/apps.setup.sql`: pgbench scale 10, 200 tables with keys,
foreign keys and comments in two schemas (1,640 pg_class rows), and the job-queue tables at
10,000 rows. The statements are `plans/apps.sql`; TPC-H is SF1.

**Liquibase's column check** (Keycloak, Metabase), warm, psql's timing:

| | base | batch 1 | PostgreSQL | batch 1 / PostgreSQL |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| a call | 92-112 ms | 3.2-4.2 ms | 2.0-2.2 ms | 1.6-1.9 |

The plan is PostgreSQL's: a semi join (HashAggregate of pg_namespace ⋈ pg_class, then
pg_attribute's index), where base ran a SubPlan per pg_attribute row (10,325 loops).

**pg_class estimates at 2,000 tables** (8,415 pg_class rows, 2,068 of kind 'r'):

| `pg_class WHERE relkind = 'r'` | base | batch 1 | PostgreSQL |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| before ANALYZE | 42 | 1,379 (initdb's statistics) | 1,132 |
| after `ANALYZE pg_class` | 42 | 2,068 | 2,068 |

pgwatch's table_stats_approx over apps now has PostgreSQL's Hash Join, where base had a
Nested Loop over an estimate of 8.

ANALYZE costs, apps database (two runs each):

| | base | batch 1 | PostgreSQL | batch 1 / PostgreSQL |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `ANALYZE pg_class` | 1.0-1.3 ms (stored nothing) | 8.1-8.7 ms | 8.6 ms | 0.95-1.0 |
| database-wide `ANALYZE` | 0.98-2.1 s | 1.2-2.0 s | 0.31-0.65 s | 3.1-3.9 |

The catalogs add about 0.25 s warm.

**Planning per statement.** `tools/bench/plancpu.py` measures it two ways:
- EXPLAIN CPU: server CPU per `EXPLAIN <statement>` over pgbench `-M simple` / `-M
  extended`, about 0.6 s of server CPU a cell, two rounds. Geometric mean of the 16 apps
  statements, engine / PostgreSQL:

  | | base | batch 1 (initdb catalog statistics) | batch 1 (catalogs analyzed) |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|
  | simple protocol | 1.78 / 1.81 | 1.77 / 2.12 | 1.90 / 2.00 |
  | extended protocol | 2.49 / 2.53 | 1.79 / 1.71 | 1.78 / 1.72 |

  The extended protocol gains about 30% because EXPLAIN's query is no longer bound at Parse
  as well as at Execute. pgjdbc's getColumns went from 12.2 to 6.7-7.0 ms (PostgreSQL 2.0
  ms), getImportedKeys from 30-32 to 20-21 ms (PostgreSQL 7.7 ms), Kysely's getTables from
  3.4-3.8 to 1.8-2.2 ms (PostgreSQL 1.2 ms).

  Over the simple protocol, catalog statistics cost the large catalog joins 10-30% more
  planning than base, because base estimated those columns with defaults. getColumns with
  the catalogs analyzed takes 8.1-8.7 ms against base's 6.5-6.6 ms. Before the MCV change,
  with the catalogs analyzed, eqjoinsel's O(n²) MCV matching was half of it (11-12 ms).
- `--summary`: each server's own `EXPLAIN (SUMMARY)` Planning Time, the median of the last
  20 of 30 in a session. These are elapsed times, and on this machine their spread between
  rounds (up to 2x on one cell) is larger than the change between builds. The geometric
  mean over apps is 2.3-3.5 for every build. TPC-H, batch 1, simple protocol: 2.97,
  ranging from q03 at 1.07 to q15 at 10.8 (its view).
- CPU of EXPLAINing plancmp's 89 queries, each engine restarted then warmed once,
  interleaved:

  | | base | batch 1 | PostgreSQL |
  |---|---:|---:|---:|
  | run 1 / run 2, round 1 | 240 / 290 ms | 180 / 190 ms | 144-177 ms |
  | run 1 / run 2, round 2 | 210 / 210 ms | 220 / 200 ms | 155-171 ms |

  Peak memory was 1,619-1,663 MB for both builds. Without the warm run, a restarted engine's
  first run pays its heap-model restore (up to 7.7 s once).

**Plan shapes and results.** plancmp is 77 / 89 at the defaults and 81 / 89 serial,
unchanged. All 22 TPC-H result hashes equal PostgreSQL's.

**pgbench** (scale 10, 4 clients, 20 s, `-M prepared`, base / batch 1 on the same data,
interleaved twice; engine CPU per transaction from /proc):

| | round 1 | round 2 |
|---|---|---|
| select-only | 16,436 / 16,095 tps, 207 / 213 us | 14,916 / 15,327 tps, 238 / 231 us |
| tpcb-like | 3,164 / 3,006 tps, 1,068 / 1,107 us | 2,863 / 3,049 tps, 1,139 / 1,111 us |

Peak RSS was 253-339 MB, alike for both builds; the differences are within the rounds'
spread. Prepared pgbench statements are rarely planned, so this batch's planner changes
barely run on that path.

**Footprint gate** (fresh engines, host-run, `bench idle --mem-pid`), base / batch 1:
- idle CPU over 5 minutes after one query: 20 / 10 ms;
- 100 idle sessions: 42.2 + 100 × 325 kB / 42.7 + 100 × 329 kB of RSS;
- the fresh data directory: 64 / 64 kB before the first database is opened.
Initdb's catalog statistics are static data, and a catalog's are stored only by an ANALYZE
of it.

**Where planning goes now** (perf, release with frame pointers, pgjdbc and TPC-H joins).
The cost is spread thinly:
- allocation, 10-15%;
- `Expr` / `JP` / `IClause` / `ColInfo` clones and drops, 10-15%;
- join search bookkeeping (`enforced_of`, `needed_above_in`, `side_ec`, `var_of`);
- EXPLAIN's builder re-planning scans for estimates (`estimate_in`, `describe_need`,
  `partial_scans`).
No single function takes more than 5%. HANDOFF has the next steps.

## postgres_fdw, round 9 (2026-11-08, agent 181)

What changed: HANDOFF.md's postgres_fdw "Round 28 (agent 181)". ANALYZE of a foreign table
reads the remote table once (it read it twice) and samples remotely when it has more rows than
the sample; grouped relations and pushed joins are estimated as PostgreSQL estimates them; an
UPDATE's / DELETE's foreign join returns its relations' whole rows as PostgreSQL's does.

How it was measured: `tools/fdwtraffic/compare.sh` as in rounds 7 and 8 (both servers in
containers on cores 4-7 at the same CPU weight, host network, psql on cores 8-9, each server's
CPU and peak memory from its cgroup). Engines are `--profile dist` with line tables: base
`8500c0bb` (round 8), new = this round. Runs interleaved base, new, PostgreSQL, new, base on a
loaded machine (load 25-45); engine columns are the mean of 2 runs, PostgreSQL's 1 run. Phases
this round didn't touch moved by up to 30% between runs of one build (base's direct updates
79 / 107 ms CPU), so only ANALYZE's difference is resolved.

| phase (statements) | PG writes / stmt | new | PG ms | base ms | new ms | new / PG time | PG CPU ms | base CPU | new CPU | new / PG CPU |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| point lookups (500) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 1,269 | 869 | **871** | 0.69 | 417 | 359 | **361** | 0.87 |
| lookups in one txn (302) | 2.99 | **1.00** | 567 | 301 | **326** | 0.58 | 202 | 153 | **164** | 0.81 |
| full scans, 20k rows (10) | 205 | **202** | **1,452** | 1,605 | 1,528 | 1.05 | **597** | 664 | 640 | 1.07 |
| range scans, ~31 rows (200) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 563 | 438 | **411** | 0.73 | **173** | 226 | 210 | 1.21 |
| foreign joins (100) | 208 | **203** | **12,610** | 15,292 | 15,309 | 1.21 | **4,982** | 6,419 | 6,443 | 1.29 |
| remote aggregates (100) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 724 | 620 | **649** | 0.90 | **461** | 457 | 480 | 1.04 |
| local join foreign (5) | 205 | **202** | **504** | 732 | 730 | 1.45 | **180** | 296 | 271 | 1.51 |
| single-row inserts (300) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 743 | 417 | **396** | 0.53 | 246 | 157 | **152** | 0.62 |
| direct updates (100) | 3.00 | **2.00** | 213 | 194 | **152** | 0.71 | 76 | 89 | **72** | 0.94 |
| inherited updates, row trigger (20 x 50) | 56.95 | **51.95** | **504** | 1,040 | 940 | 1.86 | **179** | 509 | 482 | 2.69 |
| inherited updates, sent whole (20 x 50) | 3.00 | **2.00** | **71** | 67 | 94 | 1.33 | **34** | 49 | 68 | 1.99 |
| locked local join foreign (20) | 208 | **205** | **2,971** | 3,353 | 3,262 | 1.10 | **1,283** | 1,482 | 1,472 | 1.15 |
| locked local join of a foreign join (20) | 214 | **209** | **3,251** | 3,682 | 3,430 | 1.06 | **1,339** | 1,659 | 1,524 | 1.14 |
| ANALYZE ft, fs (1) | 237 | **230** | 212 | 80 | 216 | 1.02 | 103 | 82 | 109 | 1.06 |
| update from foreign join, 10 rows (50) | 3.00 | **2.00** | 223 | 146 | **192** | 0.86 | **90** | 93 | 127 | 1.41 |
| delete using foreign join, 3 rows (100) | 3.00 | **2.00** | **216** | 254 | 256 | 1.19 | **134** | 155 | 154 | 1.15 |

What moved: ANALYZE's bytes in fell from 1,729,177 to 884,303 (PostgreSQL's 884,398): the
table is read once, through a cursor (the fdw_traffic tables are smaller than the 30,000-row
sample, so neither server samples remotely here). Read `fetch_size` rows at a time as
PostgreSQL reads them, it took 230 round trips where base's two plain queries took 9, so its
time went from 80 to 216 ms (PostgreSQL 212). Peak memory: engine 63.6-65.1 MB (base
67.5-68.1), PostgreSQL 153.1 MB.

Where the CPU of the foreign-join and full-scan phases goes (perf on the new engine's process,
both ends of its loopback, 7k samples): allocation and frees 13% (mimalloc), `Value` drops 4.9%
and clones 2.1%; the remote side's cursor (`Cursor::visit` 2.4%, `put_data_row` 2.4%, the scan
and row decoding about 10%; round 7's routed item); the local hash join 2%; the wrapper's
client side about 10%: `make_row` 1.8%, its row's NULL fill (`Vec::extend_with`) 1.2%, the client's
DataRow parsing (`Conn::process` 1.0%, `Message::parse` 1.0%, `BytesMut` split / drop 1.8%),
a `String` per value (`from_utf8_lossy` 1.7% plus its allocation) then parsed into a `Value`
(`parse_value_ex` 0.9%).

Second batch (`8ae592fb`: ANALYZE fetching 1,000 rows a round trip, the client's dense rows and
one UTF-8 check per value), measured the same way, runs interleaved base, new, PostgreSQL, new,
base, new, base (3 runs of each engine, 1 of PostgreSQL; load 30-40):

| phase | PG writes / stmt | new | PG ms | base ms | new ms | new / PG time | PG CPU ms | base CPU | new CPU | new / PG CPU |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| ANALYZE ft, fs (1) | 237 | **29** | 171 | 86 | **81** | 0.47 | 125 | 99 | **61** | 0.49 |
| foreign joins (100) | 208 | **203** | **12,992** | 14,534 | 17,388 | 1.34 | **4,656** | 6,097 | 6,246 | 1.34 |
| full scans, 20k rows (10) | 205 | **202** | **1,460** | 1,581 | 1,560 | 1.07 | **590** | 639 | 642 | 1.09 |

ANALYZE now beats PostgreSQL in round trips, time and CPU with its bytes (867 kB against
884 kB): base's 9 round trips read the table twice, batch 1's 230 read it once. The client-side
changes aren't resolved: the foreign joins' CPU per run was base 5,823 / 6,208 / 6,260 ms, new
6,239 / 6,052 / 6,446 ms (one new run's elapsed time, 36.5 s against 27-29 s, was the machine's);
by the profile above they touch about 3% of that phase's CPU. Peak memory: engine 58-62 MB new,
68-124 MB base, PostgreSQL 149 MB.

```sh
SERVER_CPUS=4-7 CLIENT_CPUS=8-9 tools/fdwtraffic/compare.sh <roll-engine> <scratch> <prefix> <port> [pg] [engine]
```

## postgres_fdw, round 8 (2026-11-07, agent 172)

What changed: HANDOFF.md's postgres_fdw "Round 27 (agent 172)". `UPDATE ... FROM` and
`DELETE ... USING` over one server's foreign tables go as one remote statement (the target
joined remotely), foreign joins are costed as PostgreSQL costs them (the clauses' operator
cost was charged twice), and foreign scans' widths count their row marks' whole rows.

How it was measured: `tools/fdwtraffic/compare.sh` as in round 7 (both servers in containers
on cores 4-7 at the same CPU weight, host network, psql on cores 8-9; each server's CPU and
peak memory from its cgroup), with two new phases at the end: `ANALYZE ft, fs`, then 50
`UPDATE ft SET v = ft.v || 'u' FROM fs WHERE ft.id = fs.id AND fs.id BETWEEN a AND a + 9`
and 100 `DELETE FROM ft USING fs WHERE ft.id = fs.id + 99999 AND fs.id BETWEEN a AND a + 2`.
Engines are `--profile dist`: base `dcba895c` (run with the new phases at 17:24), new = this
round (20:40, interleaved with PostgreSQL's run). One run each on a loaded machine (load
12-20): phases this round didn't touch moved by up to 20% between runs (full scans 1,395 ->
1,677 ms with the same code), so only the two new phases' differences are resolved.

| phase (statements) | PG writes / stmt | new | base | PG ms | base ms | new ms | new / PG time | PG CPU ms | base CPU | new CPU | new / PG CPU |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| point lookups (500) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 2.00 | 1,224 | 867 | **790** | 0.65 | 374 | 383 | **334** | 0.89 |
| lookups in one txn (302) | 2.99 | **1.00** | 1.00 | 545 | 344 | **344** | 0.63 | 190 | 150 | 183 | 0.97 |
| full scans, 20k rows (10) | 205 | **202** | 202 | **1,516** | 1,413 | 1,677 | 1.11 | **637** | 637 | 766 | 1.20 |
| range scans, ~31 rows (200) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 2.00 | 556 | 382 | **359** | 0.65 | 183 | 182 | **137** | 0.75 |
| foreign joins (100) | 208 | **203** | 203 | **12,288** | 15,032 | 15,172 | 1.23 | **4,630** | 6,138 | 6,558 | 1.42 |
| remote aggregates (100) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 2.00 | 728 | 740 | **537** | 0.74 | 492 | 548 | **411** | 0.84 |
| local join foreign (5) | 205 | **202** | 202 | **618** | 696 | 718 | 1.16 | **228** | 276 | 285 | 1.25 |
| single-row inserts (300) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 2.00 | 794 | 542 | **296** | 0.37 | 245 | 179 | **108** | 0.44 |
| direct updates (100) | 3.00 | **2.00** | 2.00 | 201 | 183 | **157** | 0.78 | 80 | 83 | **72** | 0.90 |
| inherited updates, row trigger (20 x 50) | 56.95 | **51.95** | 51.95 | **522** | 1,004 | 976 | 1.87 | **165** | 467 | 507 | 3.08 |
| inherited updates, sent whole (20 x 50) | 3.00 | **2.00** | 2.00 | **58** | 117 | 104 | 1.79 | **33** | 76 | 74 | 2.28 |
| locked local join foreign (20) | 208 | **205** | 205 | **3,091** | 3,257 | 3,552 | 1.15 | **1,321** | 1,383 | 1,636 | 1.24 |
| locked local join of a foreign join (20) | 214 | **209** | 209 | **2,897** | 3,533 | 3,680 | 1.27 | **1,255** | 1,594 | 1,785 | 1.42 |
| ANALYZE ft, fs (1) | 237 | **9** | 9 | 148 | 71 | **91** | 0.62 | **76** | 79 | 102 | 1.35 |
| update from foreign join, 10 rows (50) | 3.00 | **2.00** | 216 | **156** | 13,513 | 180 | 1.15 | **68** | 7,705 | 118 | 1.72 |
| delete using foreign join, 3 rows (100) | 3.00 | **2.00** | 207 | **248** | 26,418 | 306 | 1.23 | **113** | 15,304 | 188 | 1.67 |

What moved: the base engine ran `UPDATE ... FROM` / `DELETE ... USING` as Hash Joins of both
whole remote tables and one remote UPDATE / DELETE per row (216 and 207 round trips, 53.8 and
106.9 MB in per phase); now each is one remote statement as PostgreSQL's (2 writes against
its 3; 3.1 kB and 6.1 kB in against 4.2 and 8.2 kB): 75x and 86x less time, 65x and 81x less
CPU. Bytes in are the same or fewer than PostgreSQL's in every phase but ANALYZE (1.73 MB
against 0.88 MB: the engine fetches every row in 9 round trips, PostgreSQL samples remotely
in 237). Peak memory: engine 74.8 MB, PostgreSQL 149.6 MB.

Still behind:

- The two join-DML phases (1.15-1.23x time, 1.7x CPU): the remote server runs the engine's
  own `UPDATE base r1 SET ... FROM side r2 WHERE ...` (PostgreSQL's plan, a nested loop of
  index scans), 1.3 ms per statement against PostgreSQL's 0.4 ms measured directly on both;
  that is the executor's per-row UPDATE (routed).
- Inherited updates (1.8-1.9x time, 2.3-3.1x CPU): a flat profile (perf, dist build without
  line tables) of the row-trigger phase on the engine spreads over the loopback's kernel
  networking (15%), allocation and drops (15%), heap scans of the remote member (`exec::scan`
  6.5%: the member scan reads all 2,000 rows each statement, as PostgreSQL's does), binding
  (4.7%) and EXPLAIN-shared code paths (3.3%); no wrapper function above 1.2%.
- Joins and full scans (1.1-1.4x): as round 7 (the remote cursor keeping its rows, routed).

```sh
SERVER_CPUS=4-7 CLIENT_CPUS=8-9 tools/fdwtraffic/compare.sh <roll-engine> <scratch> <prefix> <port> [pg] [engine]
```

## postgres_fdw, round 7 (2026-11-03, agent 157)

What changed: HANDOFF.md's postgres_fdw "Round 26 (agent 157)". PostgreSQL's estimates for
never-analyzed foreign tables and one-group aggregations sent whole (so the engine now picks
PostgreSQL's plans: it no longer pushes the joins PostgreSQL doesn't push), a cursor's scan
decoding only the columns read, numeric text without allocations, and common values written
straight into DataRow messages.

How it was measured: `tools/fdwtraffic/compare.sh` (new), the fdw_traffic workload with both
servers in containers on cores 4-7 at the same CPU weight, host network, psql on cores 8-9.
Each server's CPU (`cpu.stat` usage_usec: both ends of its loopback) and peak memory
(`memory.peak`) come from its container's cgroup. Engines are `--profile dist` builds: base
`847fbdba`, new = this round. The runs were interleaved (base, new, PostgreSQL, new, base) on a
loaded machine (load 13-15); the engine columns are the mean of 2 runs, PostgreSQL's is 1 run.

| phase (statements) | PG writes / stmt | engine | PG ms | base ms | new ms | new / PG time | PG CPU ms | base CPU | new CPU | new / PG CPU |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| point lookups (500) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 1,213 | 851 | **765** | 0.63 | 402 | 381 | **341** | 0.85 |
| lookups in one txn (302) | 2.99 | **1.00** | 482 | 308 | **315** | 0.65 | **161** | 162 | 169 | 1.05 |
| full scans, 20k rows (10) | 205 | **202** | 1,508 | 1,647 | **1,453** | 0.96 | **574** | 769 | 633 | 1.10 |
| range scans, ~31 rows (200) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 732 | 450 | **452** | 0.62 | **215** | 245 | 232 | 1.08 |
| foreign joins (100) | 208 | 203 | **11,144** | 1,080 * | 13,966 | 1.25 | **4,387** | 928 * | 6,084 | 1.39 |
| remote aggregates (100) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 778 | 669 | **613** | 0.79 | 502 | 532 | **483** | 0.96 |
| local join foreign (5) | 205 | **202** | **440** | 667 | 637 | 1.45 | **162** | 293 | 264 | 1.63 |
| single-row inserts (300) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 690 | 351 | **398** | 0.58 | 208 | 133 | **150** | 0.72 |
| direct updates (100) | 3.00 | **2.00** | 217 | 157 | **178** | 0.82 | **81** | 72 | 82 | 1.02 |
| inherited updates, row trigger (20 x 50) | 56.95 | **51.95** | **408** | 912 | 987 | 2.42 | **141** | 459 | 487 | 3.46 |
| inherited updates, sent whole (20 x 50) | 3.00 | **2.00** | **33** | 81 | 97 | 2.95 | **16** | 59 | 71 | 4.37 |
| locked local join foreign (20) | 208 | **205** | **2,568** | 3,341 | 3,080 | 1.20 | **1,086** | 1,566 | 1,431 | 1.32 |
| locked local join of a foreign join (20) | 214 | 209 | **2,488** | 516 * | 3,458 | 1.39 | **1,034** | 431 * | 1,487 | 1.44 |

\* The base engine pushed these joins down where PostgreSQL doesn't (its never-analyzed
foreign table estimate, round 6's note), so those cells measured another plan. With
PostgreSQL's estimates the new engine runs PostgreSQL's plans there (Hash Joins of the
whole remote tables: 61 MB and 17 MB fetched a phase on both).

Bytes in are the same or fewer on the engine in every phase (point lookups 94 against 120 kB,
range scans 272 against 282 kB, aggregates 16 against 22 kB). Peak memory over the whole run:
engine 66-70 MB (new), 68-123 MB (base), PostgreSQL 147 MB.

What moved: the full scans' CPU fell 18% against base (769 -> 633 ms; time 1,647 -> 1,453 ms,
now ahead of PostgreSQL's 1,508), local join foreign 10% (293 -> 264), aggregates 9%
(532 -> 483 ms, ahead of PostgreSQL in time and CPU), point lookups 10%. The profile of the
full scans before: numeric formatting and parsing 11% of the server's CPU (four allocations
per value out, a digit String in), the remote cursor decoding every column of rows it then
projected (`full_row`, 13%), vectors grown per row.

Still behind, with where the time goes (perf, dist build, DWARF stacks):

- Remote scans of whole tables (local join foreign 1.45x, foreign joins 1.25x, locked local
  join 1.20x): the remote server's cursor keeps every row it returns (backward FETCH), about
  15% of the CPU of a 20k-row scan (`Cursor::visit` re-evaluating the target list per row
  2-4%, `push_row`, and the rows' drop at commit 6%); `exec::scan` decodes each row into a
  vector then grows it for the key (`scan_row_rest`, 5%). Both are outside postgres_fdw.
- Inherited updates (2.4x, 3.0x): per-statement planning of the inheritance tree and the
  per-row remote UPDATEs' execution on the remote server (the same engine), not the wrapper's
  traffic (fewer round trips and bytes than PostgreSQL).

pgbench (scale 1, 8 clients, `-M prepared`, 20 s, the engine in a container on cores 4-7,
four interleaved rounds, both orders, load 13-21): select-only server CPU per transaction base
46 / 48 / 58 / 58 us, new 44 / 52 / 53 / 60 us (medians 53 and 52.5); TPC-B-like base
419 / 443 / 443 / 471 us, new 445 / 464 / 493 / 568 us (the 568 at load 21; medians 443 and
478). Single runs moved 25% at this load, so neither difference is resolved: the only changed
code these statements run is int4 text output (written into the message, no String) and the
planner's foreign-table checks (a field test for plain tables). TPC-B-like's higher median
needs a quieter rerun before it is trusted either way. Peak memory 128-137 MB new, 130-157 MB
base.

```sh
tools/heavy.sh cargo build --profile dist -p pg-engine --bin roll-engine   # copy it aside
SERVER_CPUS=4-7 CLIENT_CPUS=8-9 tools/fdwtraffic/compare.sh <roll-engine> <scratch> <prefix> <port> [pg] [engine]
```

## postgres_fdw, round 6 (2026-11-03, agent 153)

What changed: HANDOFF.md's postgres_fdw "Round 25 (agent 153)". A level locking local relations
joins its foreign ones remotely (with the local join as the EvalPlanQual outer plan); a declared
cursor's scan takes its connection at DECLARE.

The workload is `tests/fdw_traffic.rs`'s phases plus one new phase ("locked local join of a
foreign join": `SELECT loc.id, ft.v, fs.w FROM loc, ft, fs WHERE ft.id = fs.id AND ft.id =
loc.id AND fs.id < N FOR UPDATE OF loc`, 20 statements). It runs as one psql session against
a fresh server whose postgres_fdw connects to the same server through a counting TCP proxy
(`tools/fdwtraffic/`: `run-engine.sh`, the workload `pgtraffic.py`, the proxy `fdwproxy.py`):
`writes` are the wrapper's message batches (round trips), `out` / `in` the bytes each way. The
engine is a `--profile dist` build (e73340f4) with WAL fsync on, as PostgreSQL 18.6 (the pinned
image, default settings, host network) is. Both run on the same 16-core host; the engine runs
under `nice -n 19`. One run each, back to back, on a loaded machine (load 9-15).

| phase (statements) | PG writes / stmt | engine | PG in | engine in | PG ms | engine ms | engine / PG time |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| point lookups (500) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 120 kB | **94 kB** | 989 | **819** | 0.83 |
| lookups in one txn (302) | 2.99 | **1.00** | 40 kB | **31 kB** | 517 | **256** | 0.49 |
| full scans, 20k rows (10) | 205 | **202** | 8.28 MB | 8.28 MB | **1,454** | 1,777 | 1.22 |
| range scans, ~31 rows (200) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 282 kB | **272 kB** | 466 | **442** | 0.95 |
| foreign joins (100) | 208 | **2.09** | 61.3 MB | **0.16 MB** | 11,281 | **1,060** | 0.09 |
| remote aggregates (100) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 22 kB | **16 kB** | **592** | 702 | 1.19 |
| local join foreign (5) | 205 | **202** | 1.59 MB | 1.59 MB | **634** | 890 | 1.40 |
| single-row inserts (300) | 5.00 | **2.00** | 34 kB | **25 kB** | 677 | **462** | 0.68 |
| direct updates (100) | 3.00 | **2.00** | 8 kB | **6 kB** | 178 | **154** | 0.86 |
| inherited updates, row trigger (20 x 50 rows) | 56.95 | **51.95** | 74 kB | 78 kB | **458** | 1,043 | 2.28 |
| inherited updates, sent whole (20 x 50 rows) | 3.00 | **2.00** | 1.7 kB | **1.2 kB** | **45** | 105 | 2.35 |
| locked local join foreign (20) | 208 | **205** | 16.7 MB | 16.7 MB | **2,837** | 3,171 | 1.12 |
| locked local join of a foreign join (20) | 214 | **4.95** | 16.9 MB | **0.51 MB** | 3,108 | **427** | 0.14 |

Whole run: engine 12.0 s wall, 6.6 s CPU for both sides of every connection (one process),
peak RSS 95 MB. PostgreSQL's two backends per phase run in separate processes (its CPU isn't
summed here). The engine sends fewer round trips and bytes in every phase. Two of its time
leads come from plans PostgreSQL doesn't choose: its foreign joins and the locked join of
foreign tables aren't pushed down on PostgreSQL here, because the tables were never analyzed
and PostgreSQL estimates a never-analyzed foreign table by its reltarget's width, the engine by
all of the table's columns (`costs::rel_size`; HANDOFF's round 25 note). That gap is a plan
difference to close, not a lead to keep. Where the engine is slower: whole-table remote scans
(1.22x, 1.40x joined locally: the remote side's scan and the text conversion of 20,000 rows a
statement), remote aggregates (1.19x), and the inherited updates (2.3x: per-statement planning
of an inheritance tree with a foreign member, both with and without the trigger).

Before and after this round (debug engines, same harness, in-process counters agreeing with
the proxy's): every phase's writes and bytes are unchanged but the new one, 208.95 -> 4.95 writes
per statement, 16.9 -> 0.51 MB in, 22.4 -> 1.5 s; peak RSS 147.6 -> 140.9 MB for the whole run.
The non-direct foreign UPDATE phase (rows fetched by remote ctid, agent 135's Tid Scan,
08fef0f0) takes 4.3-5.2 s in debug where round 23 measured 24 s: no extra round trips (51.95 per
statement, one per row's UPDATE, as before).

## Build profile: dist (2026-11-03)

`--profile dist` (root `Cargo.toml`: `release` with `lto = "fat"` and `codegen-units = 1`)
against `release`, same source (`8115f5af`), one build after the other with 2 cargo jobs:
`release` 10 min, `dist` 18 min; `roll-engine` 86 MB, 68 MB. pgbench scale 10, `-M prepared`,
8 clients (`-j 4`), 15 s after a 5 s warm-up, `--cache-mb 2048`, the engine on cores 24-27
at nice 19, pgbench in the pinned PostgreSQL image on cores 30-33. Three rounds of release,
dist, dist, release, a fresh engine process per run on the same data. CPU per transaction is
the engine's utime + stime over the timed run (`/proc`; `perf` isn't installed on this
machine). The machine ran at load 25-27 from other agents' builds, so single runs move a lot:
medians of six runs.

| workload | release CPU / txn | dist CPU / txn | change | mean tps release / dist |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| select-only | 0.184 ms | 0.150 ms | -19% | 18.6k / 25.7k |
| tpcb-like | 0.944 ms | 0.736 ms | -22% | 2.6k / 3.6k |
| range (`aid BETWEEN :id AND :id + 99 ORDER BY aid`) | 0.872 ms | 0.705 ms | -19% | 4.0k / 4.9k |

Peak RSS (VmHWM) 345-409 MB dist, 364-422 MB release. Both builds include d22006b9's
per-execution Gather search, which makes select-only's absolute numbers about 3.5x those
before it (agent 145 is fixing it); the ratio between the builds is what this measures.
Profile-guided optimisation on top is not tried yet (it needs `llvm-tools` and a training
run).

## Checked OLTP histogram campaign (2026-11-02)

Borrowing cached inequality histograms improves the 200k-account, eight-client
100-row range workload by 4.9% against `7f35a185`. The measured source is `5b32046d`.
It keeps the histogram's shared values and stores refreshed endpoints locally,
preserving custom-plan choice, estimates, callback order and interpolation.

Five alternating three-second samples per endpoint follow an unreported warmup pair.
The same frozen release client checks every returned ID and value inside the timer.
Both complete fixture digests match independent Python generation before and after
measurement. The workload is `SELECT id, balance FROM accounts WHERE id BETWEEN $1
AND $1 + 99 ORDER BY id`, with a named prepared statement and binary INT4 parameters.

| Eight-client range reads | Baseline | Histogram change |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Fresh fixture, operations/s | 8,419 (8,312–8,499) | 8,829 (8,643–8,836) |
| Server CPU, microseconds/operation | 469 (465–476) | 446 (446–455) |
| Process RSS, MB | 123.5 (123.5–124.6) | 105.8 (105.8–107.0) |
| Partially visible fixture, operations/s | 7,679 (7,671–7,756) | 8,066 (7,979–8,100) |
| Server CPU, microseconds/operation | 517 (513–518) | 492 (489–498) |
| Process RSS, MB | 117.0 (116.9–118.2) | 149.0 (115.3–165.6) |

The fresh fixture has 1,471/1,471 all-visible pages; the partially visible fixture
has 1,470/1,471. The initial processes had different warmup histories. RSS changes
direction across the controls, so those samples establish no memory advantage.
After restarting both processes with matched warmups, baseline RSS is 101.8 MB
(101.1–102.7) and changed RSS is 103.1 MB (102.0–103.4). The earlier large gap
does not reproduce. Throughput remains higher: 7,748 (7,731–7,782) to 7,957
(7,910–8,063) operations/s; CPU falls from 513 to 499 microseconds per operation.

One-client range throughput rises from 1,924 (1,907–1,938) to 2,022
(1,983–2,056) operations/s. A short point-read comparison had a small negative
signal. Five longer ten-second pairs reverse its direction: 34,087
(33,778–34,193) to 34,366 (34,029–34,461) operations/s. No point-read speedup
is claimed. Secondary lookup ranges overlap.

At 8,829 operations/s and 446 microseconds of server CPU per operation, the engine
uses about 3.94 CPU seconds per second across its four assigned CPUs. Client CPU
is about 98 microseconds per operation, below one CPU in aggregate. This workload
is limited by server CPU. A separate diagnostic profile has 729 samples and zero
lost samples; allocation, expression copying and heap-fetch bookkeeping remain
active. It does not assign all clone samples to one request phase.

PostgreSQL still leads this OLTP workload. A final comparison using the same checked
client gives the changed engine 8,786 (8,766–8,969) range operations/s against
PostgreSQL's 37,765 (37,428–38,162). Server CPU is 447 versus 92.5 microseconds
per operation. These are another five alternating pairs. PostgreSQL completes this workload
about 4.3 times faster.

Both endpoints use four server CPUs, separate client CPUs, host networking, durable
settings, C locale, 4 MB work memory, hash multiplier 2 and 256 MB maintenance memory.
The engine cache is configured to 2,048 MB; PostgreSQL shared buffers are 2,048 MB.
PostgreSQL has an 8 GB cgroup limit. Process RSS and PostgreSQL cgroup anon+shmem
measure different memory components. Runtime records preserve the pinned image,
executable hashes, CPU affinities and resource limits.

The client validates all point fields, every ordered range pair, and exact secondary
count/sum results, including NULL and type checks. Six Rust tests, parser controls,
and sixteen live wrong-value controls cover the validation path. Equal secondary
aggregates cannot identify their contributing rows. The initial baseline comparison
used client `55fe2f7b`; subsequent comparisons used `89e863fc`, which also rejects
malformed NUMERIC payloads whose fractional digits would otherwise be rounded away.
Both binary hashes are retained. The broad suite now checks reads before writes and
reloads each write case/client fixture.

Portal-parameter and catalog-handle borrow experiments were reverted. Their median
gains are smaller than the observed run spreads under the conservative acceptance
rule. Eight-client point reads did show a consistent 0.68% signal in that experiment;
rejecting it does not prove zero benefit. No stored-TID pruning shortcut or broader
range-plan cache was added.

The portable samples and comparison records are in `tools/bench/results/2026-11-02/`.
Raw commands, complete logs, frozen binaries, profiles and saved fixtures remain in
`~/.cache/pg-engine-perf-20261101/oltp-20261102/`.


### Verification and limits

The matched release build, complete crate suite (430 passed, 18 ignored, zero failed),
all-target Clippy, and `pnpm test:pg --unit` pass. Clippy retains eleven library
warnings, three additional library-test warnings, and one differential-test warning.
It also reports one new benchmark style warning, `chunks_exact_to_as_chunks`.
The full release regression matches 51,148/51,150 units across 230 files, with no
new differences or panics. Result rows and both remaining hunks are identical to
release baseline `7f35a185`. The generated historical debug report is unchanged.
Docker recovery e2e remains waived under the existing instruction, retaining its
baseline of twelve passes and four WAL HTTP timeouts.

The exported records include a read-only `verify.py`, raw measurement records,
fixture digests, runtime observations, gate logs, and both regression reports.
Historical build provenance lacks a complete compiler environment and clean-tree
attestation. The initial PostgreSQL comparison lacks an observed runtime CPU-budget
snapshot and exact image identity. The plan-choice and wrong-value probe records
lack complete execution identities. Those gaps remain historical limits; later
runtime snapshots do not fill them retroactively. All campaign services are stopped,
and their saved data is retained.

### Rejected btree selectivity experiment

Candidate `8b33053b` reuses a complete integer btree boundary selectivity within
one costing call. The bitwise library test and release build pass. Settings,
EXPLAIN output, prepared-plan counts, and checked query results match baseline.
The candidate was reverted at `2d6375d1` under the same conservative acceptance rule.

The short range8 comparison rises from 8,829 to 8,967 operations/s. Five longer
ten-second pairs give 8,863 (8,777–8,884) for baseline and 8,992 (8,861–9,016)
for the candidate, a 1.5% median increase. Server CPU falls from 445.1 to 438.9
microseconds per operation. The 129 operations/s gap remains below the candidate's
155 operations/s full spread. This is a small positive signal that did not clear
the acceptance threshold, rather than proof of zero benefit.

The one-client point-read median drops 3.4% in the short run and 0.8% in the longer
control. Both gaps remain within the observed spreads, and longer-run CPU is slightly
lower for the candidate. No stable point-read regression is established.
All 84 invocations, including warmups, complete with zero errors and
`checked_ops == ops`; fixtures remain unchanged. The additional real-SQL activation
test remains uncompiled in its isolated worktree, so this campaign makes no claim
that the real SQL path's reuse was independently observed. Full candidate gates
were not run after the performance gate failed. The restored production source is
identical to the previously verified `90b5525f`.

Portable records are in `tools/bench/results/2026-11-02-btree-selectivity/`.
All experiment services are stopped and fixture data is retained.

### Planning-mode diagnostic

A separate diagnostic keeps the verified histogram engine and checked client fixed.
Five alternating ten-second pairs give eight-client range throughput of 8,810
(8,787–8,883) operations/s in auto mode and 12,765 (12,705–12,888) with forced
generic plans. Server CPU falls from 448.28 to 303.14 microseconds per operation.
Both modes use approximately four server cores; client use is about 0.84 and 1.19
cores. Auto and forced-custom range controls overlap. Point auto/generic controls
also overlap, and no memory advantage is established.

A separate one-client instrumented phase pass records custom planning elapsed time
of 160.67 microseconds per call within 375.19 microseconds of inclusive execution.
Generic records 0.67 within 234.13 microseconds. Execution includes planning, so the
two timers must not be added. These are elapsed times with instrumentation overhead,
not CPU shares. Planning-span counts reconcile with every checked operation,
including warmup; they do not count actual rebuilds. Parse/Describe is outside the
spans, and no result isolates `plan_cost_estimate`.

Plans preserve scan structure, direction, and projection, but Param/Const bounds
and folding differ. The mode delta therefore includes possible executor differences.
This is a diagnostic, with no engine change, PostgreSQL comparison, or production
mode recommendation. All 84 completed invocations have zero errors and 4,802,187
checked operations. The initial run completed one block before a safe reused-port
bind failure; a continuation completed the other two blocks on distinct ports.
All owned processes and helpers are stopped, with data retained.
Portable records and the read-only audit are in
[the planning diagnostic](tools/bench/results/2026-11-02-planning/README.md).

### CPU ancestry after correcting profile decoding

Three twenty-second auto/eight-client range profiles recover inclusive
`plan_cost`/`plan_cost_estimate` ancestry in 19.19–20.55% of server CPU-clock sample
weight, and `bound_custom` ancestry in 17.24–17.70%. The nine captures across auto,
forced-custom and forced-generic modes contain 16,157 samples with zero losses.
All twelve client invocations complete 1,360,591 checked operations without errors.

A pilot exposed a one-page PIE relocation error in perf 6.15's libdw decoding.
A documented synthetic mapping replay recovers raw caller IPs; attribution uses
the original ELF and original mappings, preserving the captured sample bytes.
Independent CFI/call-site and full-record audits support this correction.

The cost bucket includes wrapper and descendant work. Finite stacks can omit
ancestors, and recovered weights are not statistical population lower bounds.
These are diagnostic observations, with no speedup or removable-cost prediction.
The sampled windows differ from whole-invocation operation accounting. All owned
engines are stopped and the profiler is restored to its stopped 512 MiB caps.
Details, decoded stacks, scripts, limits, and a portable audit are in
[the CPU ancestry diagnostic](tools/bench/results/2026-11-02-planning-cpu/README.md).

### Rejected total-only cost extraction experiment

Candidate `1713aa40` shared needed-column masks and skipped discarded metadata
extraction after the existing cost builder. Its SQL-driven bitwise cost test and

Five alternating ten-second pairs give range8 throughput of 8,871 (8,859–8,888)
operations/s for baseline and 8,820 (8,741–8,842) for the candidate, a small negative
signal of 0.575%. Server CPU is 445.22 versus 447.22 microseconds per operation.
The median gap of 51 operations/s is below the candidate's full spread of 101,
while throughput ranges do not overlap. Point1 ranges overlap. No memory advantage
or broader regression is established. The candidate selected only the engine in
Cargo; the baseline also selected benchmark examples. Restoration exposed different
dependency fingerprints, so this comparison cannot isolate the source changes.

The candidate fails the required gain threshold and was reverted at `e8425dea`
and `7cdbee00`. All 24 invocations, including warmup, check their 1,879,306 operations
with zero errors; fixture digests match. Full candidate gates and regression were
not run after performance rejection. Restored production source matches the verified
histogram baseline, and the matched-target rebuild restores its exact binary hash.
All experiment services are stopped and saved data is retained.
Records and portable audit are in
[the total-only experiment](tools/bench/results/2026-11-02-estimate-total/README.md).

## Checked scan and index measurements (2026-11-01)

Source `7f35a185` makes the 2M-row top-N query 6.8 times faster than engine baseline
`0b950a11`, with about half the server CPU. Against tuned PostgreSQL, latency and CPU
ranges overlap. PostgreSQL still leads the checked OLTP workloads.

Each comparison has one warmup pair and five recorded pairs, with endpoint order reversed
each round. The checked client receives and hashes every returned value inside the query
timer. Every top-N sample returns the same 100 rows and hash on both endpoints.

### Top-N latency, CPU, and memory

The query is `SELECT id, x FROM fact ORDER BY x DESC, id LIMIT 100`. Times below are
medians, with the five-sample range in parentheses. CPU is total server CPU per query.
Both servers have four assigned CPUs, 28 through 31. Clients use CPUs 32 through 35.

| Rows | Comparison | Before, seconds | After, seconds | Server CPU before / after, seconds |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 200,000 | Original engine to `7f35a185` | 0.0747 (0.0644–0.0806) | 0.0320 (0.0288–0.0322) | 0.07 / 0.12 |
| 2,000,000 | Original engine to `7f35a185` | 0.5495 (0.5434–0.5770) | 0.0807 (0.0796–0.0911) | 0.55 / 0.28 |
| 200,000 | Tuned PostgreSQL to `7f35a185` | 0.0351 (0.0331–0.0371) | 0.0331 (0.0287–0.0339) | 0.06 / 0.12 |
| 2,000,000 | Tuned PostgreSQL to `7f35a185` | 0.0811 (0.0738–0.0907) | 0.0807 (0.0795–0.0825) | 0.30 / 0.28 |

PostgreSQL uses `max_parallel_workers_per_gather=3`. Its large-fixture plan launches
three workers, with four participants scanning all 2M rows. Its small-fixture plan uses
one worker. The engine uses its private scan pool within the same four assigned CPUs.
The large-fixture CPU ranges are 0.28–0.34 seconds for PostgreSQL and 0.28–0.29 seconds
for the engine. These measurements establish parity on this query, with no demonstrated
advantage over tuned PostgreSQL. An earlier comparison against PostgreSQL's two-worker
default is superseded by this tuned comparison.

The 200k-row engine latency improvement costs additional CPU. The original-to-final
comparison also raises median process RSS from 98.8 to 104.0 MB at 200k rows and from
449.4 to 467.6 MB at 2M rows. These are whole-process sampled peaks, including retained
caches and allocator state. The later PostgreSQL comparison has different process
history: engine RSS is 718.0 MB at 200k and 1,106.9 MB at 2M, while PostgreSQL's cgroup
`anon + shmem` is 349.0 and 353.4 MB. Those memory measures exclude filesystem cache
differently. There is no memory-efficiency claim against PostgreSQL.

Profiles identified row decoding, allocation, and executor projection as the original
top-N costs. Workers now retain all first-key ties but discard strictly losing numeric
keys before materializing complete rows. The executor still sorts all surviving keys.
The path requires direct numeric projections, respects `work_mem`, and falls back when
lazy decoding keeps 512 consecutive rows. Inline keys avoid general lazy-column buffers.
Filtered and external-value rows keep the existing decoding path.

Dedicated 2M-row controls compare `7f35a185` with the preceding implementation at
`9ed57ecd`. Tied first keys measure 0.3614 to 0.3627 seconds, with CPU 1.06 to 1.07
seconds. Continuously improving input measures 0.7300 to 0.7122 seconds, with CPU 2.01
to 2.03 seconds. Timing and CPU ranges overlap in both controls. Median RSS changes
from 386.6 to 385.3 MB and from 687.4 to 684.5 MB, respectively. Complete fixture
checksums agree with independent Python generation over every row.

### Indexed reads

On 200k accounts with eight clients, the clean-table 100-row range scan rises from
7,496 operations per second (7,455–7,650) to 8,347 (8,292–8,356). Server CPU falls from
531 microseconds per operation (520–534) to 476 (474–478). All operations have checked
cardinalities, report zero errors, and leave the complete fixture checksum unchanged.
These are five alternating two-second samples after a warmup pair. Server CPU includes
connection and statement setup, divided by each invocation's exact successful count.
At 8,347 operations per second, 476 microseconds per operation consumes about 3.97 CPU
seconds per second, filling the four assigned server CPUs. Client CPU remains below its
four-CPU allocation. This workload is limited by server CPU.

The indexed-read source is `9ed57ecd`. Its heap-fetch and visibility-counter code is
unchanged in `7f35a185`. The optimization avoids redundant heap location and pruning
work on fully visible modeled heaps, using an exact constant-time visible-page count.
The dirty-table repeat has overlapping timing and CPU ranges. Both engines' actual
visibility maps contain 1,469 visible pages out of 1,471, despite stale catalog estimates
of 1,471. Point-query gains are smaller, and secondary lookup is neutral.

A separate checked baseline comparison gives PostgreSQL 37,421 range operations per
second at eight clients, against the original engine's 7,500. This is a separate run,
not a paired comparison with the final source. PostgreSQL's OLTP lead remains substantial.
Profiles place remaining engine cost in allocation, catalog access, and expression
execution. Reducing those costs is the next measured target.

### Evidence and verification

The portable [analytic samples](tools/bench/results/2026-11-01/analytic.tsv),
[OLTP samples](tools/bench/results/2026-11-01/oltp.tsv), and
[manifest](tools/bench/results/2026-11-01/manifest.json) record every measured round,
source revision, binary hash, and resource configuration. The manifest identifies the
pinned PostgreSQL 18.6 image. Both endpoints use host networking and durable settings.
The [runner instructions](tools/bench/README.md) describe checks and resource accounting.

Raw logs, configurations, plans, independent fixture checks, profiles, and frozen
binaries are retained in `~/.cache/pg-engine-perf-20261101/`. Client CPU measurements
and server profiles rule out client saturation for the profiled top-N path. Separate
cancellation checks on both final engine and PostgreSQL produce SQLSTATE 57014 at a
40 ms timeout, then successfully execute another query on the same connection.
Live negative controls reject prepare failure, empty point results, and analytic errors.

Final checks pass on frozen Rust source `7f35a185`: the three-target release build,
full release crate suite (426 passed, 18 ignored, zero failed), all-target Clippy, and
`pnpm test:pg --unit`. Clippy retains eleven library warnings and four additional test
warnings, all in files unchanged by this campaign.

Fresh full release regression runs of both `0b950a11` and `7f35a185` match
51,148/51,150 units across 230 files. Their result rows and difference files are identical,
with zero panics or failed engine invocations. The remaining differences are `join` 368
and the accepted internal counter difference in `partition_prune` 410. The portable
[verification summary](tools/bench/results/2026-11-01/verification.json) includes counts
and difference-file hashes. Historical generated regression reports remain unchanged.
The exact scripts and complete gate logs are in the evidence directory above, under
`run-final-gates-7f35a185.sh`, `final-gates-7f35a185/`, and
`final-regress-{baseline-0b950a11,7f35a185}-release/`.

Docker recovery e2e remains waived under the user's instruction. Its retained baseline
has twelve passes and four WAL HTTP timeout failures. All campaign services are stopped,
and their data and logs are retained.

## Historical benchmark record

The following sections retain earlier measurements and their original interpretations.
Use the checked campaign above for claims about the current scan and index changes.

## Harness

- `tools/bench/bench.rs` (`cargo build --release -p pg-engine --example bench`): the
  workloads, against a server (`--target wire --port N`, tokio-postgres, the extended
  protocol with prepared statements for OLTP, simple queries for the rest) or in-process
  (`--target inproc --data <dir>`: `Engine::open`, `Engine::connect`, `Session` with
  `parse_message` / `bind_message` / `execute_portal` as a driver would). Prints `RESULT`
  lines: throughput and latency percentiles (p50/p95/p99), peak memory, per-query min /
  median / max and a hash of the result rows (the engine's rows are checked against
  PostgreSQL's while being timed).
- `tools/bench/tpch.rs`: a deterministic TPC-H-like generator (dbgen's schema, row counts,
  key formulas and value distributions closely enough for the 22 queries to select and join
  what they do; streamed through COPY in 100k-row chunks) and the 22 queries with the
  specification's validation parameters (Q15's view as a CTE).
- `tools/bench/run.sh`: starts the reference (`pg-start`, container `a36-pg`) and the engine
  server (`engine-start`), runs pgbench from the pinned image on the host network, the
  bench client, the in-process runs, disk and memory probes. `tools/bench/suite.sh <pg|engine|inproc>
  <label>` runs everything; `tools/bench/report.py <dir> <labels...>` makes the tables
  below. `tools/bench/perf.sh` profiles a running engine with perf from a privileged
  helper container (the host has no perf and keeps perf_event from users).
- Workloads:
  - **pgbench** (PostgreSQL's own client): TPC-B-like read/write and select-only (`-S`),
    scale 1 (100k accounts) and 10 (1M), 1 / 8 / 32 clients, `-M prepared`, 15 s each,
    data from `pgbench -i -I dtGvp` (server-side generate).
  - **OLTP mix** (1M-row table with a secondary index): point select (prepared and as a
    simple query), 100-row range scan, secondary-index lookup, insert, indexed update,
    upsert (`ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE`), a 3-statement transaction, `SELECT 1`; 1 and 8
    clients, 8 s each.
  - **Analytic** at 200k rows (a table small enough for the engine to model its heap) and
    2M (too big to model): filtered scan, full float and text sorts, top-N, hash aggregate,
    `count(DISTINCT)`, hash join + aggregate, window functions, index range scan, numeric
    sum; and **TPC-H** at SF1 (6M lineitems), each query 3 times.
  - **Bulk** at 100k and 1M rows: COPY in, CREATE INDEX (plain and unique), COPY out,
    INSERT … SELECT, UPDATE of half the rows, DELETE of a quarter, VACUUM, ANALYZE, a
    count after.
  - **Streaming** (`stream-load --rows 10000000`, `stream`): a 10M-row SELECT and COPY TO
    read as they come: time to the first row, the whole time, the server's peak memory.
  - **DDL and catalogs**: 500 tables (serial key, check, default, index, added column),
    `information_schema.columns` / `.tables`, psql's `\d` queries, a `pg_class` ×
    `pg_attribute` join, an ORM-style `pg_constraint` query, a `regclass` cast, DROP.
  - **Startup and idle**: connection setup, memory per idle session (100 sessions) and per
    database (10 databases with a table and a session each), `Engine::open` and the first
    query on a database of TPC-H SF1 (in-process).

## Setup and fairness

- Machine: 36 cores, 125 GB RAM, SATA SSDs in RAID 0, shared with other agents' builds
  (load 5–15 during the runs). Both servers are pinned to the same 4 cores (28–31: the
  engine with `taskset`, PostgreSQL with `docker --cpuset-cpus`), every client to 4 others
  (32–35); in-process runs get both sets (the client is in the process). Each number is the
  best (throughput) or median (time) of the repeats given; differences under ~10% are noise.
- PostgreSQL 18.6, the pinned image (`postgres@sha256:77f5851…`, `--locale=C`), data on a
  docker volume (disk): `shared_buffers=2GB`, `maintenance_work_mem=256MB`,
  `max_wal_size=8GB`, `checkpoint_timeout=30min`, `max_connections=300`, `fsync` and
  `synchronous_commit` on, parallel query at its defaults (2 workers per gather). Settings
  that change plans (`work_mem`, `effective_cache_size`, `random_page_cost`, …) stay at the
  defaults the engine also has, so both run the same plans.
- The engine: `roll-engine --cache-mb 2048` (the block cache, as shared_buffers), WAL fsync
  on commit (the default), data in `~/.cache/a36/engine` on the same disk.
- Memory: PostgreSQL's is the container cgroup's `anon + shmem` (backends' private memory
  plus the touched shared buffers); the engine's is the process RSS.

Reproduce (from `crates/pg-engine`; release builds through `tools/heavy.sh`):

```sh
tools/heavy.sh cargo build --release -p pg-engine --bin roll-engine --example bench
tools/bench/run.sh pg-start && tools/bench/suite.sh pg pg            # ~1 h
tools/bench/run.sh engine-start && SUITE=pgbench,oltp,analytic,tpch,bulk,ddl,idle \
  tools/bench/suite.sh engine engine                                  # ~1.5 h
tools/bench/run.sh engine-stop && SUITE=oltp,ddl,tpch tools/bench/suite.sh inproc inproc
python3 tools/bench/report.py ~/.cache/a36/results pg engine inproc
tools/bench/run.sh pg-stop
```

`BENCH_BIN` / `BENCH_CLIENT` pick other engine / client binaries (a baseline copied aside),
`BENCH_DATA` another data directory, `SERVER_CPUS` / `CLIENT_CPUS` / `PG_PORT` /
`ENGINE_PORT` the pinning and ports, `CACHE_MB` the engine's block cache (2048).

## Results (2026-10-24)

Columns: **pg** PostgreSQL 18.6; **before** the engine at `b1917d6` (this round's start);
**after** the engine with this round's changes, over the wire; **in-proc** the same engine
embedded (the client in the process: no sockets, no protocol encoding). Ratios are against
PostgreSQL: above 1x the engine is faster (throughput, or PostgreSQL's time over the
engine's). Engine rows agree with PostgreSQL's in every query the harness hashes (all of
TPC-H; the analytic set), except two float sums (`hash_agg`, `hash_join`) where PostgreSQL ran
a parallel plan and added its workers' partial sums in another order: the engine's sums equal
PostgreSQL's with `max_parallel_workers_per_gather = 0` digit for digit.
