## Memory under load (round 22, agent 125)

HANDOFF.md, "Memory under load", has what changed. Release builds over the wire on one
machine shared with other agents' builds (load average 25-30, so CPU figures vary by about
10%); `base` is d6746f74 (this round's start), `new` this round's first batch. PostgreSQL
18.6 is the pinned image with its defaults (`shared_buffers` 128 MB, `work_mem` 4 MB) and
`max_connections=300`; its memory is the container's anon + shmem, the engine's its RSS.
Workload `bench burst` (`tools/bench/burst.rs`) at `burst-load --scale 2`: agent 111's mix
round-robin per session (a PL/pgSQL loop of 10 primary-key lookups, `sum()` of 100 calls of a
SQL function over a FROM, a four-table 36-column join by a random customer, psql's `\d`
column query and an information_schema.columns query, pgbench's TPC-B) for 20 s. The engine
starts afresh for each size (block cache 64 MB, the server's default), so the burst's first
statements open the database: that is the case the 4-8 GB peaks came from.

### Peak and after (MB; `after`: 60 s after the burst with the sessions idle; `closed`: 5 s after they disconnect)

| sessions | | ops/s | CPU µs/op | connected | peak | after | closed |
|---:|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 10 | PostgreSQL | 2,912 | 2,037 | 114 | 138 | 132 | 104 |
| | base | 1,222 | 3,040 | 791 | 1,197 | 569 | 556 |
| | new | 1,574 | 2,600 | 158 | 375 | 356 | 351 |
| 50 | PostgreSQL | 1,777 | 2,339 | 174 | 253 | 245 | 105 |
| | base | 844 | 5,678 | 1,130 | 3,654 | 591 | 580 |
| | new | 1,102 | 2,914 | 179 | 477 | 425 | 394 |
| 100 | PostgreSQL | 4,817 | 2,198 | 245 | 412 | 388 | 108 |
| | base | 886 | 6,642 | 1,665 | 3,172 | 650 | 628 |
| | new | 1,116 | 2,880 | 215 | 593 | 491 | 424 |
| 200 | PostgreSQL | 3,047 | 2,351 | 389 | 686 | 669 | 110 |
| | base | 63 | 75,315 | 2,483 | 4,697 | 701 | 660 |
| | new | 1,143 | 3,063 | 243 | 760 | 603 | 485 |

- **Where base's peak went** (sampling heap profile, `--features heapprof`, 200 sessions, 9.0
  GB live at the peak): 89% in `sizing::ensure`, every session laying out its own heap and
  btree models of pgbench_accounts (200,000 rows) and b_item: the rows read, their layouts and
  index keys, all but one copy thrown away. At 200 sessions the builds also starved the CPU
  (63 operations a second). `new` builds each model once (peak at 200 sessions 4.7 GB -> 760
  MB, 18x the operations).
- **The parse tree cache** held 297 MB of trees (of the 490 MB live after a warm 200-session
  run) under its "32 MB" bound: it counted 8 bytes per byte of text, a tenth of what the trees
  take. It holds 32 MB now (`tests/memory_load.rs`: 33.5 MB freed by emptying it, 32.0 MB
  counted).
- **After the burst**: `new` at 100 sessions (in another run) 579 MB at the end, 431 MB once
  the sessions closed, 434 MB until the database closed (60 s idle), then 190 MB; `base` 717
  MB closed and 634 MB three minutes later, the database closed too: nothing ran the
  allocator's collection, so the freed memory stayed. What `new` keeps with the database open
  is mostly live (264 MB live of 506 MB RSS 55 s after a 100-session burst: the size models
  ~130 MB, the block cache 64 MB, the parse trees 32 MB) plus mimalloc's partly used pages.
  PostgreSQL's backends keep their memory until they disconnect, then it is gone (104-110
  MB with shared buffers).
- mimalloc's purge delay (`MIMALLOC_PURGE_DELAY`, default 1,000 ms): 10 ms gave a 531 MB
  peak at 100 sessions (593), 0 ms 488 MB, at 3-9% more CPU per operation; left at the
  default.
- **Not fixed: per-statement working memory.** The mix with `--mix sort,agg` added (a sort of
  20,000 rows under a LIMIT, a grouping of all 200,000 accounts into 50,000 groups) at 100
  sessions: the engine peaked at 5,274 MB and 53 ops/s, PostgreSQL at 733 MB and 902 ops/s.
  The profile (sort and grouping only, 5.2 GB peak): 30% a sequential scan collecting the
  table's rows to return them in heap order (`scan.rs` `next_raw`: 200,000 rows of a table
  whose updates moved rows, about 13 MB per session; agent 117's scan path), 22% the grouping's
  states (`agg.rs` `states`), 22% its keys (each group's key twice: `groups` and `index`),
  7% the hashed order's lookups (`pghash::GroupOrder`, 16 bytes per input row), 4% the hash
  table's growth. PostgreSQL's HashAggregate stays within hash_mem (8 MB) and spills; the
  engine keeps every group (about 45 MB per session here). Spilling where PostgreSQL spills
  (and its EXPLAIN ANALYZE lines, which the engine models already) is the next step.

### Other workloads, base -> new

| | base | new |
|---|---:|---:|
| pgbench -S, 8 clients, prepared: CPU µs / transaction (3 alternated runs) | 112, 119, 119 | 108, 114, 113 |
| pgbench TPC-B, 8 clients, prepared: CPU µs / transaction | 861, 903, 934 | 829, 900, 904 |
| RSS after those runs (MB) | 316-341 | 151-188 |
| TPC-H SF 0.2, 22 queries, sum of medians of 3 (s) | 33.2 | 31.9 |
| TPC-H SF 0.2: server CPU (s), peak RSS (MB) | 57.1, 648 | 56.8, 518 |

The test `tests/memory_load.rs` `many_sessions_stay_bounded` runs 100 sessions of a
smaller mix on a database just opened, in-process with a counting allocator: +80-90 MB of
heap at the peak (release and debug), +640 MB with every session building its own models; it
fails over 40 MB + 0.8 MB a session (`MEMLOAD_SESSIONS`, `MEMLOAD_SECS` for bigger bursts).

## OLTP round 2 (round 22, agent 123)

HANDOFF.md, "OLTP round 2", has what changed: statement templates and kept plans for simple
queries, fast-path relation locks. Release builds with frame pointers, pgbench in a container
on the host network, scale 10, 15 s per run after a 4 s warm-up, the engine restarted per
binary; servers on cores 24–27, pgbench on 28–31; PostgreSQL 18.6 on the host network
(shared_buffers 2 GB). The machine ran other agents' builds and suites throughout (load
15–35, every core 75–100% busy), so CPU time moves a lot between runs; instructions per
transaction (`perf stat` of the engine process, PostgreSQL's cgroup) move less, but include
the engine's background flushes and compactions, which explains most single-run outliers.
**base** is `629f3e51`, **new** this round on it; one interleaved run each (CPU ms /
kinstr per transaction, peak memory: the engine's VmHWM, PostgreSQL's memory.peak).

**Simple protocol** (`-M simple`, pgbench's default; ORMs' unnamed statements are next):

| workload | clients | base | new | PostgreSQL | new peak (base) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| tpcb-like | 1 | 1.44 ms / 1,765 | 0.87 / 1,572 | 0.73 / 1,013 | 281 MB (517) |
| tpcb-like | 8 | 1.36 / 1,720 | 0.73 / 1,176 | 0.60 / 1,033 | 317 MB (571) |
| tpcb-like | 32 | 1.28 / 2,054 | 0.93 / 1,327 | 0.72 / 1,092 | 341 MB (648) |
| tpcb-like | 64 | 1.57 / 2,201 | 0.98 / 1,434 | 0.84 / 1,174 | 417 MB (618) |
| web-app mix | 1 | 1.91 / 1,976 | 1.07 / 1,239 | 0.89 / 1,182 | 241 MB (464) |
| web-app mix | 8 | 1.76 / 2,417 | 1.11 / 1,566 | 0.79 / 1,183 | 191 MB (917) |
| web-app mix | 32 | 1.76 / 2,362 | 1.16 / 1,692 | 0.74 / 1,182 | 227 MB (532) |
| web-app mix | 64 | 1.79 / 2,440 | 1.40 / 1,895 | 0.89 / 1,193 | 258 MB (705) |

tpcb-like over the simple protocol now costs the engine about what it costs over prepared
statements (0.6–0.9 ms) and 1.2–1.5x PostgreSQL's CPU (was 2–2.3x). Its five statements are
all templated and all keep plans (the accounts / tellers / branches UPDATEs, the SELECT by
key, the history INSERT); BEGIN / END have no literals. In the web-app mix the
`count(*) ... WHERE aid = $n` (a non-unique column: its estimate depends on the value) is
parsed through its template but planned each time. Peak memory fell with it: the base's
binding of every statement allocates more than the kept plans hold (a few KB per template
per session). A profile of the new engine's simple tpcb-like at 8 clients: no query is bound
the usual way any more; 8% is binding a template's generic statement again after a size the
planner reads changed (the tellers' and branches' modelled heaps change size every few
transactions, as PostgreSQL's would and its planner would read), 6% the parse through the
template (scan, shared-cache lookup, tree copy), 3.5% re-reading the sizes a kept plan
depends on.

**Prepared statements** (`-M prepared`): no change beyond noise, as expected (the kept plans
are for simple queries; the fast path saves the lock table's mutex for about ten relation
locks per tpcb-like transaction). Three interleaved rounds, kinstr per transaction:

| workload | clients | base | new |
|---|---|---|---|
| tpcb-like | 8 | 1,136 / 1,164 / 1,111 | 1,117 / 1,143 / 1,136 |
| tpcb-like | 64 | 1,263 / 1,252 / 1,210 | 1,242 / 1,249 / 1,170 |
| simple-update | 1 | 510 / 1,212 / 1,263 | 878 / 554 / 543 |
| web-app mix | 1 | 1,008 / 1,021 / 2,062 | 1,019 / 1,420 / 1,010 |

(The 1-client rows' spread is the background compactions the whole-process count includes.)
One interleaved matrix run, prepared, CPU ms per transaction base / new / PostgreSQL:
tpcb-like 0.91 / 0.91 / 0.52 at 1 client, 0.91 / 0.90 / 0.49 at 8, 0.98 / 0.77 / 0.64 at 32,
0.82 / 0.88 / 0.67 at 64; select-only 0.089 / 0.067 / 0.060, 0.068 / 0.075 / 0.055, 0.076 /
0.096 / 0.064, 0.098 / 0.090 / 0.078; web-app 0.85 / 0.90 / 0.58, 0.85 / 0.84 / 0.55, 0.96 /
0.85 / 0.53, 1.20 / 1.10 / 0.64. Peak memory within 10% of the base's for every row but
web-app at 64 clients (538 MB against 452: its events table, grown by the runs before).

**Where tpcb-like's CPU goes at 32 clients (prepared)**: the accounts UPDATE's index lookup
and row fetch 24% (agent 121's point lookups), sending 14% (one send per Sync), commit 7%,
row locks 5%, `update_row_rls` 4%, relation locks 3% before the fast path, the size model's
`sizing::updated` 2% (agent 126). What is left of this round's list: the prepared and
PL/pgSQL first five custom plans could use the same check to run the generic binding
(HANDOFF.md, "OLTP round 2"); unnamed extended-protocol statements likewise; the web-app
mix's non-unique `count(*)` and its bigserial INSERT; hot-row lock waits at 32+ clients.

## Replication, round 5 (round 22, agent 129)

HANDOFF.md "Round 5 (agent 129)" has what changed. Release builds; every engine with
`--cache-mb 64` (the default) on a fresh data directory, pinned with `taskset`; PostgreSQL
18.6 (the pinned image) in containers on the host network with `--cpuset-cpus`; machine
load 20-45 from other agents. Scripts were in `~/.cache/a129/bench` (apply.sh, pubn.sh,
pgb.sh, e2e.sh; gone with the cache), described here so they can be rebuilt.

**Apply** (`apply.sh`; agent 119's benchmark gave both subscriptions the slot `asub`, so its
PostgreSQL rounds never ran: each subscription now has a slot of its own, made before the
backlog). A publisher holds 60,000 pgbench-like transactions (4 clients: UPDATE of a
10,000-row table by key and INSERT into an indexed history table; one in three also DELETEs
the history's oldest row); each subscriber, created disabled with copy_data = false on its
own slot, is enabled alone and timed until its slot confirmed the end. Default
synchronous_commit of a subscription (off). CPU: the engine process, PostgreSQL's whole
container (apply worker, walwriter, checkpointer). Rounds interleaved, the order rotating.
All subscribers end identical to the publisher (md5 of every row of both tables).

| publisher | subscriber | time | CPU | memory |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | PostgreSQL 18.6 | 2.8-3.1 s | 3.1-3.3 s | apply worker 33 MB RSS |
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | engine, base ff187f1f | 4.7-5.4 s | 4.3-4.9 s | process +49-52 MB |
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | engine, this round | 4.6-5.1 s | 3.9-4.6 s | process +44-52 MB |
| engine | PostgreSQL 18.6 | 4.3-5.4 s | 4.3-5.3 s | apply worker 34 MB RSS |
| engine | engine, base | 7.0 s | 5.7-6.8 s | process +38-81 MB |
| engine | engine, this round | 7.5-8.1 s | 6.4-6.7 s | process +38-68 MB |

(Load 22 for the PostgreSQL-publisher rows, 20-40 for the engine-publisher ones; in one
round of each engine build the RSS fell, the previous round's memtable going.)
- The apply worker no longer waits for the WAL fsync of each commit: that came with
  b7d5f1fe (its session runs `SET synchronous_commit = off`); agent 119's 15.5 s / 8.6 s CPU
  predates it. Wall time is now about CPU time. What this round changed is the feedback: it
  reports flush only once the commit is durable (`Store::durable_ts`), at no measurable cost.
- The rest of the gap (about 1.3x PostgreSQL's CPU) is the engine's write path (agent 108's
  profile: the size models, memtable searches, allocation), not the apply layer.
- Engine publisher rows: the walsender side costs the engine 0.8-2.1 s CPU against
  PostgreSQL's 0.9-1.5 s; one in three backlog transactions' `DELETE ... WHERE hid = (SELECT
  min(hid) ...)` made the engine's pgbench run take minutes (not replication: the engine's
  min() over a big table with dead versions at its start; left for the planner / storage).

**Fan-out** (`pubn.sh`, agent 119's publisher benchmark): a backlog of 20,000 transactions
(UPDATE by key, INSERT into a history table; 4 clients); N PostgreSQL 18.6 subscriptions of
one publication, each into a database of its own, enabled at once and timed until every
slot confirmed the end. CPU: the engine process / PostgreSQL's walsenders; memory: the
engine's RSS peak over its RSS before / the walsenders' summed RSS peak. Two interleaved
rounds of each.

| N | PostgreSQL 18.6 | engine, base ff187f1f | engine, this round |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 1.3-2.1 s, CPU 0.16-0.29 s, 18 MB | 2.3-2.7 s, CPU 0.55-0.81 s, +28-43 MB | 2.5-2.7 s, CPU 0.46 s, +46-50 MB |
| 4 | 1.8-2.1 s, CPU 1.4-1.7 s, 71 MB | 2.8-3.4 s, CPU 2.6-3.0 s, +33-34 MB | 2.9-3.3 s, CPU 0.86-1.06 s, +36-39 MB |
| 16 | 5.5-6.6 s, CPU 4.6-6.9 s, 283 MB | 6.2-6.8 s, CPU 8.4-9.8 s, +36 MB | 6.9-7.2 s, CPU 3.7-3.9 s, +44 MB |

- CPU per walsender went from about 0.55 s per 20,000 transactions to about 0.2 s, under
  PostgreSQL's 0.3-0.4 at N = 4 and 16: decoded once (`decode::Shared`), each relation
  description parsed once (its JSON was a fifth of the profile) and pgoutput's relation
  entries kept across transactions (they were worked out again every transaction).
- Memory: the shared cache's bound is 16 MB of decoded changes per database. The peaks
  above (+28-50 MB over the run, both builds) are mostly the memtable's growth and vary by
  round as much as between the builds; at N = 16 the engine's whole growth is a sixth of
  PostgreSQL's walsenders (17 MB each).
- Wall time is the PostgreSQL subscribers' (both publishers wait for them).

**pgbench check of agent 119's batches** (`pgb.sh`: scale 10, `-M prepared`, 8 clients, 20 s,
`--cache-mb 2048` as tools/bench uses; CPU per transaction of the engine process, VmHWM; 4
rounds interleaved at load 40-45, medians and spread):

| build | select-only CPU / txn | tpcb-like CPU / txn | VmHWM |
|---|---|---|---|
| 898ef33a (before 15f967cd) | 0.100 ms (0.086-0.105) | 0.893 ms (0.825-0.929) | 384 MB (381-392) |
| ff187f1f (agent 119's last) | 0.091 ms (0.084-0.103) | 0.870 ms (0.843-0.952) | 391 MB (390-397) |
| this round | 0.097 ms (0.090-0.101) | 0.904 ms (0.774-0.922) | 385 MB (382-391) |

No regression beyond the rounds' spread (this round's build, which changes nothing on the
OLTP path, moved by as much as the two bases differ). An earlier pair of 3 rounds at load
30-35 agrees (select-only 0.078-0.082 / 0.070-0.089 ms, tpcb-like 0.62-0.78 / 0.74-0.84 ms).

**Side by side** (`e2e.sh`): initial synchronization of four tables (one of 200,000 rows)
under two pgbench clients changing them, then 015_stream.pl-style streamed transactions
(logical_decoding_work_mem = 64kB) with a rolled-back savepoint and an aborted one, REPLICA
IDENTITY FULL updates and a partitioned engine target with partition moves; a PostgreSQL
and an engine subscriber of a PostgreSQL publisher (streaming = on) and of an engine
publisher (streaming = parallel): md5 of every row identical on all three, no
synchronization slot left on the publisher.

## Replication, round 6 (agent 152)

HANDOFF.md "Round 6 (agent 152)" has what changed. Scripts: `tools/bench/repl/` (apply.sh,
pubn.sh, pgb.sh, e2e.sh; README.md there; `PERF=<container>` records a profile of the
engine's cores during the catch-up, `stacks.py` attributes it). Release builds, `--cache-mb
64`, the engine pinned to cores 24-27, PostgreSQL 18.6 (pinned image) on the host network
with `--cpuset-cpus 20-23`, machine load 16-25 from other agents. The engine ran on the host
(a `release` build), in user.slice with every agent's builds, PostgreSQL in its container's
cgroup: the comparisons with PostgreSQL lean against the engine; from 847fbdba on they are
made with both in containers (`run.sh`, `ENGINE_DOCKER=1`) and a `dist` build. Base: `3052087d`
(origin when this batch started, with the round's first commits 2eb31f22 / 90ce701d /
bb338b77); head: this batch.

**Apply** (`apply.sh`, PostgreSQL 18.6 publisher, 60,000 pgbench-like transactions; three
rounds, the subscribers' order rotating; every subscriber ends with the publisher's md5 of
every row):

| subscriber | time | CPU | memory | engine / PostgreSQL CPU |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | 2.99-3.03 s | 2.38-2.44 s | apply worker 30 MB | |
| engine, base | 4.54-4.78 s | 3.94-4.12 s | process +42-43 MB | 1.66 |
| engine, head | 4.35-4.64 s | 3.84-3.89 s | process +43-49 MB | 1.59 |

Where the engine's apply thread goes (frame-pointer build, `perf record -g`, base): size
models 22% (`exec::sizing::updated` 11.6%, `inserted_with` 10.5%; `btsim::Btree::insert` alone
9.2%: sent to agent 151, whose next pushes cache the last leaf and fold the UPDATE's second
row lookup), commit 17% (memtable inserts 6.5%, WAL append 2.7%, lock release 1.6%), locating
rows 14% (a primary-key row read three times: now once), the per-change statement wrapper 6%,
value input 3% (timestamptz text), the background flush 6.6% of the process. The memory
growth is the store's (memtable and block cache), PostgreSQL's apply worker RSS excludes
its shared buffers.

**Walsenders** (`pubn.sh`: 20,000 transactions, N PostgreSQL subscriptions enabled at once,
timed until each has the publisher's WAL end, then until every slot confirmed it; watched
from the subscriber now, since a query on the engine every 0.1 s, a connection each, was
counted as the walsender's cost):

| N | PostgreSQL 18.6 | engine, head | engine / PostgreSQL time, CPU, memory |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0.95 s, CPU 0.15 s, 16 MB | 0.80-0.99 s, CPU 0.34-0.41 s, +43-46 MB | 0.95, 2.5, 2.8 |
| 4 | 1.51-1.84 s, CPU 0.97-0.99 s, 64 MB | 0.92-1.06 s, CPU 0.82-0.93 s, +51-53 MB | 0.6, 0.9, 0.8 |
| 16 | 4.77-5.05 s, CPU 2.81-3.33 s, 256 MB | 1.66-1.67 s, CPU 2.74-2.77 s, +65-77 MB | 0.34, 0.9, 0.3 |

(Engine CPU is the whole process; PostgreSQL's the walsenders'. Base, same runs at N = 1 / 4:
0.31-0.56 s / 1.07-1.10 s.) One walsender's own work (profile, engine threads only):
decoding 48% (two thirds of it the store scan of the records), the change stream's removal
36% (`log::gc_writes` reads every confirmed record back to write a tombstone for it, once a
second), output 8%. With `--cache-mb 8` the growth at N = 1 is +28-32 MB: about 15 MB of it is
the block cache filled by reading the records back.

**Side by side** (`e2e.sh`, all scenarios: streamed transactions with rolled-back nested
subtransactions, an exception block and DDL in a rolled-back one, streaming = on and
parallel; row filter with column list; publish_via_partition_root with TRUNCATE; two-phase
with a streamed prepare; disable_on_error and SKIP; conflict statistics; run_as_owner and its
change while running; failover slots; origin = none both ways): every check identical on
the PostgreSQL and engine subscribers of both publishers. Base failed `run_as_owner` (the
engine subscriber of the engine publisher applied the next transaction with the old
setting); before the round's first commits, `via_root` (the root's TRUNCATE) and
`twophase` (engine to engine) differed too.

**test_decoding** 1,043 / 1,047 (base 1,043: the TOAST accounting fixes stream's TOAST case
and costs its multi-insert case, HANDOFF).

**pgbench check** (`pgb.sh`: scale 10, `-M prepared`, 8 clients, 20 s, `--cache-mb 2048`;
four rounds interleaved at load 16-25 with other agents' builds; CPU per transaction of the
engine process, VmHWM): select-only base 0.145-0.181 ms, head 0.148-0.168 ms; tpcb-like base
0.759-0.931 ms, head 0.736-0.932 ms; VmHWM base 410-440 MB, head 420-450 MB. No change beyond
the rounds' spread (this batch touches no OLTP path).

**Verified** (this batch): crate tests debug and release (deep_input and logical_apply
included; `run_as_owner_and_privileges` now changes run_as_owner while the worker runs),
clippy without new warnings, test_decoding 1,043 / 1,047, the side-by-side scripts above all
identical, isolation 121 / 122 (intra-grant-inplace, the known L2 difference) and the 17

**test_decoding stats units** (sent to agent 155, planner): three slot-creating function
scans in one FROM are planned as `Nested Loop (Function Scan s3, Materialize (Nested Loop
(s1, s2)))`; PostgreSQL 18.6 nests s1 and s2, then s3, so its slots are created a, b, c and
take those places in pg_replication_slots, the engine's c, a, b:

```sql
EXPLAIN (COSTS OFF) SELECT 'init' FROM pg_create_logical_replication_slot('a', 'test_decoding') s1,
  pg_create_logical_replication_slot('b', 'test_decoding') s2, pg_create_logical_replication_slot('c', 'test_decoding') s3;
-- (with generate_series(1, 1) in their place both nest s1, s2, then s3)
```

per transaction moved by up to 2x between identical runs; context switches per transaction
(`perf stat -e context-switches` of the engine) are what the change is about and held still.

- Off-CPU (`perf record -e sched:sched_switch -g`, 64 clients of tpcb-like, 16 s; the first

  little. Its VmHWM at 64 clients was 40-75 MB higher on head in both rounds (allocation by

- Per-write profile after the change (8 clients, `perf record -g -a` on the engine's cores):
  scale 1 9.8% of the engine's samples in the models (`fetched` → `Heap::prune_opt` 2.7%,
  `Kills::mark` → `kill_needed` 1.6%, `updated` 1.9%, `inserted_with` 0.5%), scale 10 5.5%
  (`row_roots` 1.5%, `updated` 1.5%, `inserted_with` 0.7%, `fetched` 0.6%).

**A clean restart against PostgreSQL 18.6** (a mixed workload, then each relation's pages per
fork and two EXPLAINs, a restart, the same again, then inserts before and after a VACUUM):

```
CREATE TABLE mw1 (id int PRIMARY KEY, k int, v text, pad char(40)); CREATE INDEX mw1_k ON mw1 (k);
CREATE INDEX mw1_v ON mw1 (v); CREATE TABLE mw2 (id bigserial PRIMARY KEY, ref int, note text);
CREATE INDEX mw2_ref ON mw2 (ref);
INSERT INTO mw1 SELECT g, g % 1000, md5(g::text), 'p' FROM generate_series(1, 60000) g;
INSERT INTO mw2 (ref, note) SELECT g % 5000, repeat('n', g % 40) FROM generate_series(1, 40000) g;
UPDATE mw1 SET k = k + 1 WHERE id % 7 = 0; UPDATE mw1 SET pad = 'q' WHERE id % 5 = 0;
UPDATE mw1 SET v = md5(v) WHERE id % 11 = 0; DELETE FROM mw1 WHERE id % 13 = 0;
DELETE FROM mw2 WHERE id % 3 = 0; VACUUM mw2;
INSERT INTO mw1 SELECT g, g % 50, md5((g * 3)::text), 'r' FROM generate_series(60001, 70000) g;
INSERT INTO mw2 (ref, note) SELECT g % 700, 'again' FROM generate_series(1, 8000) g;
UPDATE mw2 SET note = note || '!' WHERE ref % 9 = 0; DELETE FROM mw1 WHERE id BETWEEN 20000 AND 26000;
```

| pages (main / fsm / vm) | PostgreSQL, before = after | head, before = after | base before | base after |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| mw1 | 1176 / 3 / 0 | 1176 / 3 / 0 | 1176 / 3 / 0 | 1176 / 0 / 0 |
| mw1_pkey | 357 | 357 | 357 | 166 |
| mw1_k | 100 | 100 | 100 | 53 |
| mw1_v | 725 | 721 | 721 | 434 |
| mw2 | 315 / 3 / 1 | 315 / 3 / 1 | 315 / 3 / 1 | 315 / 0 / 0 |
| mw2_pkey | 134 | 134 | 134 | 97 |
| mw2_ref | 65 | 65 | 65 | 46 |

Inserts after the restart: 300 rows into mw1 at (1175,60)-(1178,91), 300 into mw2 at
(217,43)-(225,116), after VACUUM 500 rows into mw1 at (0,7)-(22,33): head's are PostgreSQL's
(base's went to (314,35) and (0,5)). `mw1_v`'s 721 pages against 725 is the model's before the
restart too (md5 text keys' page splits), not the restart's.

**First statement after a clean restart** (300,000 rows of pgbench_accounts' shape, a tenth
updated; over the wire, psql's `\timing`; two interleaved rounds at load ~40): base 186 / 202
ms, head 172 / 181 ms, VmHWM 145-148 MB both. The database opens on its first connection, so
the background builder is laying out that very table when the first statement asks for it.
What's left of the layout (perf): `heapsim::restored` 59% (the key map's inserts and their
allocations), the record's decoding 23% (`Btree::read_image` 13%), `read_shape` 2%.

**Model memory** (item 5): VmRSS of a freshly opened engine 49.7 MB; after the 300,000-row
table's models are laid out 156.7 MB, about 356 bytes per row with the primary key (1.2 GB per
million rows; PostgreSQL's pages for them: 44 MB heap and 6.8 MB index).

## Catalog round 8 (round 23, agent 131)

Release builds; **base** is `77a38372` (the branch's head when the round began), **new** each
batch. The machine was loaded throughout (load 25-45 from other agents): runs interleaved,
the engine pinned to four cores, its CPU time from `/proc/<pid>/stat`, peak RSS `VmHWM`.

### First batch: `pg_restore -j 4`'s foreign keys, DDL after its locks

PostgreSQL's regression database (a private PostgreSQL 18.6 after a full `pg_regress` run with
`PGREGRESS_KEEP=1`, `pg_dump -Fc`: 3.4 MB, 1,669 relations, 65 foreign keys) restored with
`pg_restore -j 4` into a fresh engine per run:

| | runs | runs failing a statement |
|---|---|---|
| base | 95 | 5 (`relation "21402" does not exist`, ...: one FOREIGN KEY each) |
| new | 200 | 0 |

The oid was an index's: `perms::rel_word` didn't name indexes, and `Ddl::note_lock` took a
relation it couldn't name for one dropped while its lock was waited for whenever another
session's DDL committed between its two catalog reads; an RI check locks both tables'
indexes. Spec `roll-ddl-after-lock-waits` reproduces it every time (an ALTER INDEX waiting for
another ALTER INDEX's lock, which now exists).

| | base | new |
|---|---|---|
| restore wall (15 interleaved runs each, median; a build running alongside) | 5.3 s | 5.2 s |
| pgbench `-S` tps (3 rounds) | 9,672 / 9,014 / 10,449 | 9,634 / 9,278 / 11,264 |
| `-S` engine CPU per 1,000 transactions | 236-248 ms | 251-265 ms |
| pgbench `-N` tps | 2,846 / 3,303 / 3,402 | 2,870 / 3,151 / 3,272 |
| `-N` engine CPU per 1,000 transactions | 703-803 ms | 699-763 ms |
| pgbench peak RSS (`-N`) | 489-519 MB | 494-518 MB |
| Kysely's suite: engine CPU (2 rounds), peak RSS | 11.8 / 11.0 s, 100-103 MB | 11.1 / 10.5 s, 101-103 MB |
| `catalog-tools` at 10,000 tables: engine CPU, RSS | 1.88 / 1.83 s, 170-174 MB | 1.90 / 1.86 s, 172 MB |

(pgbench: scale 10, 4 clients, 10,000 transactions each, `--no-sync`. Unchanged within the
noise, as expected: the batch changes which locks DDL takes and when it reads the catalog
again, not the statements' own work.)

`catalog-tools` at 10,000 tables (ms, first in the session / again; medians of 2 rounds):

and its pg_depend rescan derives the one relation's rows. Its first run in a process (or after
any DDL) is still 213 ms of planning: `explain::sys_tuples` generates pg_depend's 116,000
rows to count them (HANDOFF's "Left"). `\d sc0.t5000`'s first time is pg_attribute's and
pg_constraint's first reads (columns 42-49 ms, indexes 36-41 ms).

Memory after a start and reading pg_class, pg_attribute, pg_type, pg_index, pg_constraint
and pg_stat_user_tables whole in one session (the session held open while measured), from
each container's cgroup (`memory.current`; `memory.stat`'s anon, shmem), MB:

| | memory.current | anon | shmem | process RSS |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| PostgreSQL | 200 | 12 | 137 | |
| base | 116 | 114 | 0 | 141 |
| this batch | 112 | 110 | 0 | 138 |

The method: the cgroup's `memory.current` on both sides (each side's private memory, shared
memory and page cache, measured the same way), or anon + shmem without the reclaimable page
cache (PostgreSQL 149, the engine 110). The engine's RSS counts the mapped binary's resident
pages (its 70 MB text), which PostgreSQL's anon doesn't: round 11's 143 MB was RSS, and
PostgreSQL's 134 / 177 were its cgroup with and without a page cache drop.

## Catalog round 11 (round 26, agent 173)

HANDOFF.md "Catalog round 11, first batch" has what changed (catalog format 5: each
relation's directory record; pg_class and the statistics views read from them). The same
objects as round 10 (`bench scale`'s: 10,000 tables of three columns with a primary key and
a default in 10 schemas, a partitioned table of 1,000 partitions, loaded with psql), and a
second database `k1` of 1,000 such tables. The engine is the dist build of `89daa2a5` in a
container (`run.sh` `ENGINE_DOCKER=1`), PostgreSQL 18.6 in its container on the host
network, both on cores 12-15; psql `\timing` (ms) from a client container on cores 16-17;
other agents' builds and suites ran on the machine throughout (load 30-50).

First query after a server start (each on a freshly started server, then once more; the
engine's CPU and peak RSS over both). PostgreSQL restarted per run, its first numbers
include its relcache init:

| query | PostgreSQL | round 10 | this batch (CPU, RSS) |
|---|---|---|---|
| `count(*)` of pg_stat_user_tables | 43-51 / 34-39 | 75 / 9.9 | 26-33 / 7.9-8.8 (0.10 s, 58 MB) |
| pg_stat_user_tables `relname =` | 9.3-13 / 1.5-3.4 | 30 / 0.55 | 13-20 / 0.3-0.4 (0.07 s, 45 MB) |
| `count(*)` of pg_statio_user_tables | 24-28 / 9.6-17 | 52 / 6.6 | 22-31 / 5.6-6.2 (0.09 s, 53 MB) |
| psql `\dt` of a schema | 17-27 / 10-18 | 110 / 15 | 64-95 / 9.8-16 (0.15 s, 68 MB) |
| the same after a first `count(*) FROM pg_am` | | | 52-64 / 9.8-15 (first query 5.7-11) |
| `count(*)` of pg_class | 6.9-11 / 4.1-4.5 | 24 / 0.65 | 6.5-11 / 0.3-0.5 (0.07 s, 41 MB) |
| `SELECT * FROM pg_class` | 104-117 / 92-98 | 231 / 79 | 123-165 / 53-60 (0.28 s, 72 MB) |
| pg_class × pg_attribute of a schema | 39-41 / 10-12 | 141 / 51 | 145-179 / 37-60 (0.28 s, 94 MB) |

The statistics views and `count(*)` of pg_class no longer parse a table's entry: their first
read decodes the directory records (40 bytes a relation). psql's first `\dt` still pays for
the process's first catalog queries (function registry, Postgres's own pg_class rows parsed
from text; 6-11 ms in a first query of its own, 17 ms before this batch) and reads every
table's directory record before the hash join names the schema; its planning is 20 ms of
it. A first pg_attribute read parses every table's entry (its columns are the entry's).

Steady state, again in one session (PostgreSQL / engine):

| query | PostgreSQL | engine |
|---|---|---|
| `count(*)` of pg_stat_user_tables | 29-30 | 9.8-16 |
| pg_stat_user_tables `relname =` | 1.1-1.6 | 0.40-0.49 |
| pg_stat_user_tables `relid =` | 0.8-3.7 | 0.22-0.35 |
| `count(*)` of pg_stat_user_indexes | 27-42 | 17-26 |
| `count(*)` of pg_statio_user_tables | 8.6-18 | 4.0-7.3 |
| `count(*)` of pg_class | 3.3-5.8 | 0.36-0.53 |
| `SELECT * FROM pg_class` | 87-125 | 61-66 |

psql `\d sc0.t5000` (its 11 queries, steady, three rounds; with agent 171's planner round 10
underneath): PostgreSQL 8.3-9.4 ms in all, the engine 11-22 ms (round 10: 23-38). Per query,
PostgreSQL / engine: lookup 0.33-0.41 / 0.78-2.0, table 0.32-0.40 / 0.93-1.9, columns
0.44-0.63 / 0.91-2.6, indexes 0.73-0.87 / 2.7-6.2, foreign keys 0.20-0.28 / 0.53-1.3,
referenced by 3.3-3.8 / 0.78-1.4, policies 0.43-0.54 / 0.42-0.93, statistics 0.21-0.22 /
0.28-1.6, publications 0.81-0.91 / 1.9-2.4, inherits 0.25-0.27 / 1.0-1.5, partitions
0.34-0.42 / 0.99-4.3. The unqualified `\d t5000` lookup (`relname ~ '^(t5000)$'` without a
schema) still evaluates the pattern on every pg_class row (26-92 ms, in `820b1573` too,
against PostgreSQL's 0.1-1.0): HANDOFF's "Left".

Memory at 10,000 tables after reading pg_class, pg_attribute, pg_type, pg_index,
pg_constraint and pg_stat_user_tables whole in one session: the engine's RSS 21 MB fresh,
143 MB after (round 10: 22 / 166); PostgreSQL's cgroup `memory.current` after a restart and

Ahead: whole-schema listings (Django's table list and inspectdb, Prisma, Npgsql's schema
collections, pgjdbc getTables at 10k). Behind: the per-table queries after the first (planning
bound: HANDOFF), first calls at 10k (a first read of pg_attribute parses every table's entry),
SQLAlchemy's schema reflection (1,000 tables' queries), and EF Core, whose queries put
`NOT EXISTS (SELECT ... FROM pg_depend WHERE classid = (SELECT ...) ...)` beside
`relname IN (...)`: the pg_class scan checks that subplan on every row (HANDOFF).
Django's sequences query (pg_depend `refclassid = 1259 AND refobjid = x`) was 130-150 ms at
10,000 tables and is 3.6-4.3 (the most selective equality picks the rows).

minutes 0.025 s (PostgreSQL 0.64), memory with 100 idle sessions 31.1 MB (211), a fresh data
directory 3.03 MB (38.9).

## OLTP round 3 (round 23, agent 136)

HANDOFF.md, "OLTP round 3", has what changed: extended-protocol statements bound once per
text, their custom plans run as the generic binding when that is provably their plan, kept
plans by non-unique columns without a most-common-values list, DML re-planned without
re-binding, kept plans' sizes checked without a statement. Release builds with frame
pointers, pgbench in a container on the host network, scale 10, 10 s per run after a 4 s
warm-up, the engine restarted per binary and round; servers on cores 24–27, pgbench on
28–31; PostgreSQL 18.6 on the host network (shared_buffers 2 GB). **base** is `fd250f0b`,
**new** this round on it; three interleaved rounds (base, new, PostgreSQL), each cell the
range over the rounds: CPU ms per transaction (the engine process; PostgreSQL's cgroup),
in brackets the engine's thousand instructions per transaction (`perf stat` of the
process), then the median tps and the peak memory (the engine's VmHWM over the round's
runs; PostgreSQL's memory.peak, cumulative). The machine ran at load 40–55 throughout (other
agents' builds, suites and benchmarks), so single numbers move a lot; the whole-process
instruction count includes background flushes and compactions, whose timing follows the run
order (new ran after base in every round, so it often paid for base's writes: the
`ckinstr` runs below count only the connection threads).

- **Extended protocol** (pgbench `-M extended`: Parse / Bind / Describe / Execute / Sync of
  the unnamed statement, what pgx, node-postgres, JDBC before prepareThreshold and asyncpg's
  unnamed statements send): 2.3–3.0x less CPU per transaction. Select-only now costs what
  PostgreSQL's does (0.10–0.16 ms against 0.12–0.15); tpcb-like and the web-app mix are within
  10–25% of PostgreSQL (were 2.5–3.5x). The base bound every unnamed statement twice (the
  Parse's generic binding, then the Bind's custom plan). Peak memory unchanged (365 MB against
  381 at tpcb-like; the select rows' 522 against 431 is the web-app events table, which grows
  by every run and is first looked at by the size model in new's runs).
- **Simple protocol**: unchanged beyond noise in this matrix; the profile of tpcb-like at 8
  clients shows binding and planning down from 8.0% to 7.2% of the engine's samples (the
  accounts UPDATE re-planned, not re-bound, when its heap grows: 42% of executions; its
  re-bind's analysis was a smaller part than round 2 thought, plan_query is 3.1%), size
  checks 1.1% (were a statement snapshot each). A direct A/B of the connection threads'
  instructions only (`ckinstr`, three rounds at 8 clients): base 1,075–2,551, new 1,036–2,689
  thousand per transaction; the whole-process count's 1,456–1,597 against 945–1,048 above is
  background work, which ran in new's runs.
- **Web-app mix**: `count(*) ... WHERE aid = $n` keeps its plan (no most-common-values list
  on `aid`); its bigserial INSERT is 6% of the mix's CPU (size model 1.3%, the unique key's
  conflict probe 1.6%, nextval 0.4%), none of it planning.
- **Connection per transaction** (`pgbench -C`, `SELECT 1`, both servers trusting the
  client): the engine 0.55 ms of CPU per connection and 1,170 connections a second from one
  client, 2,073 from eight; PostgreSQL 7.6 ms (its fork and backend start) and 209 / 421. With
  the engine's `--password` (SCRAM) and PostgreSQL's image trusting 127.0.0.1, pgbench
  showed 49 against 134: the client's PBKDF2 (13 ms per connection in pgbench's loaded
  container) against none, measured phase by phase (startup to AuthenticationSASL 0.2 ms,
  SCRAM's server side 0.4 ms, the first ReadyForQuery 0.06 ms).
- **Hot rows** (tpcb-like `-M prepared`, one round): engine / PostgreSQL tps at 1, 8, 32, 64,
  128 clients 1,184 / 1,528, 3,140 / 4,757, 1,478 / 3,068, 1,358 / 3,253, 1,586 / 3,069; CPU
  per transaction 0.67 / 0.45, 0.91 / 0.51, 1.15 / 0.78, 1.11 / 0.92, 1.38 / 1.03 ms. The
  engine runs out of tps before CPU at 32+ (1.7 of its 4 cores busy): off-CPU, about a fifth
  of its threads' context switches are `sched_yield` spinning on the size model's relfiles
  lock (`Kills::new`, `ensure` from `updated`, `fetched`, `is_estimated`, `CopyLoad::register`;
  on-CPU `exec::sizing` is 13.9%), 6.5% the row and xact lock waits, 4% the WAL fsync.
- **Hot rows after the size model's shard locks** (`b0c4238b`, which has agent 142's
  6b079926; two rounds, the machine quieter): engine tps at 1 / 8 / 32 / 64 / 128 clients
  1,612–1,626 / 6,595–6,732 / 6,100–6,698 / 5,194–5,699 / 4,027–4,636, CPU per transaction
  0.49 / 0.47–0.49 / 0.53–0.57 / 0.63–0.64 / 0.78–0.85 ms; PostgreSQL 1,886 / 10,156 / 9,880 /
  8,309 / 7,470 tps, 0.35 / 0.29 / 0.37 / 0.46 / 0.51 ms. The same curve at about two thirds
  of the tps, CPU-bound on the 4 server cores (3.5 busy at 32 clients): the gap is CPU per
  transaction now. Off-CPU at 32: xact-lock waits on the hot rows 8% of the context switches,
  the WAL fsync 5%, `wait_durable`'s sync mutex spinning 4%, the size model's `is_estimated`
  under the branches / tellers bitmap scans 6%, the lock table's mutex under `lock_row` 2%.
  Waking the commits an fsync covers together (a condvar in `wait_durable`) changed neither
  tps nor CPU (1,588–1,732 / 5,826–6,877 / 5,796–6,267 / 4,934–5,593 / 4,272–4,306 tps,
  interleaved with the rows above) and was left out.
- **Agent 133's reproducers**: Metabase's Quartz lock query (`SELECT * FROM qrtz_locks WHERE
  sched_name = .. AND lock_name = .. FOR UPDATE`, its own transaction): 0.107 ms CPU prepared
  and 0.20 simple at 1 client (PostgreSQL 0.136 / 0.154), 16,791 tps at 4 clients on one row
  (PostgreSQL 6,616): the application's 6.7 ms is time waiting for the scheduler transaction
  that holds the row. tpcb-like's END (`pgbench -r -M prepared`, engine `--no-sync`):
  0.060 ms against PostgreSQL's 0.099 with fsync, so a row-writing commit isn't Grafana's
  157 µs average (its migrations' DDL commits are the likely cause, not measured here).

## OLTP, round 4 (round 24, agent 145)

watched kept plans and PL/pgSQL custom plans as the generic plan (ef3831c2); a plan's Gathers
found once per bound plan (a6a8b7ec); a custom plan's cost and Gathers from one estimate tree,
the serial tree below parallel_setup_cost, pg_prepared_statements read as the view is
(e8663d91).

Peak memory over the matrix: engine VmHWM 425-465 MB; PostgreSQL's cgroup memory.peak 774 MB
(shared_buffers 2 GB, cumulative).

- **select-only**: ahead on every protocol (0.61-0.98). The range read (`SELECT sum(abalance)
  ... WHERE aid BETWEEN :aid AND :aid + 99`) is the gap: 2.3-3.8x the CPU. Its profile
  (release, 8 clients, prepared, after e8663d91): the executor 47% (the aggregate over a
  100-row index scan, agent 149's), the custom plan made on every Execute 36% (re-analysis
  with the values as constants 21%, the serial cost tree 13%). PostgreSQL makes a custom plan
  for every Execute here too (8 of 8, checked), from its analyzed tree: planning the engine's
  `Analyzed` with the values substituted is agent 155's next planner item (the binder decides
  constness while it binds).
- **tpcb-like / simple-update prepared**: 1.0-1.6x. Off-CPU at 32 clients (sched_switch,
  release at f6654266): the size models' locks 26% of the connection threads' context
  switches (agent 151, landing), the WAL fsync 8.6% (fdatasync, and commits spinning on the
  sync mutex while another fsyncs), lock_row 8% (waits for the hot branches / tellers rows
  that PostgreSQL waits for too).

### The fixes, before and after (release, 8 clients, host engine, interleaved)

- **Gathers per bound plan** (a6a8b7ec): since d22006b9 every execution built its plan's
  EXPLAIN tree to start parallel workers when a relation passed min_parallel_table_scan_size
  (40% of select-only's CPU). select-only prepared 0.127-0.137 -> 0.053-0.063 ms per
  transaction, simple 0.139-0.144 -> 0.053-0.064, tpcb-like prepared 0.67-0.87 -> 0.49-0.58.
- **One estimate tree per custom plan, serial below parallel_setup_cost** (e8663d91): the
  range read prepared 0.58 -> 0.36 profile samples per transaction (the Gathers tree gone, the
  cost tree without partial paths); tpcb-like and select-only unchanged (1,419-1,489 /
  133-140 thousand instructions per transaction either side).
- **Commits sleep while another fsyncs** (storage `wait_durable`): simple-update prepared, 8
  clients 0.32-0.40 -> 0.30-0.31 ms per transaction, 32 clients 0.40-0.60 -> 0.31-0.32 ms and
  2,169-8,570 -> 11,978-12,079 tps (PostgreSQL in the container table: 9,159-15,453);
  tpcb-like unchanged.
- **memory_load** (`many_sessions_stay_bounded`, 100 sessions, release): +50.2 MB of heap at
  this round's first batch, +57.0 at d22006b9, +61.7 / +62.4 at 74a3ad38 / b0c4238b (bound
  120); debug builds vary 82-126 MB run to run on any of them.

- **The range read** (`SELECT sum(abalance) ... WHERE aid BETWEEN :aid AND :aid + 99`): 15-28%
  less CPU per transaction than base on every protocol (prepared and extended: custom plans
  from the analysis; simple: the template's analysis). Its profile after (release with frame
  pointers, 8 clients, prepared): execution 41% (the aggregate over a 100-row index scan),
  `bound_custom` 19% of which planning 13% (`plan_query_limited`) and the substitution 4.6%,
  the custom plan's cost (`plan_cost`, the EXPLAIN builder's estimate tree) 19.7%: that one
  waits for the planner to hand over its chosen path's cost (agent 155's item).
- **tpcb-like prepared** 1.33-1.59 -> 1.11-1.40; **simple-update prepared** 1.00-1.09 ->
  0.94-1.15 (its 1-client cell rerun in three more interleaved rounds: base 0.244-0.258, new
  0.250-0.254 ms, the same). Where tpcb-like prepared's CPU goes (8 clients): the UPDATEs'
  row scans 31.6% (`next_heap_ordered` 14.8%: tellers' and branches' version chains in their
  hot ranges, which PostgreSQL seq-scans too; storage, agent 149), send 12-14%, receive 6%,
  commit 7.2% (`wait_durable` 2.7%), `run_query`'s own work 8.3% (relation locks 18% of it,
  privilege checks 15%, `begin_statement` 12%, search_path copies 7-10%, settings by name 6%),
  Bind 3%, the allocator 10% as leaf. simple-update prepared: send 18%, commit 12%, receive
  7.6%, `run_query`'s own work 16.5%, pg_stat_activity updates per message 3.5%, the notify
  hub's mutex 1.7% (now per session).
- **select-only** 0.52-1.00 on every protocol (base 0.60-1.11); **tpcb-like simple / extended**
  0.69-0.94 (base 0.80-1.09).
- Peak memory over the matrix: engine VmHWM base 451 MB, new 445 MB; PostgreSQL's cgroup
  memory.peak 1,211 MB (shared_buffers 2 GB, cumulative).

- **Footprint gate** (scoreboard.sh's three measures, the engine in its container on cores 4-7,
  base and this round's measured build alternated, six runs each): fresh data directory 3.0
  MB either side; idle CPU 0-0.05 s per 5 minutes either side; with 100 idle sessions the
  container's anon + shmem 29.3 MB base, 30.7 MB the measured build (RSS 52.4-54.6 against
  55.2-56.4 MB; without sessions 24.7-25.9 against 26.1-26.4 MB). Both pass the gate (33 MB,
  and below PostgreSQL's); the growth was traced to presizing the lock table (`Txn::locks_held`
  with room for 8: a 672-byte allocation class each connection thread's mimalloc heap then
  touches), which was left out of the commit. The committed code wasn't measured again in
  dist (the heavy queue held 20+ jobs): the next scoreboard run should confirm 29-30 MB.

- **select-only**: 0.58-0.69 of PostgreSQL's CPU on simple and extended, 0.93-0.98 prepared
  (base 0.97-1.07): ahead on every protocol and client count. From the profile: the per-statement
  busy mark on the statistics mutex (spinning at 32 clients), the read-only transaction's lock
  table round trips (its own lock now on the fast path), the per-message activity updates.
- **simple-update / tpcb-like**: within noise of base: their remaining gap is execution (the
  UPDATEs' scans of the hot tellers / branches rows, 31% of tpcb-like prepared) and the store's
  commit, not session work (HANDOFF "OLTP round 6", where the time is).
- **The range read** is unchanged (2.0-2.3x): `bind_values` 20% and `plan_cost` 18% (agent
  171) are the next items.
- **Footprint gate** (scoreboard's three measures, the engine alone in its container, base and
  new alternated, six runs each): fresh data directory 3.0 MB either side; idle CPU 0.02-0.03 s
  per 5 minutes either side; with 100 idle sessions the container's anon + shmem 27.9-31.8 MB
  base (median 30.75), 29.2-31.1 MB new (median 30.83): the same within the runs' spread, under
  the 33 MB gate. Round 5's code (363366c2) measured here against the commit before it (e3e993ad):
  29.9-30.9 against 30.3-31.7 MB (medians 30.0 / 30.8): round 5 added about 0.8 MB with 100
  sessions even without the lock-table presize, not traced further.
- **Cursors** (batch 2): a 20,000-row table read through `DECLARE c CURSOR ... ; FETCH 100`
  until done (postgres_fdw's way), simple protocol, 10 s per run, two interleaved rounds,
  dist, containers as above (**cur** is batch 2):

  | clients | pg tps | pg ms | new tps | new ms | cur tps | cur ms | new/pg | cur/pg |
  |---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
  | 1 | 33-35 | 19.8-20.5 | 25-26 | 26.9-27.5 | 25-29 | 24.0-25.9 | 1.35 | 1.24 |
  | 8 | 190-202 | 14.5-15.5 | 123-142 | 22.4-24.7 | 127-162 | 19.1-22.6 | 1.57 | 1.39 |

  Peak anon + shmem over the runs: PostgreSQL 100 MB, new 163 MB, cur 73 MB. What is left is
  per FETCH (201 simple-protocol statements per transaction) and the row output, not the kept
  rows.
- **Connections**: 1,000 idle sessions with the engine's open-file limit at 1,024 (soft and
  hard): new 1,013 descriptors, all connected, 213 MB; base fails at about 640 connections
  (7 descriptors each).

## Application CPU (round 24, agent 133)

Server CPU of the application suites (tests/clients, STATUS.md "Round 7"), profiled with perf
(release engine, line tables and frame pointers, `--call-graph fp`, 199–499 Hz, from a
privileged container on the host's pid namespace) and per statement with pg_stat_statements
on both sides (track = all; the reference with track_planning on, so its planning time counts
as the engine's does). Folded stacks were grouped by phase.

Where the engine's CPU goes (share of samples, base 177b53e9):

| phase | Keycloak | Kysely | EF Core (first 180 s) | owner |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| Parse message: binding and planning the statement (`bound_as`) | 9.6% | 14.6% | 25.6% | planner (140) |
| Bind: the custom plan bound afresh (`bound_custom`) | 15.1% | 9.7% | 1.9% | planner (140) |
| of which path search (`plan_query_limited`) | 13.4% | 14.2% | 23.4% | planner (140) |
| pg_stat_statements' parallel-worker count, an EXPLAIN tree per execution | 12.4% | | | fixed here |
| custom plan cost (`plan_cost_estimate`, an EXPLAIN tree per custom plan) | 6.8% | 6.6% | 1.5% | fixed here, mostly |
| pg_stat_statements' query jumble (the parse tree through serde_json) | 5.4% | | | fixed here |
| Incremental Sort's presorted keys, an EXPLAIN tree per Sort at run time | 6.1% | 3.3% | 3.1% | executor (121) |
| correlated sub-selects run (`eval_subquery`), mostly bitmap scans | 7.9% | | 35.8% | scans (130) |
| commit (`finish_txn`) | 0.5% | 9.1% | 0.9% | OLTP (123) / catalog (131) |

(The Keycloak and Kysely columns ran with pg_stat_statements loaded; without it its rows are 0.)

Fixed here:
- `paths::parallel::may_gather`: pg_stat_statements counts each execution's parallel workers
  from the plan's EXPLAIN tree (`explain::gather_workers`); the tree is built now only when
  some relation the statement reads could get a partial path (min_parallel_table_scan_size,
  a parallel_workers option, an appendrel member, a UNION ALL / inheritance Append, or
  debug_parallel_query). Debug builds assert the tree's count is 0 otherwise.
- `PlanUses::first_custom`: the cost of a prepared statement's first custom plan (read only
  from the sixth execution on, against the generic plan's) is found when its second custom
  plan is made, not as the first runs: a driver's unnamed statement, parsed again for every
  execution (pgjdbc below prepareThreshold, Go's database/sql, Npgsql), runs once and never
  needs it. One custom plan is kept per prepared statement until then (after DDL in between,
  the second plan's cost stands in for the first's). Measured before OLTP round 3 (agent
  136), which runs an unnamed statement's value-independent custom plans as its generic
  binding with their costs likewise deferred (`PlanUses::uncosted`); statements whose text
  it keeps bindings for are costed as their plans are made, as it needs.
- `session::pgss::jumble_cached`: top-level statements' query ids, kept per thread by text,
  position, catalog and search_path (512 entries), as PostgreSQL computes them at parse
  analysis rather than per execution.

Before and after (release, fresh engines, two interleaved runs each; with and without
pg_stat_statements loaded):

| suite | before | after | PostgreSQL |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| Kysely | 10.2 / 10.1 s; pgss 11.2 / 12.0 s | 9.2 / 9.7 s; pgss 9.3 / 9.5 s | 7.6 s (pgss) |
| Grafana | 5.0 / 4.8 s; pgss 6.2 / 5.6 s | 4.0 / 4.3 s; pgss 4.7 / 4.3 s | 4.1 s (pgss) |
| Keycloak | 17.5 / 18.2 s; pgss 21.4 / 20.8 s | 17.4 / 16.6 s; pgss 18.2 / 18.4 s | 6.9–9.5 s (pgss) |
| Metabase | 37.9 s | 36.2 s | 13.2 s |

Peak RSS of those runs 98–125 MB (Metabase 174–178 MB) either way (the kept first custom
plans: 0–4 MB more).
pgbench (scale 10, 8 clients, `-M prepared`, 20 s, interleaved twice): select-only 0.115 /
0.116 ms of server CPU per transaction before, 0.110 / 0.110 after (peak 346 / 350 MB
before, 337 / 347 after); tpcb-like 0.755 / 0.768 before, 0.752 / 0.770 after.

EF Core: 919 s of engine CPU (peak 763 MB; 935 s and 841 MB with pg_stat_statements) against
172 s on PostgreSQL. Metabase's gap is mostly Liquibase's column check below (49 calls,
110 ms each) and its own application queries (`SELECT * FROM (SELECT "t"."active", ...`:
~10 ms against ~0.15 ms a call; not profiled further). Most of EF Core's gap is four Northwind
queries, about 530 s of the engine's 805 s of statement time (PostgreSQL: under 0.5 s each),
for the scans' and executor's owners:
- `char(n)` equality index conditions read every entry: on a 20,000-row table,
  `SELECT count(*) FROM t WHERE c = 'ZZZZZ'` (c char(5), btree) is an Index Only Scan with
  "Heap Fetches: 20000" and 0 rows, 84–96 ms (PostgreSQL 0.2 ms, 2 buffers). Northwind's
  CustomerID is char(5): every correlated `o0."CustomerID" = c."CustomerID"` sub-select (a
  Bitmap Heap Scan per outer row) reads all 830 orders, ~3.6 ms a run (PostgreSQL ~10 µs).
  `int` keys don't (0.8 ms for none), but 5,000 matching int rows still take 46 ms
  (PostgreSQL 1.3 ms).
- A sub-select's InitPlans that read only the outer query's row (`(SELECT o1."OrderID" ...
  WHERE c."CustomerID" = o1."CustomerID" ORDER BY ... LIMIT 1) = o."OrderID"` inside a
  correlated sub-select) are evaluated per row of the sub-select's own scan, not once per
  run: "Order Details" (2,155 rows) times two InitPlans per customer, 92 s for 8 customers
  where the plan (the same as PostgreSQL's) says Index Only Scan with `Index Cond:
  ("OrderID" = (InitPlan 2).col1)`.

For the planner's owner: Liquibase's column check (Keycloak, Metabase), `SELECT 1 FROM
pg_attribute a WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM pg_class JOIN pg_catalog.pg_namespace ns ON ns.oid
= pg_class.relnamespace WHERE lower(ns.nspname) = 'public' AND lower(relname) = 't77' AND
pg_class.oid = a.attrelid) AND lower(a.attname) = 'c5'`, isn't pulled up to a semi join
(PostgreSQL: a HashAggregate of the pg_class ⋈ pg_namespace side, then an index scan of
pg_attribute); the engine runs a SubPlan per pg_attribute row (6,401 loops): 74–110 ms a call
against 1 ms. Any qual on the namespace side keeps it a SubPlan (without one it is pulled
up), and the SubPlan's EXPLAIN shows `Recheck Cond: (oid = ns.oid)`, an equivalence the query
doesn't have (the rows are right). pgjdbc's getTables / getImportedKeys / getIndexInfo cost
5–40x PostgreSQL's per call (Keycloak's Liquibase calls getTables 991 times). For OLTP:
Grafana's 1,430 COMMITs average 157 µs against 1 µs (`--no-sync`), and Metabase's `SELECT *
FROM QRTZ_LOCKS ... FOR UPDATE` 6.7 ms against 0.4 ms.
## Query memory and spilling (round 23, agent 132)

HANDOFF.md, "Query memory and spilling", has what changed: sorts and hash aggregations
honour work_mem / hash_mem_multiplier and spill where PostgreSQL's would (decided by
PostgreSQL's own count of their memory), group keys are kept once and group states are
compact. Release builds over the wire, server pinned to 4 cores, on a machine shared with
other agents (load 30-40 in the first runs; everything at nice 19 in the later ones), engine
memory its RSS, PostgreSQL's the container's anon + shmem. PostgreSQL 18.6 is the pinned image
with its defaults (`work_mem` 4 MB, `shared_buffers` 128 MB) and `max_connections=300`.

### Burst, the sort + grouping mix (`bench burst --mix pl,fn,orm,cat,tpcb,sort,agg`, scale 2)

Agent 111's mix plus a 20,000-row sort under a LIMIT and a 50,000-group grouping of the
200,000 accounts, 20 s per size, a fresh engine per size. `base` is 57d7da04, `new` this batch
(on fd250f0b; the two differ also by agent 126's size-model shards, which don't touch this
mix's peak), run interleaved per size. MB.

| sessions | PostgreSQL peak | base peak | new peak | PostgreSQL ops/s | base ops/s | new ops/s | base / new CPU ms/op |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 10 | 172 | 730 | 577 | 170 | 38 | 32 | 67 / 70 |
| 50 | 415 | 2,784 | 1,603 | 175 | 21 | 21 | 97 / 92 |
| 100 | 711 | 5,493 | 2,941 | 175 | 32 | 28 | 81 / 80 |
| 200 | 1,398 | 5,234 | 3,677 | 161 | 20 | 16 | 76 / 93 |

The grouping now holds about 230 bytes a group (key once, 24-byte states, the hash table's
order model in 8-byte slots, no 16 bytes per input row) instead of about 900. PostgreSQL
doesn't spill this grouping (50,000 groups fit hash_mem), and the engine doesn't either. What
is left of the engine's peak is mostly the sequential scan collecting the table's moved rows to
return them in heap order (`scan.rs` `next_raw`, agent 130's: about 30 MB a session here), and
the CPU per operation (80 ms against PostgreSQL's 20) is that scan and the grouping's per-row
work, not memory. The plain mix (without sort and agg) and the `--reconnect 1` variant: peaks
and ops/s within the run-to-run noise of this machine (plain at 100 sessions 440 / 459 MB,
1,299 / 924 ops/s, at 50 sessions 975 / 879, no consistent direction across sizes);
PostgreSQL with `--reconnect 1` at 200 sessions ran out of connection slots ("too many
clients already") and is left out.

### pgbench (8 clients, prepared, 15 s, scale 1), `base` 41fc8981 (origin) against `new` (this batch on it), alternated 3 times

| | base | new |
|---|---:|---:|
| select-only, engine CPU µs / transaction | 53, 51, 52 | 51, 52, 51 |
| TPC-B, engine CPU µs / transaction | 429, 419, 415 | 422, 433, 414 |
| RSS after (MB) | 108-144 | 122-148 |

### Spills against PostgreSQL

`SET work_mem = '1MB'` over 200,000 rows of (int, md5 text) (the a132 goldens' shape), the
engine's EXPLAIN ANALYZE against PostgreSQL's:

| | PostgreSQL | engine |
|---|---|---|
| `ORDER BY t` | external merge, Disk: 9208kB | external merge |
| `GROUP BY t` (200,000 groups) | Batches: 17, Memory Usage: 2073kB, Disk Usage: 14984kB | Batches: 17, Memory Usage: 2073kB, Disk Usage: 9448kB |
| temp_file_limit = 1MB | ERROR 53400 | ERROR 53400 |

The engine's own spill files are its row encodings (`storage::row`) with a length word,
written in 256 kB appends and read in 64 kB reads, not compressed (not measured against a
compressed variant yet). Disk Usage above is the model of PostgreSQL's tapes
without its preallocation (`ltsGetPreallocBlock` grows a partition's blocks 8 -> 128 at a time,
and the holes count), hence lower.

Not measured this round, left for the next: TPC-H SF1 before / after (the analytic queries'
sorts of 2M rows now spill at the default work_mem as PostgreSQL's do; their time and peak
against base), the difftest process's growth over 150 seeds (agent 118's), and the pgbench
simple-update peak at 64 clients after 57d7da04 (agent 126's).

## Size models, round 3 (round 23, agent 142)

HANDOFF.md, "Size models, round 3", has what changed. Base is 57d7da04, head is this batch.
These are release builds. The engine ran on cores 24-27 and pgbench (`-M prepared`, a
container on the host network) on 28-31, everything under `nice -n 19`, at scale 10, 20 s
per run after a 5 s warm-up. The engine was restarted per run on one data directory, with
base and head interleaved. Load was 40-50. Wall times and tps moved by 15% between
identical runs, so CPU per transaction (the engine's utime + stime) is the figure to read.

**Model memory and the first statement after a clean restart.** `tests/restart_cost.rs`:
300,000 rows of pgbench_accounts' shape with its primary key, a tenth updated, then a clean
restart. The test now counts live allocations. Two interleaved rounds:

| | base | head |
|---|---|---|
| live after the first statement (models laid out) | 74.4 MB | 46.9 MB |
| per row | ~237 bytes | ~145 bytes |
| first statement (point SELECT, waits for the layout) | 0.39 / 0.48 s | 0.29 / 0.35 s |
| process CPU | 0.62 / 0.80 s | 0.78 / 0.56 s |
| VmHWM | 144 / 144 MB | 116 / 136 MB |

PostgreSQL's pages for these rows: 44 MB heap, 6.8 MB index. The intermediate step (key
arena plus 32-byte line pointers, before thin btree keys) measured 56.9 MB.

**pgbench, 8 clients**:

| workload | base tps | head tps | base CPU ms/txn | head CPU ms/txn | base VmHWM | head VmHWM |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| select-only | 60,809 / 61,355 / 53,946 | 61,543 / 53,188 / 54,213 | 0.063 / 0.063 / 0.069 | 0.063 / 0.069 / 0.069 | 151 / 187 / 149 MB | 182 / 145 / 148 MB |
| simple-update | 11,733 | 12,160 | 0.266 | 0.258 | 262 MB | 244 MB |
| tpcb-like | 6,126 | 6,361 | 0.474 | 0.479 | 239 MB | 230 MB |

At scale 10, pgbench_accounts is past the 400,000-row limit, so only tellers, branches and
history are modelled. Nothing moved beyond the noise.

**Page counts against PostgreSQL 18.6**: round 2's mixed workload (the script above, with
mw1's row count as a parameter, mw2 at 2/3 of it). It reports pages before and after a
clean restart, then inserts and their ctids before and after a VACUUM:

| rows | differs from PostgreSQL |
|---|---|
| 60,000 | `mw1_v` 721 against 725 (as in round 2); all else equal (main / fsm / vm, 1,100 ctids, EXPLAIN costs) |
| 300,000 | `mw1_k` 485 against 487, `mw1_v` 3,696 against 3,706; all else equal |

Base's output at 300,000 rows is identical to head's.

## Monitoring scrapes (round 23, agent 128)

What changed (HANDOFF "Server monitoring and logging, round 4"): statistics reads under
stats_fetch_consistency = cache stopped copying every cached entry per call (quadratic over
a scan of pg_stat_*_tables), the consistency setting is derived once, plus the monitoring
features (proc array, parallel worker rows, checkpoint and temp-file numbers) whose cost is
below.

Method: release builds; the engine pinned to CPUs 20-23 (`taskset`), the reference
(PostgreSQL 18.6, the pinned image, pg_stat_statements preloaded, max_connections 200 so
100 sessions and the exporter fit) to 24-27 (`docker update --cpuset-cpus`); database
`scale` with 10,000 tables (a primary key each, 50 rows in every hundredth); 100 sessions
held open on each by `pgbench -c 100 -f idle.sql` (`\sleep 5 s; SELECT 1`);
prometheus-community postgres_exporter 0.17.1 (default collectors) on each, scraped with
curl, engine and reference in turn, 4 rounds. CPU: the engine process's utime + stime, the
reference container's cgroup usage_usec, over the scrape. The machine ran at load 40-55 on
36 CPUs (other agents' builds), so wall times moved by ±20% between rounds.

| per scrape (4 rounds) | PostgreSQL 18.6 | engine |
|---|---|---|
| wall | 6.0-8.1 s | 4.3-5.0 s |
| server CPU | 3.9-5.9 s | 2.1-2.5 s |
| series | 280,581 | 280,581 |
| memory (after the scrapes) | 330 MB (container) | 171 MB RSS, 273 MB peak |

Before the read fix the engine's scrape took 367-514 s (and 304-388 s of CPU): the
stat_user_tables and statio_user_tables collectors' views, 147 s and 52 s each. A scrape
every 15 s now costs the engine about 15% of one CPU, PostgreSQL's about 30%; most of
PostgreSQL's is pg_total_relation_size over every table (5.4 s alone; the engine's 0.8 s).

Views alone, database `scale`, one statement each (psql \timing, same load):

| statement | PostgreSQL | engine before | engine now |
|---|---|---|---|
| `count(*) FROM pg_stat_user_tables` | 59 ms | 147 s | 0.9 s |
| `count(*) FROM pg_statio_user_tables` | 16 ms | 52 s | 1.0-1.1 s |
| `sum(pg_total_relation_size(relid)) FROM pg_stat_user_tables` | 5,413 ms | 60 s | 0.7-0.8 s |
| `count(*) FROM pg_stat_user_indexes` | 42 ms | 8.1 s | 0.8 s |
| `count(*) FROM pg_stat_activity` (100 sessions) | 2.3 ms | 2.3 ms | 7 ms |
| `count(*) FROM pg_locks` | 0.8 ms | 0.4 ms | 0.8 ms |

The tools' own queries (`tests/clients/suites/monitoring.py`: pgwatch 3.7.0, pg_activity
3.6.1, pganalyze-collector 0.66.1, Datadog 22.12.0, check_postgres; 193 queries, median of
three runs each) in database `scale`, total per tool:

| tool | PostgreSQL | engine |
|---|---|---|
| pgwatch | 2,859 ms | 36,307 ms (30,006 of it table_stats_approx, at its 30 s timeout) |
| pganalyze | 534 ms | 87 ms |
| Datadog | 17 ms | 36 ms |
| check_postgres | 6 ms | 21 ms |
| pg_activity | 4.8 ms | 4.9 ms |

pgwatch's table_stats_approx is planned with a hash join inside a correlated sub-select over
pg_class / pg_index (rows=201 for pg_class's relkind = 'r' where PostgreSQL's estimate is
10,075): a planner estimate, left to the planner's owner. Its other per-table metrics run
2-4x PostgreSQL's time (table_stats 878 vs 267 ms, index_stats 732 vs 160 ms).

pgbench (scale 10, 8 clients, 20 s, 6 interleaved rounds per build, client on CPUs 28-31;
engine CPU per transaction, which load moves less than tps): select-only 260-307 µs base,
269-316 µs this round (medians 294 / 288); tpcb-like 1,731-2,238 µs base, 1,578-2,192 µs
(medians 1,795 / 1,816); peak RSS 142-220 MB base, 143-223 MB. No regression beyond the
noise of a machine at load 50.

Left: pg_stat_user_tables is still 15x PostgreSQL's per row (25 pg_stat_get_* calls per
row through the evaluator, each cloning the table's entry); pgwatch's table_stats_approx plan.

## Query memory, round 2 (round 24, agent 150)

HANDOFF.md, "Query memory, round 2", has what changed. Release builds with frame pointers
(`--profile release`, `-C force-frame-pointers=yes`, line tables), engine and PostgreSQL
18.6 each pinned to the same four cores (12-15) and both at nice 19 (PostgreSQL started as
`nice -n 19 docker-entrypoint.sh postgres -c max_connections=300`, its other settings the
defaults), on a machine other agents' builds kept 60-95% busy: absolute numbers vary by
10-20% between runs, so engine binaries are compared alternated (A, B, A, B). Engine
memory is its RSS, PostgreSQL's the container's anon + shmem. Logs:
`~/.cache/a150/logs` (copied numbers below).

### The 200-session burst (`bench burst --sessions 200 --secs 20`, scale 2)

| | ops/s | CPU µs / op | peak MB | engine / PostgreSQL |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| PostgreSQL 18.6 | 2,727 | 1,419 | 695 | |
| engine at `8115f5af` | 1,683 | 1,921 | 590 | 0.62x ops, 1.35x CPU, 0.85x memory |
| engine at `8115f5af` with the three changes below | 1,863 | 1,866 | 600 | 0.68x ops, 1.31x CPU, 0.86x memory |

The engine leads on memory and trails on CPU per operation and on how many of the four
cores it keeps busy (3.2-3.4 under `perf stat`, 12-14k context switches a second). Each
operation of the mix alone (200 sessions, 10 s, origin; CPU µs / op, engine against
PostgreSQL): `pl` (PL/pgSQL loop of ten key lookups) 654 / 320, `fn` (100 calls of a SQL
function) 2,980 / 2,116, `orm` (four-table nested loop join) 3,797 / 1,102, `cat` (psql's
and information_schema's column queries) 3,161 / 4,737 (the engine ahead), `tpcb` 1,647 /
1,586.

What this batch takes out (profiles of the engine under `pgbench -M prepared -c 8`):

- `parallel_part::gathers` ran the EXPLAIN builder's costing of the whole plan at every
  execution, to place PostgreSQL's Gathers: 27% of the ORM join's CPU. A per-thread cache
  of them by plan (while the catalog version, the settings and the relation sizes the
  costing read held) took the ORM join from 1,308-1,423 to 879-939 µs per execution,
  alternated three times (PostgreSQL 355-445); agent 145's e8663d91, which keeps them with
  the bound plan, landed first and replaces it.
- PL/pgSQL formatted a cache key of the routine's body, signature and environment for every
  expression it evaluated (12.5% of the `pl` operation, `compile` 21% in all): plans are now
  found by function, expression and a hash of what can change during or between calls
  (`compile` 4% of the operation).
- Text results of text, integer, boolean and oid columns go into the DataRow without a
  String each (`server::put_text_fast`).

Where the rest goes (shares of the engine's CPU in the profiles):

- size-model upkeep on reads (`sizing::fetched`, `Kills::mark` / `done`, `row_roots`,
  `heapsim::locate_for`, `keymap::find`, `btsim::kill_needed`): about 20% of the ORM join,
  8% of the `fn` operation (agent 151);
- planning of simple-protocol statements the kept plans don't cover (`fn`'s and `orm`'s
  literals, the catalog queries): 22% of the burst, with the EXPLAIN builder's estimates
  for `presorted_keys` (5.6%) and the Gathers of plans seen once (2.8%) (agents 145, 140);
- opening a SQL function's plan per call (19% of `fn`), allocation (`_mi_page_malloc_zero`
  7-15%, mostly decoded values and output text), the wire's text output (8-12%).

### TPC-H SF1 and the analytic set (2M rows) against PostgreSQL, before and after hash join batches

Measured 2026-11-01 (servers on cores 24-27, nice 19 for the engine): `base` 7dab0879 (round
1), `new` the hash join batches (c4746101's code), PostgreSQL with `shared_buffers=2GB`
(`BENCH.md` "Results" settings), the engine with `--cache-mb 2048`, sums of the medians of 2
runs, rows hashed equal on all three:

| | time (s) | CPU (s) | peak MB | temp files / bytes |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| PostgreSQL | 10.6 | 28.6 | 1,704 | 94 / 227 MB |
| base | 24.3 | 65.7 | 1,928 | 0 / 0 |
| new | 21.7 | 58.8 | 1,765 | 38 / 75 MB |

Analytic (2M rows, median of 2): PostgreSQL / base: seq_filter 0.12 / 0.19 s, sort_text
0.95 / 2.12, hash_agg 0.19 / 0.46, hash_join 0.39 / 0.69, window 1.76 / 3.37 (window's peak
1,321 MB: the engine's WindowAgg holds its whole input), PostgreSQL's temp 405 MB, base's
253 MB.

On 2026-11-03 the engine at `8115f5af` spent 44-53 s on its first q01 after a start, with a
3.3-3.5 GB peak: lineitem's size model was built from every row (`heapsim::restored`,
`KeyMap::insert`), 1,572 MB of RSS after planning `count(*)` of lineitem alone (125 MB at
round 1). `18bb3d49` (agent 151) restored the 400,000-version model limit that keeps
big tables estimated.

### Window partitions streamed (the analytic `window`, 2M rows, 1,000 partitions)

`base` 452eca77, `new` with the window's Sort run as a SortNode and its input read a
partition at a time; fresh engine per run (data and block cache warm on disk, `--cache-mb
2048`), cores 12-15, median of 3, alternated twice; rows hashed equal to PostgreSQL's:

| | time (s) | CPU (s) | peak MB |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| PostgreSQL | 1.76 | 1.74 | 1,840 (its shared buffers) |
| base | 4.05, 8.60 | 4.10, 6.43 | 1,290, 1,276 |
| new | 3.17, 3.15 | 3.20, 3.70 | 285, 281 |

Engine / PostgreSQL: 1.8x the time, 0.15x the memory (it was 2.3x / 0.70x).

## Query memory, round 3 (round 25, agent 164)

HANDOFF.md, "Query memory, round 3", has what changed. Both servers in containers on cores
24-27 (`tools/bench/run.sh` with `ENGINE_DOCKER=1`, host network, the engine built with
`--profile dist` and bind-mounted, `--cache-mb 2048`; PostgreSQL 18.6 with run.sh's
settings, `shared_buffers=2GB`), CPU and anon + shmem from each container's cgroup, the
bench client on cores 8-11. `base` is `3df0c607` (round 2's end); `new` is this round on
`73edecba` (so its TPC-H column includes agent 160's executor round 9 too). Engines alternated
(base, new, base, new), each a fresh container; the machine had other agents' builds on it
(load 11-15), so only alternated pairs are compared. The numbers are copied from the run logs,
which went with the scratch directory.

### The analytic window query (2M rows, 1,000 partitions; three alternated pairs, 3 reps each)

| | min s | CPU s | peak MB | temp MB |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| PostgreSQL | 1.75 | 1.76 | 976 | 149 |
| base | 2.59-2.66 | 2.72-2.77 | 272-282 | 149 |
| new | 2.21-2.24 | 2.31-2.36 | 247-249 | 149 |

Rows hashed equal to PostgreSQL's. Engine / PostgreSQL: 1.27x the time (was 1.50x), 0.25x
the memory. Profile of `base` (perf in a helper container, flat): writing runs 9% in
`encode_value`, 78% of it on loading the value (rows reached in sorted order, each a vector
of its own), dropping those rows 7%; after packing: `encode_value` 3%, drops 3%. What's left:
decoding runs read back (12%, twice: the intermediate merge pass at 4 MB and the final one),
run sorting (~10%), the window and the aggregate above (~10%).

### TPC-H SF1 (one rep each, fresh servers, all 22 results hashed equal to PostgreSQL's)

| | wall s | CPU s | peak MB | temp MB |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| PostgreSQL | 14.0 | 32.2 | 1,690 | 84 |
| base | 21.4, 21.7 | 47.8, 49.1 | 1,715 | 35 |
| new | 20.0, 19.7 | 45.3, 44.8 | 1,716 | 35 |

Engine / PostgreSQL: 1.41x CPU, 1.02x peak.

**Where the peak comes from** (base, a fresh engine per cache size, 2 reps, CPU of both):

| --cache-mb | peak MB | CPU s | sum of medians s |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 2048 | 1,718 | 88.2 | 18.9 |
| 1024 | 1,509 | 131.4 | 29.6 |
| 512 | 1,038 | 189.8 | 43.1 |

Per query at 512 against 2048 (CPU s): q01 4.36 / 4.32 (a sequential scan of lineitem
costs no more uncached), q06 2.47 / 1.72, q09 22.59 / 4.89, q07 6.60 / 1.63, q17 6.69 /
1.90, q21 6.05 / 2.54, q02 2.83 / 1.05, q11 1.78 / 0.23, q20 1.57 / 0.39: the cost is index
lookups and joins reading blocks the cache no longer holds, a 16 kB block decompressed and
decoded per row fetched.

**Bulk admission** (new, a knob for the experiment only, not kept: the share of the cache a
bulk read may fill; 2 reps): 100 % 1,715 MB, 114.0 s CPU; 25 % 1,729 MB, 116.8 s; 0 %
1,732 MB, 117.7 s. Keeping big sequential scans out of the cache (PostgreSQL's ring) doesn't
lower the peak: lineitem comes in through q04 / q21's lookups by l_orderkey and q17 / q20's by
l_partkey, as PostgreSQL's shared buffers take it in at q03 (its peak goes 115 MB after q01,
1,342 MB after q03). PostgreSQL's lineitem is 879 MB of heap, the engine's decoded blocks
about 970 MB.

### The burst (`bench burst`, scale 2, 20 s per size; PostgreSQL restarted first)

| sessions | PostgreSQL ops/s | new ops/s | PostgreSQL µs CPU / op | new µs CPU / op | PostgreSQL peak MB | new peak MB |
|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| 10 | 3,324 | 2,954 | 1,161 | 1,227 | 183 | 212 |
| 50 | 3,488 | 2,575 | 1,140 | 1,444 | 318 | 329 |
| 100 | 2,884 | 2,084 | 1,374 | 1,753 | 462 | 455 |
| 200 | 2,522 | 1,723 | 1,564 | 2,003 | 750 | 712 |

At 200 sessions: 0.68x the operations, 1.28x CPU per operation, 0.95x the memory (an earlier
pass, less loaded: 1,994 / 2,891 ops/s, 1,660 / 1,341 µs). `base` can't run 200 sessions in a
container: its 1,024 open files ran out (HANDOFF: seven descriptors per connection) and the
clients hung; the open-file limit raised, `new` does.

### pgbench (scale 10, 8 clients, 30 s, prepared; alternated twice)

| | select-only µs CPU / txn | select-only peak MB | tpcb-like µs CPU / txn | tpcb-like peak MB |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| PostgreSQL | 49.1 | 326 | 341 | 346 |
| base | 56.6, 56.3 | 218, 194 | 500, 486 | 319, 322 |
| new | 52.0, 54.7 | 199, 195 | 485, 507 | 322, 385 |

Within the runs' spread: no change.

### memory_load, the footprint gate

`many_sessions_stay_bounded` (100 sessions on a database just opened, bound 120 MB), nine
release runs +42.9 to +48.1 MB, five debug runs +45.9 to +48.8 MB (round 2: release +50-62,
debug up to +126).
Footprint gate (scoreboard quick, connect): idle CPU 0.025 s / 5 min (PostgreSQL 0.525),
100 idle sessions 30.7 MB (211), fresh data directory 3.03 MB (38.9): pass.

So the limit stays at 400,000: a modelled million-row table still costs its writes a
model update per row, its first statement after a restart the record's layout, and its

**Model memory** (`tests/restart_cost.rs`, 300,000 rows of pgbench_accounts' shape with its
primary key, live allocations after the first statement): 46.9 MB (round 3) -> 30.7 MB, about
93 bytes per row against PostgreSQL's 170 bytes of pages; first statement 0.16-0.18 s
(round 3: 0.29-0.35 s).

**Page counts against PostgreSQL 18.6** (round 2's mixed workload with mw1's row count as a
parameter, script `tools/bench/sizing/mw.py` since round 5, as described in round 2: pages per fork and
four EXPLAINs before and after a clean restart, 300 inserts into each table with their
ctids, VACUUM of both, the same report, 500 more inserts and an UPDATE of every 97th row with
their ctids, the report again): at 60,000 and at 300,000 rows everything matches PostgreSQL,
every relation's main / fsm / vm pages, every ctid and every EXPLAIN cost, after the VACUUM
too. Round 3 left `mw1_k` 485 / 487 and `mw1_v` 3,696 / 3,706 at 300,000 rows and `mw1_v`
721 / 725 at 60,000 (bottom-up deletion, round 4's first batch), and the round's new
after-VACUUM checks found `mw1_pkey` 1,785 / 1,784 and mw2's index reltuples (now equal).
Still different, outside these checks: pgstatindex's empty / deleted leaf pages after a
VACUUM empties btree pages (the model doesn't delete or recycle them).

## Size models, round 5 (round 24, agent 161)

HANDOFF.md, "Size models, round 5", has what changed.

**The scripts** (`tools/bench/sizing/`, replacing agent 151's deleted `~/.cache/a151/scripts`):

- `lift.sh <label> <pg|engine> [checks]`: one server, fresh, in a container (the engine as
  run.sh's `ENGINE_DOCKER`: the dist binary bind-mounted, the same cpuset, CPU weight and
  slice as PostgreSQL's), CPU from its cgroup's `cpu.stat`, peak anon + shmem sampled every
  0.5 s. Checks: `bulk` (`pgbench -i -s $SCALE` a phase at a time: COPY g, VACUUM v,
  primary key p; then CREATE INDEX on bid, DELETE of every tenth row, VACUUM, a clean
  restart and the first statement's `\timing`), `pgbench` (select-only, simple-update,
  tpcb-like at $CLIENTS clients, $SECS s, `-M prepared`), `apply` (agent 152's apply-like
  statements, `apply.sql`, on `extra_setup.sql`'s tables), `skip` (agent 144's skip scan,
  `count(*) FROM skip_t WHERE ts = K` on index (g, ts) over 1M rows, the median of 10).
- `liftcmp.sh <out> [checks]`: the lift table: PostgreSQL, the engine at its limit and the
  engine with `ROLL_MODEL_MAX_VERSIONS` lifted, `$ROUNDS` interleaved rounds, one row per
  check (CPU ms per transaction for the pgbench checks, else CPU s; peak MB).
- `mw.py`: the page-count workload (round 2's mixed workload, mw1's row count as a
  parameter), against a reference container or an engine it starts; diff the two outputs.
- `ROLL_MODEL_MAX_VERSIONS` (new, read once at start): the engine's model limit, so one
  binary measures both columns. run.sh's `ENGINE_ENV` passes it into the container.

**The lift table** (`liftcmp.sh`, 2 interleaved rounds, scale 10 = 1M accounts, engine and
PostgreSQL 18.6 in containers on cores 8-11, clients on 0-3, the dist build of b5aeaf72, other
agents' builds running at nice 19; CPU s, or CPU ms per transaction; peak anon + shmem MB):

| check | 400k | lifted | PostgreSQL | round 4: 400k / lifted |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| COPY 1M (g) | 1.89 / 2.53 s | 2.04 / 2.22 s | 0.83 / 0.79 s | 1.77 / 2.66 |
| VACUUM (v) | 0.35 / 0.35 s | 0.51 / 0.20 s | 0.15 / 0.17 s | 0.67 / 0.76 |
| primary key (p) | 0.66 / 0.78 s | 2.62 / 2.61 s | 0.58 / 0.52 s | 0.77 / 1.66 |
| CREATE INDEX (bid) | 1.06 / 1.52 s | 1.20 / 1.19 s | 0.40 / 0.43 s | 0.91 / 1.68 |
| DELETE 10% | 0.61 / 0.69 s | 0.81 / 1.15 s | 0.15 / 0.16 s | 0.98 / 1.44 |
| VACUUM after DELETE | 0.92 / 0.98 s | 0.38 / 0.39 s | 0.27 / 0.26 s | |
| first statement after restart | 9 / 13 ms | 246 / 240 ms | 2.1 / 1.6 ms | 20 / 310 |
| select-only c8 | 0.051 / 0.057 ms, 391 / 370 MB | 0.051 / 0.050 ms, 338 / 545 MB | 0.042 / 0.053 ms, 246 / 246 MB | |
